# The engine

`internal/engine` runs one statement of [slice 1](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-1-one-integer-column), with the parameters of [slice 2](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-2-parameters), the five column types of [slice 4](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-4-the-other-types), the several columns of [slice 5](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-5-several-columns), the NULL of [slice 6](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-6-null), the WHERE of [slice 7](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-7-where-on-select), the UPDATE and DELETE of [slice 8](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-8-update-and-delete), the indexes of [slice 9](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-9-indexes), the IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS of [slice 10](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-10-if-not-exists-and-if-exists), the `default` with a literal of [slice 11](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-11-default-with-a-literal), the value on the left of a comparison of [slice 12](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-12-a-value-on-the-left-of-a-comparison), the ORDER BY and LIMIT of a select of [slice 13](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-13-order-by-and-limit-on-select), the RETURNING of an insert, an update, and a delete of [slice 14](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-14-returning-on-insert-update-and-delete), the scalar subquery in a WHERE of [slice 15](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-15-a-scalar-subquery-in-where), the arithmetic of [the piece of work arithmetic](../../docs/todo/arithmetic.md), and the text in expressions of [the piece of work text functions](../../docs/todo/text-functions.md), on a database of the [storage core](../core/README.md), a `*core.DB`, and gives SQLite's results and SQLite's errors. A statement is its own transaction, unless a transaction that spans statements is open on its connection; see [Transactions](#transactions). What a statement of slice 1 is, token for token, is in [the README of `stmt`](../sql/stmt/README.md#slice-1).

The interface:

| Name | What it does |
| --- | --- |
| `New(d, host, Options)` | Gives an `*Engine` for the database `d`. The engine reads the monotonic clock of the host, for the duration in its events, and draws the direction of each query from the host. `Options.Logger` takes the events, and the default discards them. |
| `Engine.Conn()` | Gives a `*Conn`, a connection of the engine, which holds nothing until it runs a statement. See [Connections](#connections). |
| `Conn.Exec(ctx, src, args...) (Result, error)` | Runs the statement of the text `src`, with the arguments `args`, each an `*s4qlpb.Value`, for its parameters by position. A query is run to its end, and its rows are dropped. The rows of a RETURNING are not held at all. The result comes with an error too. See [Parameters](#parameters). |
| `Conn.Query(ctx, src, args...) (*Rows, error)` | Runs the statement with its arguments and gives its rows. A statement that is not a query is run as `Exec` runs it. Its rows have no columns and no rows, and `Rows.Result` gives its result; Query gives these rows also with its error. An insert, an update, or a delete with RETURNING that succeeds gives the rows of its RETURNING, with its result in `Rows.Result`; see [RETURNING](#returning). |
| `Conn.ExecAll(ctx, src, args...) (Result, error)` | Runs the statements of the text `src` one after another, as `stmt.Split` reads them, each as `Exec` runs a text of one statement, with as many of `args` as it has parameters, in order. A statement that fails ends the run with its error, and those before it stay. The result is that of the last statement that sets the count of changes. See [Several statements](#several-statements). |
| `Conn.Begin(ctx) error` | Begins a write transaction on the connection. It waits for the place of the single writer as long as `ctx` allows. See [Transactions](#transactions). |
| `Conn.BeginReadOnly(ctx) error` | Begins a read-only transaction on the connection, which sees the state of its beginning. It waits for nothing. |
| `Conn.Commit(ctx) error` | Commits the transaction of the connection. The transaction is over when `Commit` returns, also with an error. |
| `Conn.Rollback(ctx) error` | Rolls the transaction of the connection back: nothing that it did is kept. |
| `Conn.Close(ctx) error` | Closes the connection, and its reader, if it has one. It rolls back a transaction that is open on the connection. |
| `Rows.Columns()` | The columns of each row, each a `Column` with the name as the table stores it and the declared type, such as `INT` or `TEXT`. |
| `Rows.All()` | The rows, as an `iter.Seq2[[]*s4qlpb.Value, error]`, in the order in which the scan of the query reads them, which for a select with ORDER BY is the order of its ORDER BY. The rows of a RETURNING come in the order in which the statement changed them, or in its reverse. See [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows). The sequence ends at the first error, and can be used once. |
| `Rows.Close()` | Ends the rows. |
| `Rows.Result()` | The result of a statement that is not a query. |
| `Rows.Holds()` | Whether the rows hold something until they end: the read transaction of a query, or the connection in a transaction. The rows of a RETURNING outside a transaction hold nothing. |
| `Result{Changes, SetsChanges}` | See [Changes and the last insert rowid](#changes-and-the-last-insert-rowid). |
| `ErrNoStatement` | The error of a text that is empty or holds only spaces, comments, and semicolons. SQLite prepares such a text into no statement and gives no error. |
| `ErrArguments` | Matched by the error of a statement that is given another number of arguments than it has parameters. |
| `Check(src, n) error` | The errors of the text `src` and of the number `n` of its arguments, as `Exec` gives them, without the database and without an event. |
| `CheckAll(src, n) error` | `Check` for `ExecAll`: the errors that `ExecAll` gives for the text `src` and `n` arguments before any statement of the text runs, without the database and without an event. |
| `ErrTooLarge` | Matched by the error of a commit whose commit object the store refuses as too large. The error also matches `core.ErrTooLarge`. |
| `ErrRowsOpen` | The error of a begin, and of a statement in a transaction, while the rows of an earlier query of the connection have not ended. Matched by the error of a commit of a write transaction while a loop reads its rows. |
| `ErrTxOpen` | The error of a begin on a connection whose transaction is open. |
| `ErrNoTx` | The error of a commit or a roll back on a connection without a transaction. |
| `ErrTxDone` | The last error of rows whose transaction ended while a loop over them ran. |
| `ErrInternal` | Matched by the error of a fault of s4ql. See [Errors](#errors). |

An `Engine` is safe for concurrent use. A `Conn` is not, and `Rows` are not.

## Connections

A statement runs on a `Conn`, which is to the engine what a connection of a pool is to its database: it runs one statement at a time. The driver gives each connection of `database/sql` a `Conn` of its own.

- **A `Conn` owns one reader of the core,** a `*core.Reader`, on which every read transaction of the connection runs. It opens the reader for its first statement that reads, so a connection that only writes has none, and closes it in `Conn.Close`. So the connections that are open are the queries that can run at once, and nothing in the engine or in the core limits their number.
- **A connection runs one read at a time.** A statement that reads while the rows of an earlier query of the same connection have not ended fails at once, with an error that matches `core.ErrReaderBusy`. It does not wait for the rows, and [the README of the core](../core/README.md#concurrency) says why. A statement that changes the database needs no reader, and runs. This is the rule for a statement of its own. In a transaction, open rows refuse every statement; see [Rows in a transaction](#rows-in-a-transaction).
- **A reader that cannot be opened fails the statement that needed it,** and the next statement that reads tries again. If the cache cannot be opened, that is a local failure, which stops the instance as any other.
- **`Conn.Close` returns `core.ErrReaderInUse`, and closes nothing, while a loop over the rows of a query of the connection runs.** Rows of the connection that have not ended must not be used after its close: their read transaction has ended with the reader, and what they give then is an internal error. A statement on a closed `Conn` is an internal error, and so is a begin. Closing a closed `Conn` does nothing.
- **`Conn.Close` rolls back a transaction that is open on the connection,** which frees the place of the writer.
- **Why a `Conn`, and not a reader that is passed to each call.** A statement decides whether it reads only after it is parsed, so the engine must be the one to open the reader. A connection is also where the state of a connection belongs, such as a transaction that spans statements. maragubot chose the `Conn` on 2026-11-19, after Markus had agreed that each connection of the pool has a reader of its own. `Engine.Exec` and `Engine.Query` are gone.

## One statement, one transaction

A statement is its own transaction of the core, unless a transaction is open on its connection. This section is about a statement of its own. [Transactions](#transactions) is about a statement in a transaction that spans statements.

- **A statement that changes the database runs between `DB.Begin` and `Tx.Commit`.** The engine annotates the transaction with the text of the statement before the commit; see [Annotations](#annotations). Any failure rolls the transaction back, and so does a panic of an operator. A commit ends the transaction whatever it gives.
- **A statement with RETURNING commits before it gives its first row.** `Conn.write` gives the rows of an insert, an update, or a delete with RETURNING only once `Tx.Commit` has returned nil, so a row that a caller has read belongs to a committed change. If the statement or its commit fails, `Query` gives the error and no rows, also where the outcome of the commit is unknown. The engine holds the rows, and they hold nothing: no transaction, not the reader of the connection, and not the connection. A statement of the connection runs beside them, also one that reads, and so does a begin. `Rows.Holds` reports false for them, so that the driver gives them also when it has closed meanwhile. See [RETURNING](#returning).
- **A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS looks its object up in a read transaction first** (`Conn.nothingToWrite`). Where it does nothing there, it succeeds without the place of the writer, as SQLite only reads the schema for it under a read lock, so it does not wait for a write transaction of another connection. While the rows of an earlier query of the connection hold its reader, it looks on a reader of its own, which it closes when it has looked. Otherwise, and where the read transaction cannot begin, it begins a write transaction as every statement that changes the database does, and looks again there, which decides. Where it does nothing then, `Conn.write` finds that its sink is `nothing` and rolls the transaction back. Either way the statement is neither annotated nor committed, and the log does not change. See [IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS](#if-not-exists-and-if-exists).
- **A query runs in a read transaction of the reader of its connection,** which `Reader.Read` starts. Its `Rows` hold the read transaction, and with it the reader, until they end. They end when `Rows.All` has given the last row or an error, when the loop over it stops early, when the loop panics, or at `Rows.Close`. One of these must happen.
- **`Rows.Close` during a loop over `Rows.All` ends the loop at its next row,** and the read transaction ends then. The loop holds the transaction also while it is suspended in its body, so an iterator that is suspended must be resumed or stopped for the transaction to end.
- **A failure of the end of the read transaction after a loop that stopped early is the error of the next `Rows.Close`.**
- **A panic while a query is built ends its read transaction too.**
- **`Rows.All` yields to the host between two rows,** so that under the simulation other tasks run while a query is open.
- **A failure of the end of the read transaction after the last row is the last error of `Rows.All`.**

## Several statements

`Conn.ExecAll` runs the statements of a text one after another, as `mattn/go-sqlite3` runs them in its `Exec`. `Conn.Exec` and `Conn.Query` stay one statement for each text. The decisions, with what mattn does, are in [several-statements.md](../../docs/todo/several-statements.md).

- **`stmt.Split` reads the statements,** each parsed once, with its spans in the whole text, and the part of the text that it runs as. Each part then runs through the code of `Exec` after the check of its text (`Conn.execPlan`), so it is a transaction of its own, or a statement of the transaction of the connection, with its own event, whose `statement.sql` is the text of its part, and its own annotation. Nothing is wrapped around the statements: a statement that fails ends the run, and the statements before it stay.
- **The arguments** go to the statements in order, as many to each as it has parameters. More than the statements have are refused before any statement runs, if every statement checks; a statement with too few fails after the statements before it ran. An argument that no column holds, or whose value its statement refuses, fails its statement, and its error names it by its number among the arguments of its statement, as if the statement ran alone. `CheckAll` gives the errors that come before any statement runs.
- **What comes before any statement runs** is logged as the event of a statement of the whole text: an error of the whole text from `stmt.Split`, `ErrNoStatement`, and too many arguments. The error of a statement that `stmt.Split` refused comes after the statements before it ran, as the event of a statement whose text is the rest of the text.
- **The result** is that of the last statement that sets the count of changes, also of one that failed, and none if none does: SQLite's `sqlite3_changes` after the statements.
- **The differential tests** judge `ExecAll` on the generated texts of several statements, of the profile `several` and of `mixed`, against running the statements one after another (`pair.several`). The oracle reads the statements with SQLite's own tokenizer and `sqlite3_complete` (`sqliteParts`), not with `stmt.Split`. The instance of the pair runs each statement alone through `pair.step`, which judges it against SQLite by the rules of a statement alone, up to the first that fails; the other instance runs the whole text in one `ExecAll`, whose error, with its offset in the whole text, result, tables, and commits with their annotations must be those of the statements alone. The refusals before any statement runs have rules of their own: the spaces that mattn trims (`spaceRule`, on SQLite's tokens) and more arguments than parameters. `TestConn_differential` runs the texts in transactions too, and compares the commits of both instances after the end of each transaction (`pair.sameLog`). The statements of such a text are not read both ways by `pair.bothWays`, since the other instance runs the whole text; which row of a statement with arithmetic fails first may differ between the two instances, and the other instance's evaluation error is then justified by `pair.justify`.
- **The simulation** runs the texts of several statements of the profile `several`, in the default and the hammer environment, and of `mixed` through `ExecAll` (`run.several` in `simulation_test.go`). Each statement of a text, as `sqliteParts` reads them, is an attempt of its own, with its own ID and arguments, so that every check of a statement alone judges it. The engine's events tell which statements ran: a statement whose event is ok is committed then outside a transaction, since the engine logs its event after its commit returned, also when its instance crashes before the call returns and its caller never learns of it, and the statements after the first that failed must not be in the log. `run.verifyTexts` requires a statement of a text in the log only after every statement before it succeeded, each in a commit of its own, in order. In the profile `several`, the crash of the default environment aims at an instance that runs a text after a statement of it succeeded. Eight bugs of `bugs.go` are of the loop of `ExecAll` and its commit path: `oneCommit`, `goOn`, `commitFailed`, `commitLater`, `annotateText`, `argsFromStart`, `undoInTx`, and `firstResult`; `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` plants each on the profile `several`, `commitLater` in the default environment and the others in the hammer one. The decisions and the counts are in [several-statements.md](../../docs/todo/several-statements.md#the-simulation-and-planted-bugs).
- **Tested** in `several_test.go`, and against mattn in `TestSeveral` of `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck`.

## Transactions

A transaction that spans statements is open on a `Conn` from `Conn.Begin` or `Conn.BeginReadOnly` to `Conn.Commit` or `Conn.Rollback`. Every statement of the connection runs in it meanwhile. The code is in `tx.go`.

The product owner decided the design:

- **A write transaction takes the place of the single writer when it begins,** as SQLite's `begin immediate` does, and holds it until its commit or its roll back. It sees the newest state that is committed, and its own writes. Nothing outside sees its writes before its commit.
- **A read-only transaction is a read transaction.** It sees the state of its beginning, never writes, takes no place of a writer, and blocks no one.

The builder decided the details below.

### The begin

- **`Begin` waits for the place of the writer as long as its context allows.** It has no deadline of its own. If the context ends first, `Begin` gives the error of the context. On an instance that does not serve, it fails as a write of its own does, with an error that matches `core.ErrNoLease` or `core.ErrStopped`.
- **`BeginReadOnly` waits for nothing.** It starts a read transaction on the reader of the connection, and fails as a query does.
- **Both fail with `ErrTxOpen` while a transaction is open on the connection.**
- **Both fail with `ErrRowsOpen` while the rows of an earlier query of the connection have not ended,** also the rows of a statement of its own.
- **When a begin fails, nothing has begun,** and the engine logs no event for it.

### A statement in a transaction

- **The text and the arguments are checked first,** as everywhere. Three checks follow, in this order (`Conn.ready`):
  1. The context has not ended. If it has, the statement fails with the error of the context.
  2. The instance serves now, by the clocks, as a statement of its own needs it. The engine asks `Tx.Serving` or `ReadTx.Serving` of the core. If the instance does not serve, the error matches `core.ErrNoLease` or `core.ErrStopped`.
  3. No rows of an earlier query of the connection are open. If some are, the statement fails with `ErrRowsOpen`.
- **Only `Serving` looks at the clocks.** The other calls of an open transaction of the core do not, because the commit of a write transaction is judged by its manifest swap.
- **In a write transaction, a statement that changes the database runs between a savepoint and its release:** `Tx.Savepoint` and `Tx.Release` of the core, on SQLite's `savepoint s4ql_statement`. The engine annotates the statement before the release. A query takes no savepoint.
- **A statement that fails has no effect, and the transaction stays open.** When the statement fails, or an operator panics, the engine calls `Tx.RollbackTo`. That call restores the cache, and what the transaction had recorded of each table for its commit object, which is its net effect. The work of the statements before it is kept, as in SQLite.
- **The engine annotates last, because an annotation cannot be taken back** from its spool file. A roll back to a savepoint before an annotation makes the core fail the transaction. The engine never makes such a roll back.
- **After an error of the cache, the core lets the transaction only roll back.** Every later statement fails, the commit fails, and the roll back works.
- **`create table`, `drop table`, `create index`, and `drop index` are transactional.** The later statements of the transaction see them, and a roll back undoes them.
- **A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that does nothing releases its savepoint without an annotation** (`Conn.writeIn`), so the commit object of the transaction does not hold it. A transaction of only such statements writes no commit object, as a transaction that changed nothing.
- **The texts `begin`, `commit`, `end`, `rollback`, `savepoint`, and `release` stay "not supported",** also inside a transaction, and the transaction stays open.

### A read-only transaction

- **Every statement sees the state of the beginning,** whatever commits meanwhile.
- **A statement that would write fails with SQLite's `attempt to write a readonly database`,** with the code 8 and the extended code 8, as in SQLite under `pragma query_only = 1`. SQLite's errors of the names come first: `no such table`, `has no column named`, and `already exists`. With indexes they are also `there is already a table named`, `there is already an index named`, `no such column` for a column of a create index, and `no such index`. So do the number of values of an insert and the errors of the columns of a create table. SQLite gives them when it prepares the statement, and refuses the write when it steps it. For an update and a delete, `no such column` of SET and of WHERE comes first too, and so does "not supported" for a value of WHERE of another type than its column. The engine looks the names up in the state of the transaction.
- **A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that does nothing succeeds,** because SQLite only reads the schema for it. One that would write gives `attempt to write a readonly database`, after the errors of its names and of its columns, as the plain statement does.
- **The transaction stays usable after such an error.**
- **`Commit` and `Rollback` end it, and write nothing.** On an instance that has stopped they return nil, because there is nothing to commit.

### The commit and the roll back

- **A write transaction commits as a statement of its own does.** All its changes are one commit object, and every read that starts after `Commit` has returned nil sees them.
- **The transaction is over when `Commit` returns,** with or without an error. `Rollback` ends it too, whatever it returns.
- **`Commit` and `Rollback` on a connection without a transaction give `ErrNoTx`.**
- **A transaction that changes nothing in the end writes no commit object, and its commit succeeds.** This is the core's rule. Examples are a create and a drop of the same table, a transaction whose statements all failed, and one that only read.
- **The commit object holds, in order, the statements of the transaction that succeeded and that write,** each with its arguments. It holds no statement that failed and no query. See [Annotations](#annotations).
- **A commit object that the store refuses as too large gives an error that matches `ErrTooLarge`.** The error also matches `core.ErrTooLarge`, and its message starts with `s4ql: the transaction is too large to commit: `. Nothing is committed. A statement of its own gets the same error. The tests set the limit of the store to 4 KiB with `simstore.Options.MaxSize`, which the commit object of 100 inserts exceeds.
- **An instance that has lost its lease ends the write transaction that is open,** unless it is committing, so that it can take the lease back; the core decides this (`DB.endForLease`). Every later statement and the commit of the transaction fail with an error that matches `core.ErrTxLeaseLost` and `core.ErrNoLease`, `Rollback` returns nil, and the connection is usable after either. Rows of a query of the transaction that a loop reads end at their next row with that error. The event `transaction` of such a transaction has the outcome `lease_lost`, at Debug, with the error, and `transaction.end` is what the application called; a statement refused so has the outcome `lease_lost` too. The transaction's writes are never in the log.
- **On an instance that has lost its lease or has stopped, the commit fails and the roll back works.** The statements fail as said above. `Commit` gives the error of the core. One that matches `core.ErrNoLease` or `core.ErrStopped` means that nothing is committed. One that matches `core.ErrOutcomeUnknown` means what it means for a statement of its own. `Rollback` frees the place of the writer, and returns nil, except after a stop by a local failure, where the roll back in the cache can give its error. The spec: "A write transaction that is already open commits only if its manifest swap still succeeds."

### Rows in a transaction

The rows of a query in a transaction read the state of the transaction. Their end does not end the transaction.

- **While the rows of a query of the transaction are open, every statement of the connection fails with `ErrRowsOpen`,** also a write. The scan holds a statement of SQLite on the connection of the transaction. A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that would do nothing fails so too, because `Conn.ready` checks the rule before the statement reads anything.
- **`Commit` and `Rollback` first end the rows that no loop reads.** A later `Rows.All` on them gives nothing.
- **Rows that a loop over `Rows.All` reads cannot be ended from outside.** A loop reads them while its body runs, and while its iterator is suspended. `Commit` and `Rollback` mark such rows. The loop then ends at its next row with `ErrTxDone`, and reads nothing more.
- **The end of the transaction runs when that loop ends,** after the scan of the core has ended. It is the roll back of a write transaction, or the end of a read transaction. `Rows.Close` after the loop gives the error of that end, which is nil when the end worked.
- **Until the loop ends, a write transaction keeps the place of the writer.** Every statement and every begin of the connection fail with `ErrRowsOpen`, and `Conn.Close` gives `core.ErrReaderInUse`.
- **A `Commit` of a write transaction does not commit in that case.** It rolls the transaction back, and fails with an error that matches `ErrRowsOpen` and ends with `so the transaction is rolled back and not committed`. A `Commit` of a read-only transaction ends it, and returns nil.
- **Why the end is left to the loop.** SQLite cannot commit while a statement of the connection reads: the commit of the core drops temporary tables, and SQLite refuses that then. A roll back can run then, but it aborts the statement, and the loop would finish the aborted statement later, on the write connection, while the transaction of the next writer runs there. So the roll back waits for the loop. A commit cannot be left to the loop in the same way, because the transaction is over when `Commit` returns: the transaction is rolled back, and the error of `Commit` says so.
- **A driver whose `Rows.Close` ends the loop does not meet this case.** `database/sql` closes the rows of a `sql.Tx` before it calls `Commit` or `Rollback` of the driver.
- **The rows of a RETURNING in a write transaction are rows of the connection, as the rows of a query are** (`Conn.queryText`). The statement gives them at once, after the release of its savepoint, and they read nothing. The rules above hold for them all the same: while they are open, every statement of the connection fails with `ErrRowsOpen`, and a `Commit` while a loop reads them rolls the transaction back. So one rule covers all open rows of a transaction. A roll back takes the change back, also when its rows were read.

## The stages

A statement goes through three stages. A test can call each on its own.

1. **`stmt.Check`** reads the text into a typed statement, or gives SQLite's syntax error, "not supported", or no statement.
2. **`NewPlan`** turns the statement into a `Plan`. A plan is plain data, and `Format` prints it. It holds each name as a `stmt.Name`, as the statement writes it, and nothing of the catalogue: whether a table or a column exists is not known when the plan is made.
3. **Execution** runs the plan in a transaction of the core. `buildQuery` and `buildSink` turn the plan into a tree of operators inside the transaction. They look the names up there, and give SQLite's errors about them in SQLite's order. The engine then runs the tree.

The plans of the four statements, as `Format` prints them:

```
CREATE TABLE "T""1" (V1 INT PRIMARY KEY) WITHOUT ROWID, STRICT

create table "T""1" (V1 INT, primary key (V1))
```

```
drop table t1;

drop table t1
```

```
insert into t1 ("v") values (-  9223372036854775808)

insert into t1 ("v")
  values (-9223372036854775808)
```

```
select * from été

query
  project *
    scan été
```

The plans of the statements of several columns, of slice 5:

```
create table t1 (v1 int not null, v2 text primary key, v3 real not null) strict, without rowid

create table t1 (v1 INT, v2 TEXT, v3 REAL, primary key (v2))
```

```
insert into t1 values (1, 'a', ?)

insert into t1
  values (1, 'a', ?1)
```

```
insert into t1 (v3, "V1", v2) values (?, 2.5, ?)

insert into t1 (v3, "V1", v2)
  values (?1, 2.5, ?2)
```

```
select v2, "V1", v2 from t1

query
  project v2, "V1", v2
    scan t1
```

The plans of the statements with NULL, of slice 6:

```
create table t1 (v1 int, v2 text primary key, v3 real not null, v4 blob) strict, without rowid

create table t1 (v1 INT NULL, v2 TEXT, v3 REAL, v4 BLOB NULL, primary key (v2))
```

```
insert into t1 values (NULL, ?, null)

insert into t1
  values (null, ?1, null)
```

The plan of a select with WHERE, of slice 7:

```
select v2 from t1 where v1 >= ? and v2 is not null

query
  project v2
    filter v1 >= ?1 and v2 IS NOT NULL
      scan t1
```

The plans of an update and a delete, of slice 8:

```
update "T1" set v1 = ?, v2 = null where v1 = ? and v2 is not null

update "T1" set v1 = ?1, v2 = null
  filter v1 = ?2 and v2 IS NOT NULL
    scan "T1"
```

```
delete from t1

delete from t1
  scan t1
```

The plans of a create index and a drop index, of slice 9:

```
create index t1_v2_v3 on t1 (v2 asc, "V3" desc);

create index t1_v2_v3 on t1 (v2, "V3" desc)
```

```
Drop Index t1_v2_v3;

drop index t1_v2_v3
```

The plans of a create table with IF NOT EXISTS and a drop table with IF EXISTS, of slice 10. A create index and a drop index print the clause in the same place:

```
CREATE TABLE If Not Exists "t" (v int primary key) strict, without rowid

create table if not exists "t" (v INT, primary key (v))
```

```
drop table IF EXISTS t1

drop table if exists t1
```

The plan of a create table with defaults, of slice 11:

```
create table t (k int default -1 primary key, b real default .5, g text not null default null) strict, without rowid

create table t (k INT DEFAULT -1, b REAL NULL DEFAULT 0.5, g TEXT DEFAULT null, primary key (k))
```

The plan of a select with values on the left of its comparisons, of slice 12:

```
select v1 from t1 where 1 < v1 and ? >= v2 and null = v3 and v2 = ?

query
  project v1
    filter v1 > 1 and v2 <= ?1 and v3 = null and v2 = ?2
      scan t1
```

The plans of a select with ORDER BY and LIMIT, of slice 13:

```
select v1, v2 from t1 order by v2 desc, v1 limit 10

query
  project v1, v2
    limit 10
      order by v2 desc, v1
        scan t1
```

```
select v1 from t1 where v2 > ? order by v1 limit ?

query
  project v1
    limit ?2
      order by v1
        filter v2 > ?1
          scan t1
```

A plan is a `*CreateTable`, a `*DropTable`, a `*CreateIndex`, a `*DropIndex`, an `*Insert`, an `*Update`, a `*Delete`, or a `*Query`. An insert, an update, a delete, and a query read their rows from a tree of nodes: `*Scan`, `*Filter`, `*Order`, `*Limit`, `*Values`, and `*Project`. A `*Filter` holds its conditions, each a `Condition`. The expressions are `*Integer`, `*Real`, `*Text`, `*Blob`, `*Null`, `*Parameter`, `*Subquery`, which is in [Scalar subqueries](#scalar-subqueries), and `*Arithmetic`, which holds an expression of arithmetic or of text and is in [Arithmetic](#arithmetic) and [Text](#text). `*Null` is the literal NULL, which `Format` prints as `null`. `Format` prints a `*Parameter` as `?1`, by its number.

- **A `*CreateTable` holds every column, in the order of the statement, and the position of its key** in `PrimaryKey`.
- **A column of a plan is NOT NULL unless it is `Nullable`.** `ColumnDef.Nullable` is true for a column that is not the primary key and has no `not null`. `Format` prints ` NULL` after the type of such a column, and nothing more for any other, so the plans of slices 1 to 5 print as before.
- **The key is never `Nullable`,** as SQLite marks the key of a WITHOUT ROWID table NOT NULL. So a table with `not null` on its key and one without have the same plan and the same commit object, and the cache declares both in the same way. SQLite records another text of the schema for each, which s4ql does not expose.
- **The commit object has `nullable` on each column that takes NULL.** `buildSink` sets it only when it is true, as the core gives the table back. A replay of the log into an empty cache gives the same table.
- **A `ColumnDef` has `Default`, the literal of its DEFAULT as an expression:** a `*Null` for DEFAULT NULL, and nil for a column without a DEFAULT. `NewPlan` sets it from `stmt.Column.Default`. `Format` prints ` DEFAULT ` and the literal after the ` NULL` of the column, and nothing more for a column without one, so the plans of slices 1 to 10 print as before.
- **The commit object has `default` on each column that has one.** `buildSink` evaluates the literal and hands it to the core as `Column.default` of the commit format. See [DEFAULT with a literal](#default-with-a-literal).
- **A `*CreateIndex` holds the name of the index, the name of the table, and the columns of the index, in the order of the statement.** Each column is a `stmt.IndexColumn`, with its name and whether it is descending. `Format` prints ` desc` after a descending column, and nothing after any other, so `asc` and no direction print the same. `SQL` holds the text that SQLite keeps for the index, which `Format` does not print. A `*DropIndex` holds the name of the index.
- **A `*CreateTable` and a `*CreateIndex` have `IfNotExists`, and a `*DropTable` and a `*DropIndex` have `IfExists`,** which is true for a statement that says the clause. `Format` prints `if not exists` or `if exists` before the name of such a statement, and nothing more for any other, so the plans of slices 1 to 9 print as before.
- **A plan holds no access.** A `*Scan` names its table, and a `*Filter` its conditions. How the rows are read is chosen when the operators are built, in the transaction, where the indexes of the table are known; see [Indexes and the choice of an access](#indexes-and-the-choice-of-an-access).
- **An `*Insert` has `Columns` nil when the statement has no list of columns.** Its `*Values` node has one row, which holds all its values in the order of the statement.
- **A `*Project` has `Star` for `*`, and otherwise `Columns`:** the names in the order of the select list, each as often as the list names it.
- **A select with WHERE has a `*Filter` below its `*Project`,** with the `*Scan` as its `Source` and its `Conditions` in the order of the text. A `Condition` has `Column`, a `stmt.Name`, `Operator`, a `stmt.Operator`, and `Value`, an expression, which is nil for IS NULL and IS NOT NULL. `Format` prints the filter on a line of its own, each column as the statement writes it and each operator as its `String` gives it, so `==` prints as `=` and `<>` as `!=`. A select without WHERE has no filter, and its plan prints as before.
- **A `Condition` has `ValueFirst`, which is true for a comparison that the statement writes with its value on the left,** as in `5 < a`. `NewPlan` sets it from `stmt.Condition.ValueFirst`. `Operator` is then the operator turned round, as the statement pass gives it, so the plan of `5 < a` is the condition `a > 5`. `Format` prints every condition with its column first, and does not print `ValueFirst`. See [A value on the left of a comparison](#a-value-on-the-left-of-a-comparison).
- **A select with ORDER BY has an `*Order` below its `*Project`,** over the `*Filter`, or over the `*Scan` of a select without WHERE. Its `Columns` are in the order of the text. Each is an `OrderColumn`, with `Column`, a `stmt.Name`, and `Descending`. A column need not be in the select list. `Format` prints `order by` and the columns on a line of their own, each as the statement writes it, with ` desc` after a descending column and nothing after any other, so `asc` and no direction print the same.
- **A select with LIMIT has a `*Limit` below its `*Project`,** over the `*Order`, the `*Filter`, or the `*Scan`, whichever is the topmost that the select has. Its `Count` is an `*Integer` from 0 or a `*Parameter`. `Format` prints `limit` and the count on a line of their own. The `?` of LIMIT has the number after those of WHERE. A select with neither clause has neither node, and its plan prints as before.
- **An `*Order` says what the order is, and not how the rows get into it.** The engine reads the rows in that order by the primary key or by an index where one holds them so, and has the storage sort them otherwise. That is part of the access, which a plan does not hold.
- **An `*Update` and a `*Delete` have the rows that they change as their `Source`:** a `*Scan` of the table, or a `*Filter` over it if the statement has a WHERE. `rowsOf` builds it, as it builds the rows of a select. An `*Update` holds its assignments in `Set`, in the order of the text. An `Assignment` has `Column`, a `stmt.Name`, and `Value`, an expression. `Format` prints each assignment with `=`, also one that the statement writes with `==`. The parameters of SET have the numbers before those of WHERE.

**Why a tree of iterators, and not a bytecode machine like SQLite's.** The lead recommended it on 2026-11-18, and Markus left the choice to the lead: "I'll trust your recommendation." Each operator is tested alone, a plan is data that tests can read, and the oracle stays rows and errors compared with SQLite, which does not change between SQLite's releases. A bytecode machine that mirrors SQLite's would give `EXPLAIN` as a second oracle and SQLite's order of checks more cheaply, but it ties the engine to an instruction set that SQLite says is not an interface. Where SQLite's order of checks or of errors is visible, the engine reproduces it on purpose, and the differential tests find the places. The plan is the seam: an executor of another kind can replace the operators behind it without touching the parser or the pass after it. The trade-offs are in [research/sql-front-end.md](../../docs/research/sql-front-end.md) §6.3.

## The storage interface

The operators and the engine do not call the core. They call seven interfaces in `storage.go`:

| Interface | Holds |
| --- | --- |
| `reader` | `table`, `indexes`, `index`, `scan`, `get`, and `rowsRead` of one transaction. `table` gives nil when there is no table of the name, and `index` gives nil when there is no index of the name. `indexes` gives the indexes of a table in the order in which they were created, which is the same on every instance. `scan` reads the rows of a table by one of its indexes, or by its primary key, as a `core.IndexScan` says, in the order of that key or in its reverse, or sorted by the columns of its `Order`. `rowsRead` is the number of rows that the transaction has read from the storage, by its scans and its gets. |
| `writer` | A `reader`, with `createTable`, `dropTable`, which drops the indexes of the table too, `createIndex`, `dropIndex`, `insertNew`, which inserts a row only if its key is new, `upsert`, which inserts a row or replaces the row with its key, `delete`, which deletes the row of a key and reports whether there was one, and `keys`, which runs a function with an empty `keySet` of a table. It reads its own writes. |
| `keySet` | A set of primary keys of a table, for a statement that changes the rows that it finds. `add` adds a key, unless the set has it. `all` gives the keys in the order of the key, as SQLite compares keys. The table and the set can change between two keys: `all` gives the least key of the set above the one before. The set lasts for the call of `keys`. A set in the order of the key of an index, as `keys` gives it for columns `by`, takes the values of those columns of each row, and `all` gives the primary keys in that order. |
| `database` | `begin`, which gives a write transaction, and `reader`, which gives a reader that its owner closes. |
| `readConn` | A reader of the database: `read`, which gives a read transaction and fails while the one before it on the reader is open, and `close`. |
| `readTx` | A `reader`, with `position`, `serving`, and `end`. |
| `writeTx` | A `writer`, with `position`, `annotate`, `serving`, `savepoint`, `release`, `rollbackTo`, `commit`, and `rollback`. One savepoint is open at a time. |

Only a transaction that spans statements calls `serving`, `savepoint`, `release`, and `rollbackTo`; see [A statement in a transaction](#a-statement-in-a-transaction).

A name of a table or of an index is compared as SQLite compares names: without regard to the case of the ASCII letters, and every other character exactly.

The adapters `coreDB`, `coreTx`, `coreReader`, `coreReadTx`, and `coreKeys` put the interfaces on a `*core.DB`, a `*core.Tx`, a `*core.Reader`, a `*core.ReadTx`, and a `*core.KeySet`. They change two things:

- **A lookup of a table or of an index that does not exist gives nil,** where the core gives an error that wraps `core.ErrNoTable` or `core.ErrNoIndex` (`lookUp`, `lookUpIndex`). The operators turn the nil into SQLite's error.
- **A refusal of the core becomes an internal error** (`fromCore`). The refusals are `core.ErrTableExists`, `core.ErrNoTable`, `core.ErrIndexExists`, `core.ErrNoIndex`, `core.ErrReservedName`, and `core.ErrInvalid`. The engine checks for each before it calls the core, so one that the core still returns is a fault of the engine or of the pass after the parser. So are `core.ErrTxDone`, a call on a transaction that has ended, and `core.ErrReaderClosed`, a read on a reader that is closed. The error of `Tx.Commit` passes through as it is: its `core.ErrInvalid` is a commit time that the commit object cannot hold, which comes from the clock. One refusal of `createTable` and of `createIndex` is "not supported" instead: that of a statement in the cache that is longer than the length limit, which wraps `core.ErrTooLongForCache` beside `core.ErrInvalid`; see [Errors](#errors).

Every other error of the core passes through as it is.

The adapters pass `serving`, `savepoint`, `release`, and `rollbackTo` of a write transaction to `Tx.Serving`, `Tx.Savepoint`, `Tx.Release`, and `Tx.RollbackTo` of the core, and `serving` of a read transaction to `ReadTx.Serving`. Each goes through `fromCore`. So a refusal of a savepoint, such as a second one while the first is open, is an internal error.

The adapters pass `scan` to `ReadTx.ScanIndex` and `Tx.ScanIndex` of the core, with the `core.IndexScan` as it is. It names the index, and no name means the primary key. `Equal` pins the leading columns of the key to values, `Lower` and `Upper` bound the column after those, and `Backward` reverses the order. `Order`, of slice 13, has the cache sort the rows by its columns, and `Backward` then turns only the rows that tie. The cache names the index in its statement, so SQLite reads that index and no other, and SQLite compares the values. The core refuses a scan that it cannot run with `core.ErrInvalid` or `core.ErrTooBig`: a bound that is NULL and a NULL in `Equal` for a column that does not take it are among these. So the engine never asks for such a scan. [The README of the core](../core/README.md#the-interface) has the scan in full.

The adapters pass `indexes` and `index` to `Indexes` and `Index` of the core, `rowsRead` to `RowsRead`, and `createIndex` and `dropIndex` to `Tx.CreateIndex` and `Tx.DropIndex`. Each call that can fail goes through `fromCore`.

The adapters pass `delete` and `keys` to `Tx.Delete` and `Tx.Keys` of the core, and `add` and `all` of a `keySet` to `KeySet.Add` and `KeySet.All`. Each goes through `fromCore`. The cache keeps the set in a temporary table; see [the README of the core](../core/README.md#a-write-transaction).

The interfaces exist for the tests:

- **The operators are tested alone, against a fake.** `fake` in `operator_internal_test.go` keeps tables in memory, records every call, and fails or panics on demand. Its transaction, `fakeTx`, records a look-up of the indexes of a table as `indexes` and the name of the table. It records a scan that pins or bounds something, or that names an index: `range` for a scan by the primary key, or `index` and the name of the index, then the name of the table, `=` and the value of each pinned column, and each bound as an operator and an integer. A scan backward records `backward` last, and a scan of every row forward records nothing. It records a `create index` with the names of the index and the table, and a `drop index` with the name of the index. It records a `delete` as `delete`, the name of the table, and the key, and `keys` as `keys` and the name of the table. Its set of keys records each `add` as `add` and the key, and each key that `all` gives as `key` and the key. Its four calls of a transaction that spans statements do nothing and record nothing. `spanning` in `tx_internal_test.go` wraps it, records them, and fails them on demand.
- **The fuzz tests run on a database in memory too.** `memory` in `memory_internal_test.go` is fast enough for many more texts and sequences than the core is. Its savepoint is a copy of its tables. It holds the definition of an index and nothing else. For a scan, it sorts every row of the table by the key of the index, each column in its direction and with its own comparison, and keeps the rows that the pinned values and the bounds let through. For a scan with an order, it then sorts those rows by the columns of the order, and the rows that tie by the primary key, or by its reverse for a scan backward, and counts every one of them as read before it gives the first, as the core does. That is a second way to the rows of a scan by an index, and the fuzz tests compare it with SQLite. Its set of keys is a map, and its `all` looks for the least key above the one before each time.

## The operators

Operators are of two kinds. A source gives rows to the operator above it. A sink runs a statement that changes the database, and gives the number of rows that it changed.

| Operator | Kind | What it does |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `scan` | source | Gives the rows of a table as its `access` reads them: no row, the row of one primary key by a `get`, or a scan by the primary key or by an index, in the order of that key or in its reverse, or, for an access whose rows are sorted, in the order of the ORDER BY. The zero `access` reads every row in the order of the primary key. It checks the context before each row, and ends with the error of the context when the context has ended. The cache stops a sort of the rows of the access when the context ends during it, and the scan then ends with the error of the context too. |
| `filter` | source | Gives the rows of its source for which every one of its conditions is true, in the order of the source. |
| `limit` | source | Gives the first rows of its source, at most as many as its count, in the order of the source, and ends the loop over the source with the last of them. |
| `values` | source | Gives rows of constants. It checks the context before each row too. |
| `project` | source | Gives the columns of each row of its source at its positions: those that the query names, in the order of its list, each as often as the list names it. For `*`, they are all the columns, in the order of the table. |
| `insert` | sink | Writes each row of its source into a table as a new row. The key of the table can be at any position. |
| `update` | sink | Sets columns of each row of its source, the rows that an UPDATE finds in a table. The key can be among the columns. |
| `remove` | sink | Deletes each row of its source, the rows that a DELETE finds in a table. |
| `createTable` | sink | Creates a table. |
| `dropTable` | sink | Drops a table, by the name that the table stores. |
| `createIndex` | sink | Creates an index. |
| `dropIndex` | sink | Drops an index, by the name that the index stores. |
| `nothing` | sink | Writes nothing. It is the sink of a statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that does nothing; see [IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS](#if-not-exists-and-if-exists). |

- **The operators build only the plans that a statement gives.** A plan of another shape fails with an internal error: an insert of several rows or of no rows, an insert into a table whose key is not exactly one of its columns, an insert that names a column twice, a table with a key of several columns or outside its columns, a table whose key takes NULL, an index that names a column twice, a projection of no columns, a filter of another node than a scan, a test for NULL with a value, a comparison without one, an update or a delete of another node than a scan or a filter of one, an update or a delete of a table whose key is not exactly one of its columns, an update that sets a column twice, and an update that sets no column. What SQLite gives for these has not been compared with SQLite, so the engine gives no answer of SQLite's for them. A slice that adds such a shape replaces its internal error with what the differential test has compared with SQLite.
- **`project` resolves the names of the select list in their order,** once `scan` has found the table, and gives the columns their names as the table stores them. `select B from t` with a column `b` gives a column named `b`, and a column that is named twice is given twice, with the same name.
- **The names of the select list are resolved before those of the conditions,** as SQLite resolves them, though the filter is below the projection in the plan. `build.node` builds the scan, then the positions of the projection, and then the nodes between the two, in `build.ordered`. Since slice 13 these are the filter, the order, and the limit, each only if the select has its clause. `build.ordered` makes its checks in SQLite's order. `build.conditions` looks the columns of the conditions up, and then the columns of the ORDER BY are looked up, in the order of the text. `build.values` evaluates the values of the conditions and checks their lengths, and then the argument of the LIMIT is evaluated and its length checked. `build.types` checks the types of the values of the conditions, and then `limitCount` checks the argument of the LIMIT. Last, `build.reach` gives the scan its access. An update and a delete have no order and no limit: they go through `build.conditions` and `build.bind`, which is `build.values`, `build.types`, and `build.reach`. The order of the errors is under [Errors](#errors).
- **No operator sorts.** The `*Order` of a plan becomes no operator. It becomes part of the access of the scan: the scan reads by a key that holds the rows in the order of the ORDER BY, or the storage sorts the rows of the scan, as `core.IndexScan.Order` says. `filter` gives its rows in the order of its source, so the order of the scan is the order of the query. See [Indexes and the choice of an access](#indexes-and-the-choice-of-an-access).
- **`limit` is above the filter, so it counts the rows that pass the conditions.** It ends the loop over its source with the last row that it gives, so the scan reads no row after that one. With a count of 0 it gives no row and does not start its source, which then reads nothing. It checks the context first, as `scan` does.
- **`filter` has SQLite's logic of three values.** A comparison with NULL on either side is unknown: a NULL in the row, the literal NULL, or a nil argument. The WHERE leaves out a row for which a condition is unknown, as it leaves out one for which a condition is false. IS NULL and IS NOT NULL are always true or false.
- **`compare` compares two values of one type as SQLite does.** Integers and reals compare by their number, with 0.0 equal to -0.0. Text and blobs compare by their bytes, as memcmp does, and the shorter is first where it begins the other, which is SQLite's BINARY collation for text. Two values of different kinds are an internal error, which the exact types rule out.
- **`scan` reads by the primary key or by an index where the conditions of the filter above it allow it, or where the ORDER BY of its query makes it the better way.** `build.access` gives the scan its `access`, by the rules of `chooseAccess`; see [Indexes and the choice of an access](#indexes-and-the-choice-of-an-access). A scan of a statement without WHERE and without ORDER BY reads every row.
- **`buildValues` places each value of an insert at the position of its column in the table,** and gives a column that the insert leaves out its default, as SQLite does, or NULL where the column has none. The expression of that column is nil. So each row that `insert` gets has a value for each column of the table, in the order of the table. See [DEFAULT with a literal](#default-with-a-literal).
- **`insert` checks each row before it writes,** in `insert.check`. First, no column that does not take NULL holds NULL: the first such column gets `NOT NULL constraint failed`, and the key is among these columns. Then each value that is not NULL has the type of its column. Both checks go through the columns in the order of the table. A NULL that the statement gives, as a literal or as an argument, and the NULL of a column that it leaves out, from DEFAULT NULL or for want of a default, are the same to both. See [Types](#types).
- **`insert` writes every row through `insertNew`,** which is `core.Tx.Insert`: SQLite's `insert ... on conflict do nothing`. It returns false for a key that the table has, also from an earlier row of the same statement, and `insert` then fails with `UNIQUE constraint failed`. For a row whose record is too long it fails with `core.ErrTooBig`, which is `string or blob too big`. SQLite builds the record of a row of a WITHOUT ROWID table before it looks for its key, so the two errors come in SQLite's order, and `insert` calls no `get` before the write. The lead decided this on 2026-11-20, after an independent review. An insert calls `upsert` only for the planted bug `skipUnique`, which writes the row so that it replaces the row with its key. See [Types](#types) for the order of the two errors.
- **`update` and `remove` find their rows as a select finds them.** `build.change` builds their source from the same `scan` and `filter`, with `build.conditions`, `build.bind`, and the same choice of an access, and makes the lookups in SQLite's order of errors; see [Errors](#errors). Their scan reads forward. A path that the tests force holds for an update and a delete too.
- **`changeRows` collects the keys of the rows first, and changes the rows after.** While the scan runs, it adds the key of each row that the source gives to a `keySet`, and only the set changes. Then `all` gives the keys back, one at a time, and `changeRows` changes the row of each: `update` sets its values, and `remove` deletes it with `delete`. The core reads each key by a statement of its own, which has ended when the row changes. So SQLite never has a cursor open on a table that changes, which its documentation leaves undefined. A row that the statement writes, also under a new key, is never in the set. So each row that the statement finds is changed once, and none is missed, also when SET writes the key or the columns that WHERE reads. The core keeps the set in the cache, so a statement changes any number of rows and holds none in memory.
- **The rows of an update change in SQLite's order** (`changeOrder`, `build.changeOrder`). SQLite changes each row as its access finds it, in one pass, unless the update sets a column of the primary key or of the index that its access reads by, its WHERE has a subquery, or it has RETURNING; it then collects the keys first, and changes the rows in the order of the primary key. The engine follows both: in one pass, the order of the key of the access that the rules choose, forward, and otherwise that of the primary key. The order is that of the rules' access also under a path that the tests force, so it does not depend on the path. The order decides only which error a failing update gives, such as `NOT NULL constraint failed` of one row before `string or blob too big` of another. A delete deletes in the order of the key, since no row of a delete fails on its own. Where SQLite's planner reads by another access than the rules, as SQLite reads an update without WHERE through an index that is narrower than the table, the order differs; see `docs/todo/update-order.md`.
- **In one pass, the error of the WHERE of a row comes after the changes of the rows before it,** as SQLite finds, checks, and writes a row before it reads the next. `changeRows` stops collecting at the first evaluation error or `string or blob too big` of the conditions of a row, changes the rows that it collected, and gives the error of the first of them that fails, or else that of the WHERE. In two passes, the error of a WHERE comes before any row changes.
- **The keys of an update in one pass by an index are a set of the core in the order of the index** (`core.Tx.KeysBy`): `changeRows` adds the values of the key of the index of each row, and the set gives the primary keys in that order, as SQLite orders the values of each column, whatever the order in which the scan found them.
- **`update` reads the row of each key with `get`, sets its values, and writes it.** A row whose key stays is written in place, with `upsert`. A row under a new key is the old row deleted, with `delete`, and the new one inserted, with `insertNew`. As for an insert, `insertNew` fails with `string or blob too big` for a record that is too long before it looks for the key, and returns false for a key that the table has, also from an earlier row of the same statement; `update` then fails with `UNIQUE constraint failed`. A new key that is the old one by SQLite's comparison, such as -0.0 for 0.0, is no change of the key, and the row is written in place, as SQLite does.
- **An update that fails leaves no change, also when it fails after it has changed rows.** The transaction of a statement of its own rolls back, and a statement in a transaction that spans statements rolls back to its savepoint.
- **`update.check` checks the values of SET before any row changes,** as `insert.check` checks a row. First, no column that the update sets and that does not take NULL gets NULL, in the order of the table, the key among these columns. SQLite checks NOT NULL on each row that it changes, so the NULL fails only an update that finds a row. The check reads that from its source with `finds`, which stops at the first row, so the `check` of a sink takes a context. Then each value that is not NULL has the type of its column, in the order of the table, whether the update finds a row or not. `remove` has nothing to check.
- **The commit object holds the net effect of an update and a delete, and its format did not change.** An update is the row as it is afterwards. A change of the key is that row and the delete of the old key. A delete is the delete of the key. The core records the keys that a transaction touches, and gives the net effect for each. `TestConn_netEffect` reads it from the log.
- **A source is a push-style iterator,** an `iter.Seq2`. The operator above ranges over the rows, and ends the source by leaving the loop. Everything runs on the goroutine of the caller.
- **No operator uses `iter.Pull`.** It starts a goroutine, and [`simlint`](../simlint/simlint.go) forbids it in code that runs under the simulation.

## Indexes and the choice of an access

Slice 9 adds `create index` and `drop index`. With an index, a statement with a WHERE can read its rows in more than one way, and the engine chooses the way by fixed rules. Since slice 13, the ORDER BY of a select and whether it has a LIMIT are part of the choice, and the choice says how the rows get into the order: by the key that the scan reads in, or by a sort. The rules are in `access.go`.

### The statements

- **`create index i on t (a, b desc)` and `drop index i` run as a create table and a drop table do.** Their plans are `*CreateIndex` and `*DropIndex`. `buildCreateIndex` and `buildDropIndex` in `operator.go` build their sinks, `createIndex` and `dropIndex`. Both statements are transactional, both are annotated as every write is, and both leave the count of changes.
- **The engine gives the storage the definition of the index.** It is an `s4qlpb.CreateIndex`: the name of the index as the statement writes it, the name of the table as the table stores it, and each column as its position in the table, with `descending` only when it is true. The commit object holds this definition, and each instance builds the index in its cache; see [the README of the core](../core/README.md#the-interface). An index is not unique.
- **A table and an index share their names.** A create index under the name of a table fails, and so does a create table under the name of an index. A drop index of the name of a table gives `no such index`.
- **`drop table` drops the indexes of the table.** Their names are free for new tables and indexes afterwards.
- **An index has at most 250 columns** (`core.MaxIndexColumns`). The storage core holds no more in an index, where SQLite takes 2,000. A create index of more columns is "not supported", as `index of more than 250 columns`, at the 251st column.
- **The errors of both statements are SQLite's, in SQLite's order.** They are under [Errors](#errors).

### The rules

`chooseAccess(t, indexes, conditions, o)` is one pure function of the table, its indexes in the order in which they were created, the conditions of the WHERE, and `o`, an `orderBy`: the columns of the ORDER BY and whether the query has a LIMIT. The number of the LIMIT is not in it. It has no statistics and reads no row. So the choice is the same on every instance and for every state of the rows.

A candidate is the primary key or an index. The key of the primary key is its columns. The key of an index is its columns and then the columns of the primary key that it does not hold, as SQLite stores an index of a WITHOUT ROWID table (`core.IndexKey`, the one definition, which the core scans by too). The rules take a primary key of any number of columns. A table that a statement creates has a primary key of one column.

The rules, in the order in which `chooseAccess` tries them:

1. **No row,** if a condition is never true (`never`): a comparison with NULL, or IS NULL of a column of the primary key.
2. **The row of one primary key, by a `get`,** if `=` pins every column of the primary key (`pointOf`). This holds also when an index pins more columns.
3. **Otherwise, a scan by the candidate that fits the conditions and the ORDER BY best.** Each candidate is fitted to the conditions (`fit`) and placed in the order of the ORDER BY (`orderBy.place`). The candidate that pins the most leading columns of its key wins. Of those that pin as many, `orderBy.better` decides by a bound on the next column and by whether the candidate delivers the order, in the ranking of the query in the table below. Of those that are still the same, the primary key wins, and then the index that was created first.
4. **Every row, in the order of the primary key,** if the winner of rule 3 is the primary key, and pins nothing and bounds nothing.

The rows of the winner are sorted, unless it delivers the order. No row and the row of one primary key have nothing to sort, and deliver every order.

The ranking of rule 3, among the candidates that pin as many columns:

| Query | Wins first | Wins next | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Without ORDER BY, with a LIMIT or without | A bound on the next column. | | As in slice 9. A LIMIT alone changes nothing in the choice. |
| With ORDER BY and LIMIT | Delivering the order. | A bound on the next column. | A scan that delivers the order reads only up to the last row that the query gives. |
| With ORDER BY and without LIMIT | A bound on the next column. | Delivering the order. | The query reads every row of its access, and a bound leaves rows out. Among candidates that are the same in that, one that delivers the order saves the sort. |

When a candidate delivers the order (`delivers`):

- **Take the columns that the conditions pin out of the key of the candidate and out of the ORDER BY.** A pinned column is one that `pinOf` pins, with `=` or with IS NULL of a column that takes NULL. It has one value in every row that passes the filter, so it orders nothing.
- **The candidate delivers the order if what is left of the ORDER BY is the start of what is left of the key, column for column,** with every column in the direction in which the key holds it, or every column in the other direction. In the first case the scan runs forward, and in the second backward. The key is `core.IndexKey`, as for the fit.
- **An ORDER BY that ends before the key does is delivered.** One of pinned columns alone is delivered by every key, forward.
- **An ORDER BY that names a column that the key does not have next is not delivered.**
- **An ORDER BY with one column in the direction of the key and one in the other is not delivered.**
- **NULL needs no rule.** A key holds NULL before every value in a column that it holds ascending, and after every value in one that it holds descending. That is where ORDER BY puts NULL for ASC and for DESC, and a scan backward turns both round together.
- **A pinned column is taken out wherever it is in the key, not only at its start.** With an index on `(a, b)`, `where b = 5 order by a, k` is delivered: every row that passes has one value of `b`. Whether the scan itself pins the column does not matter, because the filter drops every row with another value in it, and the rows that are left are in the order of the rest of the key. SQLite's own planner skips only the leading pinned columns. [The todo of slice 13](../../docs/todo/slice-13.md) has the decision.
- **An ORDER BY is not cut after the primary key.** `order by a, k, b` with an index on `(a)` is sorted, though no two rows tie after `k`, so `b` orders nothing. The sort gives the right order, and only costs more than it must. The cut can be built later without a change of a format.

How a candidate is fitted:

- **A column is pinned by `=`, or by IS NULL of a column that takes NULL** (`pinOf`). The first condition, in the order of the text, that pins the column gives its value. `a = 1 and a = 2` pins `a` to 1, and `b is null and b = 'x'` pins `b` to NULL.
- **IS NULL of a column that does not take NULL pins nothing.** No row has NULL there, and the storage refuses a scan that pins such a column to NULL. For a column of the primary key, rule 1 reads no row. For any other such column, the statement reads the rows that its other conditions leave, and the filter rejects every one of them.
- **`fit` pins the leading columns of the key, one after the other, while a condition pins each.** At the first column that no condition pins, it takes the tightest bounds of the conditions that compare that column with `<`, `<=`, `>`, or `>=`, and stops. Of two bounds at the same value, the open one is tighter (`tighter`). `!=`, IS NOT NULL, and a condition on a later column of the key pin nothing and bound nothing.
- **A comparison with its value on the left pins and bounds as the same comparison with its column first.** The statement pass gives `5 < a` as the condition `a > 5`, so `fit` bounds `a` from below. See [A value on the left of a comparison](#a-value-on-the-left-of-a-comparison).
- **The pinned values and the bounds reach into the columns of the primary key at the end of the key of an index.** With an index on `(a)` and the primary key `k`, `a = 1 and k > 3` pins `a` and bounds `k`.
- **The bounds are stated by value, also for a column that the index holds in descending order.** The lower bound is the smallest value that the scan lets through, and the upper bound the largest. The storage turns them into the ends of the range of the index.
- **Bounds that hold no value are passed on as they are.** `k > 5 and k < 3` is a scan by the primary key between the two bounds, which gives no row.

What follows from the rules:

- **The access only leaves rows out.** The filter above the scan tests every condition on every row that the access gives. So the rows of a statement do not depend on the choice.
- **The order of the rows of a query with ORDER BY does not depend on the choice either, but for the rows that tie.** An access either delivers the order or has its rows sorted. See [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows).
- **More pins win over the order, also with a LIMIT.** With an index on `(a)` and one on `(b)`, `where b = 'x' order by a limit 1` reads by the index on `(b)` and sorts.
- **With nothing pinned and nothing bounded, an index that delivers the order wins over the scan of every row with a sort,** with a LIMIT or without. So an index wins over the primary key by pinning more, by a bound, or by delivering the order.
- **A LIMIT without ORDER BY changes nothing in the choice.** `TestChooseAccess_orderBy` requires for each of its cases that the access without the ORDER BY is the one of slice 9, with a LIMIT and without.
- **The rules cannot tell a query that finds its row at once from one that finds none.** They have no statistics. With goqite's index and no message ready, goqite's receive reads every message of its queue, where an index on `(queue, timeout)` would read none; see [The rows read from the cache](#the-rows-read-from-the-cache). [The todo on performance](../../docs/todo/performance.md) has this, beside the ways to make a query with `order by` cheaper that are not built.
- **The errors of a statement and their order do not depend on the choice.** `build.ordered`, and `build.bind` for an update and a delete, check the names, the lengths, and the types first, and give the scan its access last.
- **An update and a delete find their rows by the same choice.**
- **A point lookup reads no index of its table.** Rules 1 and 2 do not depend on the indexes. So `build.access` calls the rules first without the indexes, and reads the indexes of the table (`reader.indexes`) only when the answer is neither "no row" nor the row of a primary key.
- **The rules know the WHERE of slices 7 and 12, and the ORDER BY and the LIMIT of [slice 13](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-13-order-by-and-limit-on-select), and nothing else.**
- **The cache sorts every row of an access or none.** Where the key of the access delivers the first columns of the ORDER BY and not the rest, the access is sorted like any other. A sort within the groups of the first columns is not built; it is in [the todo on performance](../../docs/todo/performance.md).
- **A scan whose key has more than 63 columns that it does not pin is sorted by SQLite also where the rules say that the order is delivered,** as slice 9 found for a scan without ORDER BY. The rows are in the right order, and the rows read and the plan of the cache are those of a sort.
- **`chooseAccess` fails only with an internal error,** for two bounds of values of two types, which the check of the types refuses before.

Examples, on `create table t (k int primary key, a int not null, b text, c real) strict, without rowid` with `create index ia on t (a)` and then `create index iab on t (a, b)`. `TestEngine_indexes` pins each, also after a replay of the log into an empty cache:

| WHERE | Access |
| --- | --- |
| `a = 10` | A scan of `ia` with `a` pinned. Both indexes pin one column, and `ia` was created first. |
| `a = 10 and b = 'x'` | A scan of `iab` with `a` and `b` pinned. |
| `a = 10 and b > 'x'` | A scan of `iab` with `a` pinned, from a lower bound of `b`. |
| `a = 10 and k < 2` | A scan of `ia` with `a` pinned, to an upper bound of `k`. `ia` bounds the next column of its key, `k`, and `iab` does not bound its next column, `b`. |
| `b = 'x'` | A scan of every row. No key starts with `b`. |
| `k = 2 and a = 10` | A `get` of the row with the key 2. |

Examples with ORDER BY, on `create table r (k int primary key, a int not null, b text, c real, d blob) strict, without rowid` with the indexes `rk` on `(k desc)`, `ra` on `(a)`, `rb` on `(b)`, `rbc` on `(b, c desc)`, and `rd` on `(d)`, created in that order. The golden file `testdata/plans/rules.txt` pins each:

| Query | Access | Order |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `order by k` | Every row. | Delivered, forward. The primary key wins over `rk`, which delivers the order as well. |
| `order by k desc limit 3` | Every row. | Delivered, backward. |
| `order by a` | A scan of `ra`, which pins nothing and bounds nothing. | Delivered, forward. An index that delivers the order wins over every row and a sort. |
| `order by c` | Every row. | Sorted by `c`. No key starts with `c`. |
| `order by c desc, k limit 5` | Every row. | Sorted by `c desc, k`. A LIMIT does not save the sort. |
| `order by b desc` | A scan of `rb`. | Delivered, backward. `rb` and `rbc` both deliver it, and `rb` was created first. |
| `order by b desc, c limit 2` | A scan of `rbc`, whose key is `(b, c desc, k)`. | Delivered, backward: both columns are in the other direction than the key holds them. |
| `order by b, c` | Every row. | Sorted by `b, c`. Against `rbc`, one column is in the direction of the key and one in the other. |
| `where b = 'x' order by c desc` | A scan of `rbc` with `b` pinned. `rb` pins as many columns, and `rbc` delivers the order. | Delivered, forward. |
| `where b is null order by b desc, c, k desc` | A scan of `rbc` with `b` pinned to NULL. | Delivered, backward. The pinned `b` orders nothing, and `c, k desc` is the reverse of the rest of the key. |
| `where k > 3 order by a` | A scan of the primary key from a lower bound. | Sorted by `a`. Without a LIMIT, the bound wins over the order of `ra`. |
| `where k > 3 order by a limit 1` | A scan of `ra`. | Delivered, forward. With a LIMIT, the order wins over the bound. |
| `where a >= ? order by a desc limit ?` | A scan of `ra` from a lower bound of `a`. | Delivered, backward. One index gives the bound and the order. |
| `where b = 'x' order by a limit 1` | A scan of `rb` with `b` pinned. | Sorted by `a`. More pins win over the order of `ra`, also with a LIMIT. |
| `order by a, k, b` | Every row. | Sorted by `a, k, b`. The key of `ra` is `(a, k)`, and it does not have `b` next. |
| `where k = 3 order by c limit 1` | A `get` of the row with the key 3. | Delivered. |
| `where c > null order by d` | No row. | Delivered. |
| `where b = 'x' and c > 1.5 limit 1` | A scan of `rbc` with `b` pinned, from a lower bound of `c`. | None. A LIMIT without ORDER BY changes nothing. |

goqite's receive is `select id from goqite where queue = ? and ? >= timeout and received < ? order by priority desc, created limit 1`. Its access in the three schemas of the golden files, with its LIMIT and without:

| Indexes | The receive | The receive without its LIMIT |
| --- | --- | --- |
| None | Every row, sorted by `priority desc, created`. | The same. |
| goqite's index, on `(queue, priority desc, created)` | That index with `queue` pinned. The order is delivered, forward. | The same. |
| goqite's index, and one on `(queue, timeout)` | goqite's index with `queue` pinned. Both indexes pin one column. The order is delivered, forward, and with a LIMIT that wins over the bound of the other index. | The index on `(queue, timeout)` with `queue` pinned, to an upper bound of `timeout`, sorted by `priority desc, created`. Without a LIMIT, the bound wins. |

goqite's index delivers the order because `queue` is pinned: the key of the index is `(queue, priority desc, created, id)`, and `priority desc, created` is the start of what is left of it, in the directions of the key. In the third schema, SQLite's own planner reads the receive by the index on `(queue, timeout)` and sorts. The first of the roadmap's examples of slice 13, `select v1, v2 from t1 order by v2 desc, v1 limit 10` on a table with an index on `(v2, v3 desc)`, reads every row and sorts: the key of the index is `(v2, v3 desc, v1)`, which does not have `v1` next. The second, `select v1 from t1 where v2 > ? order by v1 limit ?`, reads every row in the order of the primary key `v1`, delivered: with a LIMIT, the order wins over the bound of `v2` by the index.

The golden files in `testdata/plans` hold these examples, the other rules case by case, and the queries of goqite and glue. See [The golden plans](#the-golden-plans).

### The golden plans

`TestAccessOf_golden` in `access_test.go` keeps the access of a fixed set of named queries as golden files, one file for each schema in `testdata/plans/<schema>.txt`. A schema is a table and its indexes, in the order in which they are created. A file holds the statements of the schema, and then a block for each query: its name after `## `, its text, its arguments, and its access. The access is the one that `AccessOf` gives: no row, every row, a `get` with the value of each column of the primary key, or a scan of the primary key or of an index, with the columns of its key, the values that it pins, and its bounds, each open (`<`) or closed (`<=`). The direction that the engine draws is not in it.

The block of a select with ORDER BY has one line more, after `order: `. It is `delivered, forward` or `delivered, backward` for an access that delivers the order, with the direction in which the scan must run to deliver it, and `sorted by` and the columns of the ORDER BY, each with ` desc` if it is descending, for an access whose rows are sorted. No row and a `get` are `delivered, forward`. `AccessOf` answers for a select with ORDER BY and no WHERE too, so such a query has a block. The block of a statement without ORDER BY has no such line.

The schemas are goqite's table with no index, with goqite's index, and with an added index on `(queue, timeout)`, each with goqite's receive and other queries of goqite's shape; glue's table of sessions with its index on `expiry`, with the sessions that expire first and last, of slice 13; the example of the roadmap, and its two examples of slice 13; the examples above; and a table with a case for each rule, four of them with a value on the left, and the cases of slice 13 in the table above.

The receive is in the files five ways. "receive" has `timeout <= ?` and no ORDER BY and no LIMIT, and "receive as goqite writes it" has goqite's `? >= timeout`, of slice 12. "receive in order" and "receive in order as goqite writes it" are the same two with goqite's `order by priority desc, created limit 1`, and "receive in order without a limit" is the last without its LIMIT. `TestAccessOf_golden` requires that the first two have the same access in each of the three schemas, and the two in order too. In each schema that has goqite's index, it requires that the receive in order as goqite writes it reads by goqite's index with `queue` pinned, with the order delivered, forward.

A change of the rules changes these files. The test then fails, and names each query whose access changed, with the old lines after `- ` and the new after `+ `.
