It also fails for a schema whose file is missing and for a file of no schema. After a change of the rules that is meant, rewrite the files with:

```sh
go test ./internal/engine -run TestAccessOf_golden -update
```

and commit them with the change. In review, a diff of `testdata/plans` is a change of the choice of an access, and each changed block shows which query reads its rows in another way.

### The order of the rows

The spec says: "Unspecified row order varies." With indexes, the order of the rows of a select without `order by` depends on the access. The engine varies it further, so that no application comes to depend on it. A select with `order by` gives its rows in that order, and only the order of its rows that tie, which are equal in every column of the `order by`, is unspecified. The engine varies that order where it sorts.

- **For each query, the engine draws whether its scan runs forward or backward** (`Engine.reading`). Forward is the order of the key of the access, and backward is its reverse. The draw holds for every access: every row, the primary key, and each index. A select without WHERE is drawn too. A LIMIT without ORDER BY gives the first rows in the direction that the query drew.
- **The rows of a select with ORDER BY come in the order of its columns:** by the first, and among rows that are equal in it by the next. An ascending column has NULL before every value, and a descending one after every value, as in SQLite. The cache orders the values, in a sort and in a key alike, so the order within a type is SQLite's: integers and reals by their number, with 0.0 equal to -0.0, text by its bytes, and blobs by their bytes and then their length. A column holds one type and NULL, so values of two types never meet.
- **Where the storage sorts, the rows that tie come in the order of the primary key, or in its reverse, by the draw of the query.** It is the same draw that turns the scan of a query without ORDER BY. The storage adds the primary key to the sort as its last terms, so the order of a sort is total.
- **Where the access delivers the order, the rows that tie come as its key holds them, and the draw decides nothing.** The spec has this since 2026-11-21: "Where an index delivers the order, tied rows come in the order of that index." To vary them, the engine would have to read a whole group of rows that tie before it gives the first, which defeats the index. That order is no promise either: it depends on the index that the rules pick, and the rules may change. The scan runs in the direction that delivers the order. With an index on `(a)` and the primary key `k`, the rows of one `a` come in the order of `k` for `order by a`, and in its reverse for `order by a desc`. An ORDER BY of pinned columns alone is read forward.
- **So the rows that tie vary from one query to the next only where the rows are sorted.** SQLite gives rows that tie in the order in which it reads them. `TestEngine_orderBy` runs a query with a sort 40 times on an engine with a seed and requires both orders of its rows that tie, each at least 5 times, and requires one order of 40 runs of a query whose order an index delivers.
- **The draw is the low bit of `host.Host.Uint64`, one draw for each query.** An odd number is backward. The engine draws when it builds the query, also for a query that then reads no row or one row, and for one whose access delivers the order of its ORDER BY. The draw is made whether it is used or not, so the n-th query of two engines with one seed has the same draw.
- **An engine with a seed of its own draws from a splitmix64 sequence of that seed, and nothing from the host.** The seed is `Options.Directions`, an option of the engine and nothing of the public package: a run that must be had again from a seed sets it, which are the tests of the engine (also through `Options.Seed` and `Engine.SetSeed` in `export_test.go`), its simulation, and the simulator of the site. The driver sets none, so in production each direction is a draw of the host. The n-th query of two engines with one seed reads in the same direction.
- **An update and a delete without RETURNING read forward, and draw nothing,** as SQLite reads them. The order in which they find their rows shows only in the error of an update whose rows fail with different errors, which `changeRows` changes in SQLite's order; see [The operators](#the-operators).
- **For each insert, update, and delete with RETURNING, the engine draws one direction** (`Engine.order`). Forward gives the rows in the order in which the statement changed them, which is the order of the primary key of the rows that it found, whatever its access; backward gives them in the reverse. The scan of the statement reads forward all the same. The draw is a draw as a query's, from the host or from the seed of the engine, so the statement counts as a query in the sequence of the draws. The engine draws once the statement has its write transaction, before it builds the sink, so also for a statement that then fails, and for an insert, whose one row no draw can turn. A statement in a read-only transaction draws nothing. Under a path of the tests that has a direction, the path decides the order, and the engine draws nothing. SQLite gave the rows of an update and a delete in the order of the key in every case measured, also through an index; s4ql promises no order.
- **The rules set a direction only for an access that delivers the order of an ORDER BY:** the direction that delivers it. `build.reach` puts the direction of the statement on every other access that the rules give: the direction of the scan for a statement without ORDER BY, and the direction of the rows that tie for an access whose rows are sorted.
- **The engines of the tests have seeds, so that a failure can be run again in the same directions.** An instance of the tests draws a seed, logs it when its test fails, and takes it from the flag `-directions`. In the generated tests `TestDifferential` and `TestConn_differential`, the instance of a sequence derives its seed from the seed of the sequence, so that what the tests count of the directions is a function of their seeds. The simulation derives the seed of each engine from the seed of the run. All three are under [How it is tested](#how-it-is-tested).

### A forced path, for the tests

The comparison of rows cannot tell one access from another. So the tests can force the access of every statement of an engine, and read the access that a statement got.

A `path` in `access.go` forces an access. The tests set it as a `Path` of `export_test.go`, through `Options.Path` and `Engine.SetPath`. The zero value forces nothing.

| Field of `Path` | What it forces |
| --- | --- |
| `Full` | Every row of the table, in the order of the primary key. No condition is tested for being never true. |
| `Primary` | A scan by the primary key, as `fit` fits the conditions to it, also where the rules read one row by a `get`. |
| `Index` | A scan by the index of that name, as `fit` fits the conditions to its key. |
| `Limited`, `Pins` | At most `Pins` pinned columns, for a path of the primary key or of an index. An access that would pin more pins that many and bounds nothing. |
| `Forward`, `Backward` | The direction of the scan, for a query, an update, and a delete. The engine then draws nothing. For a query whose rows are sorted, it is the direction of the rows that tie. A query whose access delivers the order of its ORDER BY reads in the direction that delivers it, whatever these say. |
| `Sort` | A sort of the rows of every query with ORDER BY, also where the key of its access delivers the order. With none of `Full`, `Primary`, and `Index`, the access is the one that the rules choose for the statement without its ORDER BY. |

- **`forcedAccess` says what a path of the primary key or of an index reads.** It reads no row for conditions of which one is never true, as the rules do, because the storage takes no scan for such conditions.
- **A path of an index that the table of a statement does not have reads as the rules say.**
- **A statement without WHERE and without ORDER BY has no access to force.** It reads every row, in the direction of the path, if the path has one.
- **Without `Sort`, a forced access delivers the order where its key does, as `delivers` says, and is sorted otherwise** (`path.place`). That holds for `Full` and `Primary`, by the primary key, and for `Index`, by the key of the index. The rule reads the conditions and not the pins of the scan, so an access that `Limited` lets pin less delivers the same orders. A path `Primary` in place of a `get` is sorted where the rules deliver: its key has no column left for the ORDER BY, and the sort is of one row at most.
- **No row has nothing to sort, and delivers every order, also under `Sort`.** So does the row of one primary key that the rules read by a `get`. So does a scan by the primary key that pins every column of it, as the path `Primary` reads the row of a `get`: it reads one row at most, and the cache's SQLite does not sort it (`orderBy.sort`).
- **`Options.ScanAll`, `Engine.SetScanAll`, and `Engine.ScansAll` are the path `Full`.**
- **Four functions show the tests the choice,** each as an `Access`: which index, the pinned values, the bounds, and the direction. `ChooseAccess` is `chooseAccess`. `AccessOf` gives the access of the text of a select, an update, or a delete with WHERE, or of a select with ORDER BY, on a table and its indexes, and reads no rows. `PathAccessOf` does the same under a path. `Rows.Access` gives the access of the rows of a query, with the direction that the engine drew.
- **An `Access` says how the rows come in order.** `Delivered` is true for an access that delivers the order of an ORDER BY, and `Backward` is then the direction that delivers it, also from `AccessOf`. `Sorted` is true for an access whose rows the storage sorts, and `By` then holds the columns of the sort. A statement without ORDER BY has neither. The text of an `Access` ends in `, in order` or `, sorted`.

### The rows read from the cache

`TestEngine_rowsRead` pins how many rows named queries read from the cache. The number is `statement.rows_read` of the event of the query: the rows that the cache gave the scan or the `get` of the statement, as `core.ReadTx.RowsRead` counts them, whether or not the conditions hold for them. It depends only on the rows of the table and on the access. It does not depend on the direction, on the machine, or on timing: the test runs each query as the rules choose, then forward, and then backward, and requires the same number each time. For a query with ORDER BY, the test also requires the same `statement.order` each time. These counts are the first of the [performance measurements](../../docs/todo/performance.md) of the project.

A table `t (k int primary key, a int not null, b int not null, c text)` holds 4,000 rows: 40 for each value of `a`, with `b` from 0 to 6. The rows that each query reads:

| Query | No index | Index on `(a)` | Indexes on `(a)` and `(a, b)` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `where k = ?`, a point lookup, which gives 1 row | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| `where a = ?`, which gives 40 rows | 4,000 | 40 | 40 |
| `where a = ? and b >= ?`, which gives 22 rows | 4,000 | 40 | 22 |
| `where k >= ? and k < ?`, which gives 100 rows | 100 | 100 | 100 |

A table shaped like goqite's holds 3,000 messages in 3 queues of 1,000. The query is goqite's receive without its `order by` and its `limit`, as goqite writes it, `select id from goqite where queue = ? and ? >= timeout and received < ?`, and with `timeout <= ?`. Both give 400 rows, by the same access and with the same rows read:

| Indexes | Access | Rows read |
| --- | --- | --- |
| None | Every row. | 3,000 |
| goqite's index, on `(queue, priority desc, created)` | That index with `queue` pinned: the rows of the queue. | 1,000 |
| goqite's index, and one on `(queue, timeout)` | The index on `(queue, timeout)`. It pins as many columns, and it bounds the next one. | 500 |

Since slice 13 the test also runs goqite's receive as goqite writes it, with its ORDER BY and its LIMIT: `select id from goqite where queue = ? and ? >= timeout and received < ? order by priority desc, created limit 1`, and the same with `timeout <= ?`. The table again holds 3,000 messages in 3 queues of 1,000, with 4 priorities, none of them received. The test has two cases: the timeout of every message is in the past, so that every message is ready, or it is in the future, so that none is. The receive gives 1 row in the first case, the oldest message of the highest priority of its queue, and none in the second. The last row of the table is the receive without its LIMIT, which gives 1,000 rows and none:

| Indexes | Query | Access and order | Rows read, a message ready | Rows read, none ready |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| None | The receive | Every row, sorted. | 3,000 | 3,000 |
| goqite's index | The receive | That index with `queue` pinned, delivered. | 1 | 1,000 |
| goqite's index, and one on `(queue, timeout)` | The receive | goqite's index with `queue` pinned, delivered. | 1 | 1,000 |
| goqite's index, and one on `(queue, timeout)` | The receive without its LIMIT | The index on `(queue, timeout)`, sorted. | 1,000 | 0 |

- **With goqite's index and a message ready, the receive reads one row.** The index holds the messages of a queue in the order of the ORDER BY, the first of them passes the filter, and the LIMIT ends the scan there. The index on `(queue, timeout)` beside it changes nothing.
- **With goqite's index and no message ready, the receive reads the 1,000 messages of its queue.** The filter drops each, so the scan goes on to the end of the queue. The index on `(queue, timeout)` would read none of them, as the last row of the table shows, but the rules have no statistics and cannot tell the two cases apart. It is in [the todo on performance](../../docs/todo/performance.md).
- **Without an index, the receive reads all 3,000 rows, whatever it gives.**

What a query with ORDER BY or LIMIT reads follows from its access and its order. `TestEngine_orderBy` pins each case on a table of 12 rows, with the access and the order of its event:

- **A delivered order reads the rows up to the last one that the query gives.** With `limit 1`, that is up to the first row that passes the filter. Without a LIMIT, it is every row of the access. Where the filter drops rows, the scan reads more than the query gives: `where a = 1 and b is not null order by c desc limit 2` reads 3 rows by an index on `(a, c desc)` and gives 2.
- **A sort reads every row within the pins and the bound of its access, whatever the LIMIT.** `order by d desc limit 2` without an index on `d` reads 12 rows and gives 2. The storage has read them all when it gives the first. SQLite sorts whole rows, and also the rows that the filter then drops, because the filter is the engine's.
- **`limit 0` reads nothing,** with ORDER BY or without, by a scan, by a sort, or by a `get`. It gives the errors of the names and of the arguments all the same.
- **A LIMIT without ORDER BY reads up to the last row that it gives, in the direction that the query drew.** `limit 5` reads 5 rows. Where a filter drops rows before the LIMIT, the number depends on the direction: `where c > 2.0 limit 2` reads 5 rows forward. The test pins such a query forward only.
- **A LIMIT above the number of rows reads every row of the access.**
- **When a query stops before the end of a sort, the core counts the rows of the scan with a second statement,** a count of a column of the primary key by the same conditions on the same index (`core.conn.unread`), which SQLite stops, as it stops the sort, when the context ends. SQLite does not say how many rows its sorter holds. See [the README of the core](../core/README.md#the-interface).

## IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS

Slice 10 adds a clause to four statements: `create table if not exists`, `drop table if exists`, `create index if not exists`, and `drop index if exists`. Where the plain statement would do its work, the statement with the clause does the same. Where SQLite does nothing for it, it does nothing and succeeds. `TestEngine_exists` pins where that is, and which errors still come, against SQLite 3.54.1.

- **`buildSink` gives a statement that does nothing the sink `nothing`,** which writes nothing. `buildCreateIndex` and `buildDropIndex` give it for the statements of an index. The plans carry the clause; see [The stages](#the-stages).
- **A create table does nothing where a table has its name,** in any case of the ASCII letters, whatever its columns are. SQLite stops at the table. So the statement does nothing before the errors of `CreateTable.ColumnsError()`, and before `string or blob too big` of the length limit (`schemaTooBig`). An index of the name still gives `there is already an index named %s`. Without a table of the name, every error of the plain statement comes.
- **A drop table does nothing where no table has its name,** also where an index has it.
- **A create index does nothing where an index has its name,** on any table, also where the table of the statement lacks a column of the statement. Two errors come first, as in SQLite: `no such table: main.%s`, and then `there is already a table named %s`. The statement does nothing before `no such column`, before `string or blob too big` of the length limit (`indexTooBig`), and before the refusal of more than 250 columns (`core.MaxIndexColumns`). SQLite takes an index of more than 250 columns. So a create index of 251 columns does nothing where an index has its name, as in SQLite, and is "not supported" where none has.
- **A drop index does nothing where no index has its name,** also where a table has it.
- **The errors that the statement pass gives from the text alone stay, also where the object exists:** those of a reserved name, of the bare name IF after IF NOT EXISTS, and of a declaration that s4ql does not support. SQLite does nothing for some of these texts where the object exists, such as a create table with a column type that s4ql does not support. This is the known difference of slice 10 in [the todo](../../docs/todo/slice-10.md).
- **A statement that does nothing is neither annotated nor committed,** so the log does not change. See [One statement, one transaction](#one-statement-one-transaction) for a statement of its own, [A statement in a transaction](#a-statement-in-a-transaction) and [A read-only transaction](#a-read-only-transaction) for one in a transaction, and [Annotations](#annotations).
- **The four statements leave the count of changes, and their event says whether they did nothing.** See [Changes and the last insert rowid](#changes-and-the-last-insert-rowid) and `statement.nothing` under [Events](#events).

## DEFAULT with a literal

Slice 11 adds `default` with a literal to a column of a create table. An insert with a list of columns stores the default in each column that the list leaves out. `TestEngine_defaults` pins what the engine does with a default against SQLite.

- **The plan carries the default, and the sink of a create table hands it to the core,** as `Column.default` of the commit format; see [The stages](#the-stages).
- **The sink of an insert fills the defaults in, and the core fills nothing in.** `buildValues` takes the columns of the table that the transaction gives, which is the core's schema as the cache or the log holds it. It gives each column that the list leaves out its default, or NULL where the column has none, and the core gets whole rows. So an insert after a replay of the log, a restart, or a roll back uses the default of the log.
- **A NULL that the statement gives stays NULL,** as a literal or as a nil argument, whatever the default of its column.
- **DEFAULT NULL is NULL.** In a column that does not take NULL, and in the key, an insert that leaves the column out gives `NOT NULL constraint failed`, as for a column without a default.
- **The key can have a default.** An insert that leaves the key out stores the default as its key, and the second such insert gives `UNIQUE constraint failed`.
- **The core keeps a default of -0.0 as 0.0,** as SQLite stores it. The engine hands the default as the statement writes it, and the plan prints -0.0. `memory` in `memory_internal_test.go` keeps 0.0 as the core does.
- **`checkLength` checks only the values that the statement gives.** SQLite checks the length of a value when it binds it. A default is only in the record of the row, which the core measures, so a default that makes a row too long gives `string or blob too big` for the record. The order of the errors is under [Types](#types).
- **An insert without a list, an update, a delete, and a select do not meet the defaults.** An insert without a list gives every column a value.
- **A create table with IF NOT EXISTS does nothing where a table has its name, also where that table has other defaults,** and stays out of the log.
- **A table whose statement in the cache is longer than the length limit is "not supported",** where SQLite takes it under a lowered limit. The canonical form of a default can make the statement of the cache longer than the text; see [Errors](#errors).

## A value on the left of a comparison

Slice 12 lets a comparison of a WHERE have its literal or its `?` on the left and its column on the right, as in `5 < a` and goqite's `? >= timeout`, in a select, an update, and a delete. `TestEngine_valueFirst` pins what the engine does with such a comparison against SQLite.

- **The statement pass turns the comparison round, and the engine reads every condition one way.** `5 < a` reaches the engine as the condition `a > 5`, with `ValueFirst` true; see [the README of `stmt`](../sql/stmt/README.md#the-typed-form). So the fitting of the conditions to the key and to indexes, the filter, and the rule of exact types run it as they run `a > 5`. SQLite gives the two orders the same rows, for every operator and every type.
- **The plan carries `ValueFirst`, and `Format` prints the condition with its column first;** see [The stages](#the-stages). Nothing in the engine reads `ValueFirst` but the planted bug `unturnedBound`.
- **NULL on the left gives no row,** as a literal and as a nil argument, as `a = null` does.
- **No message needs the order of the text.** `no such column` has the offset of the column, which the pass sets from the text, whichever side the column is on. The refusal by type names the value and the column, as in `a string literal compared with the column t.a of type INT`, which reads true for either order. The number of an argument in a message is its number in the order of the text, as the pass numbers the parameters: `a = ? and ? < b and c > ?` has the parameters 1, 2, and 3.
- **The errors come in the order of the other side.** The names come first, in the order of the text, also after a value on the left that is too long. Then comes `string or blob too big`, and then the refusal of the first value of another type than its column, in the order of the text, whichever side the value is on. The refusals of the arguments, for their number and for an argument that no column holds, are made before the database.
- **The access is the same for both orders.** `TestChooseAccess` pins it case by case, the golden plans hold goqite's receive both ways, and `TestEngine_rowsRead` reads both with the same counts. See [The golden plans](#the-golden-plans) and [The rows read from the cache](#the-rows-read-from-the-cache).
- **A literal that is too long in the WHERE of an update or a delete is the known difference of slice 8, on either side.** SQLite gives `string or blob too big` there only when it steps the statement, and resets the count of changes. s4ql gives the error before, and keeps the count. See [the todo](../../docs/todo/slice-08.md).

## RETURNING

Slice 14 adds `returning` to an insert, an update, and a delete, with columns of the table or `*`. The statement gives one row for each row that it inserts, updates, or deletes. `Conn.Query` gives the rows, and `Conn.Exec` drops them. `TestEngine_returning` and `TestConn_returning` pin what the engine does with the clause against SQLite. The code is in `returning.go`.

- **The plan carries the clause.** An `*Insert`, an `*Update`, and a `*Delete` have `Returning`, which is nil for a statement without the clause, and otherwise has `Star` or `Columns`, the names in the order of the text. `Format` prints it on the line of the statement, as in `update t set v = 1 returning *`.
- **The sink holds the rows, in Go memory** (`returning`). It adds each row once it has written it (`returning.add`): an insert the row that it inserted, an update the row after the change, and a delete the row as it was, which it reads with a `get` just before it deletes it. `Conn.write` gives the rows, as the source `returned`, once the commit has returned nil, and `Conn.writeIn` once the savepoint of the statement is released. A statement that fails gives none.
- **`Conn.Exec` holds no rows** (`dropReturns`). The sink is built with its RETURNING, so the columns are looked up and fail as through `Query`, and the count of changes is the same. Then the sink drops the clause before it runs: it adds no row, and a delete reads no row with a `get`.
- **The rows cannot be read from the cache in their place.** Outside a transaction they must outlive the write transaction, which the commit ends before the first row is given, and the rows of a delete are gone from the table.
- **So the memory of a statement with RETURNING grows with the rows that it changes, without a bound.** `changeRows` keeps the keys of any number of rows in the storage, and `returning` keeps the rows themselves in memory. A spool in the cache would bound it, and is not decided; see the open item of slice 14 in [the todo of the storage core](../../docs/todo/storage-core.md).
- **The values are those that a later select reads.** A real that is -0.0 is given as 0.0, which is what the table stores for it. Every other value is given as the sink wrote it: the default of a column that an insert leaves out, NULL, and the values of SET, the key among them. An update gives every row that it finds, also one whose values do not change, and a delete every row that it deletes. So the rows are as many as the count of changes.
- **The columns are those of a select of the same columns:** each with its name as the table stores it and its declared type. A column that the clause names twice is given twice, and `*` gives every column in the order of the table. A statement that changes no row gives no rows, with its columns.
- **A column of RETURNING is looked up after every other name of the statement** (`buildReturning`), as SQLite does it: for an insert after the number of its values, and for an update and a delete after the columns of WHERE. The lengths and the types of the values come after it. The first column, in the order of the text, that the table does not have gives SQLite's `no such column` of a select list (`noSuchResultColumn`). A failed constraint gives its error and no row. In a read-only transaction the statement gives `attempt to write a readonly database` after the errors of its names, those of RETURNING among them.
- **A context that ends while the caller reads the rows does not end them** (`returned.rows`). The change is made by then, outside a transaction committed too, and the rows hold nothing, so an error of the rows would say something false about the change. This holds in a transaction too, where the rows hold the connection, but nothing that the context bounds. In the driver, `database/sql` can still close the rows when the context ends, which the package doc says.
- **The commit format does not change.** The annotation of the statement is its text, the clause in it, and its arguments, as for any statement.
- **The rest is where it belongs:** the commit before the first row under [One statement, one transaction](#one-statement-one-transaction), the open rows of a transaction under [Rows in a transaction](#rows-in-a-transaction), the draw of the direction under [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows), and the event under [Events](#events).

## Scalar subqueries

Slice 15 lets the value of a comparison of the WHERE of a select, an update, or a delete be a scalar subquery, on either side of its column, as in goqite's receive, `id = (select id from goqite where ... order by priority desc, created limit 1)`. Its value is the value of its one column in its first row, or NULL without a row. `TestEngine_subquery` pins what the engine does with it against SQLite. The code is in `subquery.go`, and [the todo of slice 15](../../docs/todo/slice-15.md) has each decision and SQLite's facts behind it.

- **The plan carries the subqueries.** A `*Query`, an `*Update`, and a `*Delete` have `Subqueries`, one `*Query` for each subquery in the order of the text, of the shape of a select, whose own `Subqueries` is nil. The value of the condition is a `*Subquery`, with its `Number`, from 1, and the `Offset` of its parenthesis. `Format` prints the value as `(subquery 1)`, and the query of each subquery under a line `subquery 1` after the nodes of the statement.
- **The engine computes each subquery once, after every check, before the statement reads a row** (`build.compute`). It reads in the transaction of the statement, before the statement chooses its access and reads or changes a row of its own. So an update or a delete of the table that its subquery reads sees the rows as they were before the statement. A statement that can give or change no row whatever its subqueries give computes none, and each value is NULL: a select with LIMIT 0, one with another condition that is never true, and an update or a delete in a read-only transaction, which gives `attempt to write a readonly database` and reads nothing.
- **A subquery reads as a query with a LIMIT, up to its first row.** `chooseAccess` gives it its access for its conditions and its ORDER BY, and the scan stops at the first row that passes the filter, so it reads no more than the select of its text with `limit 1`. LIMIT 0 reads nothing and gives NULL. A value that is NULL makes its comparison never true, so the access of the statement is `none`.
- **Where the ORDER BY does not decide the first row, the engine draws it.** A subquery reads forward or backward as the engine draws for a query (`reading.inner`), one draw for each subquery that reads, after the draw of the statement, so a caller that relies on SQLite's first row is seen. A statement without a subquery draws as before, and a path that a test forces holds for the subqueries too. See [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows).
- **The errors come where SQLite gives them** (`build.walk`, `subqueries`). In a select, SQLite finds the table of the statement and then those of its subqueries before any name, and gives `row value misused` for `*` of more than one column at its comparison: with the offset of the column when the column is on the left, and with none (-1) when the subquery is on the left. In an update and a delete, a subquery finds its table where the walk over the WHERE reaches it, and `*` of more than one column gives `sub-select returns N columns - expected 1` after the names of the WHERE, or `row value misused` where the subquery is on the left of `!=` or `<>`, or of an arithmetic expression. The names of a subquery come where it is in the text, and so do the lengths and the types of its values.
- **A correlated subquery is "not supported"** (`subqueries.uncorrelated`): one with a name that its table does not have and the table of the statement has. The walk takes the name as SQLite does, and the refusal comes after every error that SQLite gives for the names of the statement, so s4ql gives SQLite's error wherever SQLite has one.
- **The rule of exact types holds for the two columns** (`subqueries.types`). The column of a subquery is of a STRICT table, so the declared types decide, whatever the rows: INT and INTEGER are one type, and a subquery whose column is of another type than the column that it is compared with is "not supported", also where it finds no row.
- **The LIMIT of a subquery takes an integer from 0,** by the rule of slice 13, where SQLite reads it as X <> 0.
- **The commit format does not change.** The annotation of the statement is its text, the subquery in it, and its arguments.
- **The event** has the number of subqueries, and what they read once the access is chosen; see [Events](#events).

## Arithmetic

The piece of work arithmetic lets the value of an assignment of SET, and either side of a comparison of the WHERE of a select, an update, a delete, or a scalar subquery, be an arithmetic expression: `+`, `-`, `*`, and `/` on integers and reals, and a `-` before an operand, as in goqite's receive, `set received = received + 1`. The engine computes it with the evaluator of [`internal/expr`](../expr/README.md). `TestEngine_arithmetic` pins what the engine does with it against SQLite. The code is in `expression.go`, `operator.go`, and `subquery.go`, and [the todo of arithmetic](../../docs/todo/arithmetic.md#the-engine-and-the-driver) has each decision and each difference from SQLite.

- **The plan carries the expressions.** An `*Arithmetic` is the value of an assignment or of a condition, and holds the `stmt.Expression` of the pass. `Condition.Expression` is the side that a condition tests in place of a column, as in `a + 1 = 5`; its `Column` is then the zero `Name`. `Format` prints each binary operator in parentheses, a negation with `-`, and a parameter as `?N`, so that the text shows the tree: `update t set v = v + 1, w = -(? * 2.5)` prints as `update t set v = (v + 1), w = -(?1 * 2.5)`, and `where a * 2 < b * 3` as `filter (a * 2) < (b * 3)`.
- **The engine handles an expression in three steps, each where SQLite gives the errors of its kind,** as the comment at the top of `expression.go` says. First it looks the names up where the walk over the statement reaches them, in the order of the text, as SQLite resolves them (`resolver`). Then, once SQLite would have given every error of the names and of the lengths, it checks each expression against the table, binds it to the arguments, and checks its type against the other side (`bindExpression`, `comparable`). Last it computes each expression without a column once for the run, and each expression with a column for each row.
- **Text in an expression, `||`, `lower`, and `hex`, runs by the same three steps;** see [Text](#text).

The examples below are on a table `t`, of `create table t (k int primary key, a int, b int, r real, s text, n int not null) strict, without rowid`, with an index `t_a` on `a`, and the rows (k, a, b) (1, 10, 0), (2, 20, 2), (3, 30, 3), and (4, NULL, 4). So `b` is 0 in the row of `k = 1`.

### When an evaluation error comes

An evaluation error is an integer overflow, a division by zero, or a real result that is not finite, which [the evaluator](../expr/README.md#semantics) gives where SQLite gives a quirk. Whether a statement gives one depends on which values the engine computes, by this rule:

- **An expression without a column is a constant of one run of the statement.** The engine computes it once, after the checks that come before the access (the names, the lengths, the refusals of the expressions of SET, and the refusals of types of WHERE), and before it chooses the access. The refusal of the type of a value of SET and its NOT NULL come after, in `update.check`, so a constant fails before them. It computes the constants in the order of the text: those of SET first, then those of WHERE, then those of each scalar subquery (`build.constants`, `build.compute`). A constant serves a lookup by the key and a bound of an index as an argument does. A constant that is NULL makes its comparison never true, so the access is `none`. The error of a constant comes whatever the rows are: on an empty table, with LIMIT 0, and beside a condition that is never true, such as `k is null` on the key.
- **Two places compute no constant.** In a read-only transaction, an update or a delete computes none, and gives SQLite's `attempt to write a readonly database` after the names, the lengths, and the refusals of the types of WHERE, as for a plain value. It checks no expression of SET there either, as it checks no plain value of SET: `update t set a = s + 1` gives the read-only error, and not the refusal of the TEXT column. The constants of a scalar subquery are computed only when the subquery is computed, which it is not for a statement that can give no row whatever its subqueries give: with LIMIT 0, with a comparison of a column alone with NULL, as in `k = ?` or `k = ? + 0` with NULL, or with a test for NULL of the key (`never` over the conditions that are not computed for each row). A subquery with a LIMIT 0 of its own gives NULL and computes none of its constants either. A condition that is computed for each row does not count there, also where its value is NULL: `k + 0 = ?` with NULL gives no row, but the subquery is computed, and an error of its constants comes. `TestEngine_arithmetic` pins both. See [Scalar subqueries](#scalar-subqueries).
- **An expression with a column is computed for each row that the statement reads, in WHERE, or changes, in SET.** It never serves the access, and the engine never rewrites it: `k + 1 = 5` is not turned into `k = 4`.
- **The engine evaluates the conditions of a row in the order of the text, and stops at the first that is not true,** as `&&` does in Go (`filter.holds`). `b <> 0 and a / b > 1` gives no error for the row with `b = 0`, and `a / b > 1 and b <> 0` gives `division by zero`. Both sides of one comparison are computed, in the order of the text, also where the first is NULL, so that an error of the second comes (`condition.computed`), as an operator of the evaluator computes both its operands.
- **A row that the access does not read is not evaluated.** So the outcome depends on the access: a condition that serves as the lookup of the key, or as a bound of the key or of an index, guards every other condition, wherever it stands in the text. `select k from t where a / b > 1 and k > 1` reads by the range of the key and gives the rows 2 and 3. The same select by a path that reads every row fails with `division by zero`. The bound of an index guards in the same way, as in `a / b > 1 and a >= 20`.
- **With LIMIT, the draw of the direction decides too.** A select with LIMIT stops reading at its last row, and a select without ORDER BY reads forward or backward as the engine draws it; see [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows). So the draw decides whether the statement reaches a row that fails. `select k from t where 10 / (k - 1) > 0 limit 1` gives the row 4 read backward, and fails with `division by zero` read forward. A scalar subquery reads up to its first row, so the same holds for its conditions.
- **An update finds its rows before it changes one.** It evaluates its WHERE on every row that its access reads while it collects the keys. Then, for each row that it found, in SQLite's order (`changeOrder`), it computes the values of SET in the order of the text, each from the row as it was before the statement (`update.newRow`). In one pass, an error of the WHERE of a row comes after the changes of the rows that it found before. A delete evaluates its WHERE in the same way.
- **The outcome is SQLite's answer, or an error that SQLite's own result for the failing operator justifies:** for an overflow, a real from two integers; for a division by zero, NULL from two operands that are not NULL; and for a result that is not finite, an infinity. The differential pair checks this; see [The tests of arithmetic](#the-tests-of-arithmetic).
- **A statement that fails changes nothing.** A statement of its own rolls its transaction back. A statement in a transaction rolls back to its savepoint, and the transaction stays open and usable, as after a constraint error. RETURNING gives no rows.

### The types of arithmetic

The rule of exact types holds for arithmetic as for a literal or an argument; see [Types](#types).

- **A value has one of four classes:** integer (an INT or INTEGER column, an integer literal or argument, or an expression of type integer), real, text, and blob. NULL meets every class. An expression whose every value is NULL, because an argument of it is NULL (`expr.TypeNull`), has the class of what it is (`expressionClass`): text for an expression of text, and for arithmetic a class of its own, `classNumber`, which meets integers and reals only. The two sides of a comparison, and a value of SET and its column, must be of one class.
- **Arithmetic never meets text or a blob, whatever its arguments** (`comparable`). A TEXT or BLOB column compared with arithmetic or set to it is refused from the types alone, and so is a string or blob literal, a text or blob argument, or an expression of text compared with arithmetic. This holds also for arithmetic whose every value is NULL, which has the class `classNumber`. SQLite converts the number by the affinity of the column, or orders a number before text and blobs.
- **The messages** follow `s4ql: not supported: `:

  | Statement | Message |
  | --- | --- |
  | `select k from t where s = k + 1` | `an expression of type integer compared with the column t.s of type TEXT` |
  | `select k from t where s = k + ?`, with NULL | `an expression of type null compared with the column t.s of type TEXT` |
  | `select k from t where k = r * 1` | `an expression of type real compared with the column t.k of type INT` |
  | `select k from t where k + 0 = 1.5` | `a real literal compared with an expression of type integer` |
  | `select k from t where k * 1 = r * 1` | `an expression of type real compared with an expression of type integer` |
  | `select k from t where k + 0 = (select s from t)` | `subquery 1, of the column t.s of type TEXT, compared with an expression of type integer` |
  | `update t set a = r + 1` | `an expression of type real for the column t.a of type INT` |
  | `select k from t where s + 1 = 1` | `column of type TEXT in arithmetic (at offset 22)`, from the evaluator |
  | `select k from t where k + ? = 1`, with `'1'` | `argument 1 in arithmetic, which is text (at offset 26)`, from the evaluator |

- **A column alone in SET is copied.** `set a = b` copies the value of the row before the statement, without the evaluator, where the two columns are of one class, for every type; INT and INTEGER are one class. Otherwise it is refused: `update t set a = s` gives `the column t.s of type TEXT for the column t.a of type INT`.
- **A column compared with a column alone is compared as SQLite compares them.** The statement pass takes `where a = b` since the engine step of arithmetic. Where the two columns are of one class, the engine compares the two values of the row: numbers by their number, and text and blobs byte by byte. Otherwise it is refused: `where k = s` gives `the column t.s of type TEXT compared with the column t.k of type INT`. Neither a copy nor such a comparison serves the access.

### The order of the errors with arithmetic

The errors come in SQLite's order, and `TestEngine_arithmetic` pins it against SQLite:

1. **The names.** SQLite resolves the names of the value of each assignment of SET before its column: `update t set z = x` gives `no such column: x`, and `update t set z = 1, a = x` gives `no such column: z`. A name in double quotes in an expression gets the question about quotes, as in `update t set a = a + "x"`. The names of WHERE come after those of SET, and the names of each comparison in the order of the text. A missing column under IS NULL ends the walk at the next comparison with an expression, as at any comparison: `where x is null and a + y = 1` gives `no such column: x`. `row value misused`, for a subquery of `*` beside an expression, has the offset of the leftmost leaf of the expression, as SQLite gives it (`leftmostOffset`).
2. **The lengths** of SET, and then of WHERE, also of an argument inside arithmetic, which gets SQLite's `string or blob too big` before the refusal of a text or a blob in arithmetic (`argumentLengths`).
3. **The checks and binds of the expressions of SET,** in the order of the text (`update.bind`), but in a read-only transaction, and then the types of WHERE (`build.types`).
4. **The constants,** as [When an evaluation error comes](#when-an-evaluation-error-comes) says.
5. **NOT NULL and the class of a value of SET.** `update.check` refuses the class of a value of SET after NOT NULL, as for a plain value. An update with a value that is computed for each row, an expression with a column or a column alone, checks NOT NULL for every column that it sets, on each row that it changes, in the order of the table (`update.newRow`). There its refusals of types come before `NOT NULL constraint failed`. A NULL from arithmetic into a column that does not take NULL gives SQLite's `NOT NULL constraint failed`, as a NULL argument does.

### The error, the event, and the rest

- **An evaluation error is the evaluator's `*expr.Error`, which the engine passes through as it is.** It matches `expr.ErrEvaluation` and its kind. A fault of the evaluator, which matches `expr.ErrInternal`, becomes an internal error of the engine (`fromExpr`). An evaluation error is not "not supported", and not SQLite's error.
- **An evaluation error leaves the count of changes,** as "not supported" does (`ran` in `engine.go`): it is s4ql's own refusal, and not SQLite's error. See [Changes and the last insert rowid](#changes-and-the-last-insert-rowid).
- **Through the driver, it matches `s4ql.ErrEvaluation` and one of `s4ql.ErrOverflow`, `s4ql.ErrDivisionByZero`, and `s4ql.ErrNotFinite`,** with a message such as `s4ql: integer overflow in + (at offset 17)`. The offset is that of the node of the operator, which starts at its first operand.
- **The event** is one for each statement, as before. An evaluation error has the outcome `evaluation_error`, at Debug, with the error in `error`, which names the kind, the operator, and its offset. No attribute was added: the error says what failed and where, and the attributes that the event has say how the rows were read. See [Events](#events).
- **Negative zero is stored as 0.0, as SQLite stores it.** Measured with SQLite 3.54.1, `update t set r = -0.0 * 1` on a REAL column stores 0.0 and gives 0.0 through RETURNING, in a table WITHOUT ROWID and in a rowid table. The evaluator of s4ql gives -0.0. The core stores 0.0 in the cache and in the commit object, a replay into an empty cache gives 0.0, and RETURNING gives 0.0, as the core and `returning.add` did for -0.0 before. A comparison takes 0.0 and -0.0 as equal.
- **The path of each row allocates little.** A condition allocates nothing: it compares the values of the evaluator as a `scalar`. A value of SET allocates one value for each row that gets it.
- **The commit format does not change.** Commits record rows, and the annotation of the statement is its text with its arguments.

### The tests of arithmetic

- **The pinned cases.** `TestEngine_arithmetic` and `TestNewPlan_arithmetic` in `expression_test.go` pin the access, the rule of evaluation, the order of the errors, the messages, and the count of changes.
- **The differential pair.** `TestPair_arithmetic` runs hand-written cases (`arithmeticCases`) and generated statements (`arithmeticCalls`) against SQLite through the differential pair, on the core and in memory. The generated statements are 100 seeds of 40 calls, and 8 seeds under `-short`, the race detector, or the address sanitizer. The pair's rules for a statement with arithmetic are in `expression_test.go`:
  - `pair.checkArithmetic` applies the rules below to each statement with arithmetic that SQLite prepares.
  - `arithmeticRefusals` gives every "not supported" that the rules of s4ql give for the statement, those of the rule of exact types among them. The engine may give any of them, since the engine's own tests pin their order.
  - `pair.justify` requires an evaluation error to be at an operator of the statement whose result in SQLite alone shows the quirk, for a row that the conditions before it in the order of the text hold for. For a condition of an update or a delete without a subquery, the row must also be in the range of the model's access of the select of its WHERE (`pair.writeGuard`, through `stmttest.JustifyWithin`), since a write finds its rows as a select does and no other check compares its access with the model's.
  - `pair.due` requires, where the engine gives no error, that no operator shows a quirk for a row that the access reads and that every condition before the operator holds for. For a select without a subquery, the rows that the access reads are the range of the model's access (`stmttest.ChosenAccess`), chosen from the conditions that are not computed for each row, with each constant as SQLite computes it (`pair.modelGuard`); for an update, a delete, and a select with a subquery, they are those that every condition that may serve the access holds for. A select with LIMIT that gives fewer rows than its LIMIT read every row of its access, and is judged by the range of the model's access too (`readEvery`); one that gives as many is not checked for a row, nor is a subquery.
  - The other instance of the pair reads a write with arithmetic by the rules, and `pair.everyPath` leaves statements with arithmetic out.
  - A write runs once, through `Query` where it has RETURNING, so that its rows are compared with SQLite's, and through `Exec` otherwise; a read runs through both. The calls run also in read-only transactions, where an update or a delete never has an evaluation error, and they change the key with arithmetic and with a copy of a column, on an integer and a real key, with indexes that run with the key and against it.
- **The driver.** `TestArithmetic` in `conn_test.go` of the root package runs goqite's whole receive, the evaluation errors through `Exec`, `Query`, and `QueryRow` in a transaction and outside one, negative zero, and a restart with an empty cache.
- **The generated tests.** `stmttest.Sequence` writes arithmetic since the generated checks of arithmetic of 2026-11-25: through the profile `arithmetic`, and in `mixed` in one comparison of a WHERE in eight and one assignment of SET in eight. The README of `stmttest` has the odds. `TestDifferential` runs 80 sequences of the profile and `TestConn_differential` 80, and the pair's rules above check each statement. After a statement that fails with an evaluation error in a write transaction, `TestConn_differential` reads its table back in the transaction and compares it with SQLite's, so that an update that keeps the rows it changed before the row that failed is found; about one sequence in a hundred reaches such an update (`pair.changedBefore`).
  - `pair.reachOfArithmetic` and `pair.reachServed` in `expression_test.go` count what the sequences reach: each evaluation error in SET, in WHERE, and in a subquery; a select with LIMIT that failed on a row, and one that gave its rows where a row that it did not read fails; a condition computed for each row that rules rows out; an update that sets a column from itself, reads a column that its SET sets before, copies another column, or computes its key; and a constant that serves a get, the pins, or a bound. The pair also counts a statement that succeeds in a write transaction after an evaluation error in it.
  - `pair.checkAccess` checks the access of a select with arithmetic against the model's choice for `pair.reducedSelect`: the conditions that are not computed for each row, each constant replaced by the value that SQLite gives it.
- **The simulation.** The default environment has a cell of 8 seeds of the profile, and the hammer environment one of 12. A refusal of a statement with arithmetic must be one of `arithmeticRefusals`, and an evaluation error must be justified on the state that the statement saw (`run.arithmetic`), for a statement of its own and in a transaction. `tallyOf` counts the statements with arithmetic by how they ended (`arithmeticKinds`).
- **The driver check.** `TestGenerated` in `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck` runs 40 sequences of the profile, and no longer leaves statements with arithmetic out. `pair.checkEvaluation` justifies an evaluation error, also of one run of a prepared statement that runs several times, whose other runs must give what SQLite gives. `pair.checkRefused` checks a refusal against `pair.arithmeticRefusals`. A run that failed with an evaluation error and a refused statement do not run in SQLite, and are excused as `excusedEvaluation` and `excusedArithmetic`.
- **The planted bugs.** Seven bugs of `bugs.go` are of arithmetic: `setFromNew`, `nullCompares`, `evalStops`, `errorSkipsRow`, `copyAcross`, `copySelf`, and `constantsServeNothing`. `TestDifferential_plantedBugs` plants all seven in the first 30 sequences of the profile `arithmetic`, where 10, 6, 5, 10, 4, 15, and 4 find them, and requires 3, 2, 1, 3, 1, 5, and 1. It finds each but `constantsServeNothing` by any difference from SQLite or from the rules of arithmetic, and `constantsServeNothing`, whose rows and errors are SQLite's, by `pair.checkAccess`. `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` plants the other six in the hammer environment with the profile `arithmetic`; see [How it is tested](#how-it-is-tested) and [Results](#results).

## Text

The piece of work text functions lets the places of arithmetic hold text: the operator `||` and the functions `lower` and `hex`, as in `'m_' || lower(hex(?))`, the id of a message of goqite. The engine computes it with the evaluator of [`internal/expr`](../expr/README.md#semantics), by the three steps of [Arithmetic](#arithmetic). `TestEngine_text` pins what the engine does with it against SQLite. The code is in `expression.go`, `operator.go`, and `subquery.go`, and [the todo of text functions](../../docs/todo/text-functions.md#the-engine-and-the-driver) has each decision, each difference from SQLite, and the statements that were measured.

- **Text runs where arithmetic runs:** as the value of an assignment of SET, and on either side of a comparison of the WHERE of a select, an update, a delete, and a scalar subquery. It runs in every statement of `ExecAll`, and through the driver's `Prepare`, `Exec`, and `Query`.
- **The plan is the `*Arithmetic` of arithmetic,** which holds any `stmt.Expression`. `Format` prints a concatenation in parentheses and a call as the statement writes it: `update t set s = 'm_' || lower(hex(b)) where lower(s) = ?` prints as `update t set s = ('m_' || lower(hex(b)))` over `filter lower(s) = ?1`.
- **The values are the evaluator's, which are SQLite's.** `NULL || 'a'` and `lower(NULL)` are NULL, and `hex(NULL)` is the empty text. A NULL compares with nothing, and a column that does not take NULL refuses it with SQLite's `NOT NULL constraint failed`.
- **A comparison of text compares the bytes,** as the collation BINARY does. A condition holds a text as a `scalar`, which shares its bytes with the row or with the value of the evaluator, so a condition still allocates nothing of its own.

The examples below are on a table `t`, of `create table t (k int primary key, s text, u text, v text, b blob, n int) strict, without rowid`, with an index `t_s` on `s`.

### The types of text

The rule of exact types holds for text; see [Types](#types) and [The types of arithmetic](#the-types-of-arithmetic).

- **An expression of text has the class text, whatever its arguments** (`expressionClass`). It is a concatenation or a call at its root, and it keeps the class where an argument that is NULL makes every value NULL. It meets a TEXT column, a string literal, a text argument, NULL, a subquery of a TEXT column, and another expression of text, and nothing else (`comparable`). SQLite orders a number before text, so its `lower(s) > 1` is true for every row whose `s` is not NULL, and it converts text by the affinity of a column.
- **Inside text, the evaluator refuses what is not text:** a number in `||`, `lower`, or `hex`, and a blob in `||` or `lower`, as a column by its declared type and as an argument by its value. SQLite converts each. The message has the offset of the column or the `?` in the whole text, as in `column of type INT in a concatenation (at offset 17)` for `update t set s = n || 'a'`. Within one side of a comparison the columns come before the arguments, and the two sides come in the order of the text.
- **The messages** follow `s4ql: not supported: `:

  | Statement | Message |
  | --- | --- |
  | `select k from t where lower(s) = 1` | `an integer literal compared with an expression of type text` |
  | `select k from t where lower(s) = ?`, with 1 | `argument 1, which is an integer, compared with an expression of type text` |
  | `select k from t where n = lower(s)` | `an expression of type text compared with the column t.n of type INT` |
  | `select k from t where lower(s) = n + 1` | `an expression of type integer compared with an expression of type text` |
  | `select k from t where lower(s) = ? + 1`, with NULL | `an expression of type null compared with an expression of type text` |
  | `select k from t where lower(s) = (select n from t)` | `subquery 1, of the column t.n of type INT, compared with an expression of type text` |
  | `update t set n = lower(?)`, with NULL | `an expression of type text for the column t.n of type INT` |
  | `update t set b = hex(b)` | `an expression of type text for the column t.b of type BLOB` |
  | `select k from t where lower(b) = 'a'` | `column of type BLOB in a call of lower (at offset 28)`, from the evaluator |
  | `select k from t where hex(?) = s`, with 1.5 | `argument 1 in a call of hex, which is a real (at offset 26)`, from the evaluator |

### The order of the names in text

SQLite looks the names inside a call up in an order of its own, and the engine copies it, so no statement is refused for its names. `resolver` in `expression.go` walks the names as SQLite's `resolveExprStep` does:

- **A missing column inside the argument of a call does not end the walk.** It is pending, as a missing column under IS NULL is; see [The order of the errors with arithmetic](#the-order-of-the-errors-with-arithmetic).
- **A later missing column replaces a pending one.** A column that the table has, and a call, go on beside it.
- **Any other node ends the walk with the pending column:** an operator, a literal, a parameter, or the next comparison.
- **A subquery that the walk reaches with a pending column looks up less than it does otherwise** (`subqueries.pendingNames`). In an update and a delete it finds its table and looks no column up. In a select, which found the table before, it looks up the column of its select list. Then the pending column ends the walk.
- **The value of an assignment of SET is a walk of its own** (`resolver.all`). A column that is still pending at its end fails it, before the column of the assignment is looked up.

So, where `w` is another table and no table has a column `nosuch`, `nosuch1`, `nosuch2`, `nosuch3`, or `x`:

- `delete from t where hex(nosuch) = nosuch2` gives `no such column: nosuch2`, and `delete from t where hex(nosuch) = s` gives `nosuch`.
- `select * from t where hex(nosuch) || s || x = 'a'` gives `x`.
- `select * from t where lower(nosuch) = s and nosuch2 is null` gives `nosuch2`, and with `'a'` in place of `s` it gives `nosuch`.
- `update t set s = lower(nosuch1), nosuch2 = 1` gives `nosuch1`.
- `delete from t where lower(nosuch1) <> (select nosuch3 from w)` gives `nosuch1`, where the select of the same WHERE gives `nosuch3`.

`TestEngine_text` compares 34 such statements with SQLite.

### When a text is too long

SQLite gives `string or blob too big` where it would make a text longer than the length limit, and [the README of the evaluator](../expr/README.md#semantics) has the length at which each of the three fails. `bindExpression` passes the limit of the database (`build.limit`) to `Program.Bind`, and `fromExpr` gives SQLite's error (`tooBig`, code 18) for the evaluator's `expr.ErrTooLong`. It is SQLite's error and not an evaluation error: an `*s4ql.Error` through the driver, with the outcome `sqlite_error` in the event. SQLite gives it when it steps the statement, so an update or a delete that fails with it sets the count of changes to 0, also where the engine gives it before any row (`computedTooBig`, which `built` reads in `Conn.write` and `Conn.writeIn`); SQLite's error for an argument that is too long, which it gives when it binds it, leaves the count.

SQLite gives the error where it computes the text, and it computes a constant once: one without a call at the start of the statement, before any row, and one with a call when the first row reaches it, unless the access needs it before. The engine gives the error in the same places, but where the list below and the differences under it say otherwise. In the examples, the two arguments together are longer than the limit.

- **A concatenation without a column and without a call, inside text with a column or inside a value of SET, is computed once before any row** (`hoisted`, `build.hoist`, `computeHoisted`). `update t set u = ? || ? || s`, `update t set u = s || (? || ?)`, and `delete from t where ? || ? || s = 'x'` fail on an empty table. `update t set u = s || ? || ?`, which is `(s || ?) || ?`, and `where lower(? || ?) || s = 'x'` do not.
- **A value of SET without a column that is too long fails on the first row that the update changes** (`build.change`), where SQLite computes it. `update t set u = ? || ?` and `update t set u = lower(? || ?)` give no error on an empty table, or with `where k = 99` where no row has that key.
- **A side of a comparison without a column and without a call is computed before the access,** as for arithmetic (`build.constants`). `where u = ? || ?` fails on an empty table and with LIMIT 0.
- **A side without a column and with a call is computed before the access too, but its error comes there only where its condition may serve the access** (`mayServe`): where the other side is a column alone, the operator is not `!=`, and the column is the key or a column of an index. Otherwise the condition keeps the error (`condition.failed`), serves no access, and gives the error on the first row that it is evaluated on. `where u = 'zz' and v = lower(? || ?)` gives no error where no row has `u = 'zz'`, and `where v = lower(? || ?) and u = 'zz'` fails on the first row. `where lower(u) = lower(? || ?)` and `where u <> lower(? || ?)` give no error on an empty table.
- **In a scalar subquery, a side without a column and without a call is computed before any row of the statement,** also where the statement does not compute the subquery: beside `k is null`, with LIMIT 0, and in a subquery with LIMIT 0. A side that may serve the access of the subquery is the exception, and is computed with the subquery, as SQLite codes it in place where the subquery seeks; only the concatenations without a call among its operands are computed before any row (`select k from t where k is null and u = (select s from e where s = ? || ? || ?)` fails on the key `s` of `e`, and `... where s = ? || ?)` does not).
- **Text with a column is computed for each row,** and fails on the row whose text is too long, such as `hex` of a text of 500 bytes under a limit of 1,000.
- **In a read-only transaction, an update or a delete computes nothing,** and gives `attempt to write a readonly database`, as for arithmetic.
- **A statement that fails changes nothing, and a transaction goes on after it,** as after an evaluation error. SQLite does otherwise in a transaction; see [Where text differs from SQLite](#where-text-differs-from-sqlite).

### The access with text

The examples are on a table `q`, of `create table q (id text primary key, v text, w text) strict, without rowid`, with an index on `v`.

- **Text without a column serves the lookup of the key and the bounds of an index, as an argument does.** `id = 'm_' || ?`, `id = 'm_' || lower(hex(?))`, and `id = lower(?)` read one row by the key, `id > 'm_' || ? and id < 'n'` reads the range of the key, and `v = lower(?)` reads by the index. `id = ? || ?` with an argument that is NULL reads nothing.
- **Text with a column serves nothing.** `lower(id) = ?`, `id = 'm_' || v`, and `v = lower(w)` read every row.

### Where text differs from SQLite

[The todo](../../docs/todo/text-functions.md#the-engine-and-the-driver) has each difference with the statements that were measured.

- **The types are stricter.** Text compared with a number, set into a column of a number or a blob, or meeting arithmetic is refused, and so is a number inside text, or a blob in `||` or `lower`. SQLite orders or converts.
- **A constant with a call that may serve the access fails before any row,** where SQLite computes it only when it uses it. `select k from t where k = 99 and s = lower(? || ?)` fails with `string or blob too big`. SQLite finds no row by the key and gives no row. `TestEngine_text` pins it.
- **A scalar subquery is computed whenever the statement may give a row,** as for arithmetic, where SQLite computes it when a row reaches it. `select k from t where k = 99 and u = (select s from t where s = ? || ?)` fails in s4ql and gives no row in SQLite. `TestEngine_text` pins it.
- **Open: a statement that fails in a transaction.** In s4ql, a statement that fails with `string or blob too big` changes nothing, as after every other error. SQLite keeps the rows that the statement changed before the row that failed, where it holds no statement journal for the statement, so the reads after it in the transaction differ. With the rows `'a'` and 600 bytes of `'b'` under a limit of 1,000, `update t set s = s || s` fails in both in a write transaction, and SQLite's first row is `'aa'` after it, where s4ql's is `'a'`. Arithmetic has no such case, since SQLite gives no error for it. `TestEngine_text` pins the difference. Whether s4ql copies SQLite here is a question of design, and it is not decided.
- **The rows that a statement evaluates are those of its access.** Where the engine's access reads a row that SQLite's does not, or the reverse, one fails with `string or blob too big` and the other does not. SQLite also evaluates the terms of a WHERE that an index covers before the others, and those with a subquery last, where the engine evaluates them in the order of the text. The review of the step found statements where the engine gives no row and SQLite fails: a condition of NULL on a column without an index, which makes the engine read nothing, after a text that is too long, and SQLite's propagation of a constant of an equality into the other terms of a select, which makes them constants that it computes before any row. They are open in [the todo](../../docs/todo/text-functions.md#the-engine-and-the-driver).
- **For a row that both evaluate, s4ql gives SQLite's value.**

### The tests of text

- **The pinned cases.** `TestEngine_text` in `expression_test.go` runs its statements through the duo of the engine and SQLite, which runs each on both and compares the answer, the count of changes, and the tables. It has the values, in SET, in WHERE, and in a subquery; each refusal of the types with its message; the order of the names; the access, read from the event; the texts that are too long, under a limit of 1,000 with arguments of 600 bytes, on an empty table, on one row, and on two; the failed statement in a transaction; the read-only transaction; and the plan.
- **The driver.** `TestText` in `conn_test.go` of the root package computes goqite's id, `'m_' || lower(hex(?))` of 16 bytes, in SET and in WHERE, as SQLite does: directly, prepared, in a transaction, and after a restart with an empty cache. It has the refusals of the types through `Prepare`, `Exec`, and `Query`, and a text that is too long, which changes nothing and leaves the transaction usable.
- **The driver check.** The hand sequence "text in expressions" of `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck` runs text through the calls of the driver against SQLite, also in statements that are prepared and then run, and has the refusals of the types.
- **The shared rules.** `stmttest.ArithmeticRefusals` types text as the engine does, and `stmttest.MissingColumn` walks the names as `resolver` does, so `pair.checkArithmetic` judges a statement with text that `FuzzSQL` gives it.
- **Open.** The generators of `stmttest` do not write text yet, so the generated tests, the simulation, and the driver's generated test do not meet it, and no bug of text is planted. Mutation testing of the new code of the engine is open too.

## How to add a statement of the next slice

1. Add a type or a field to `stmt`. Its README has [the steps](../sql/stmt/README.md#how-to-add-the-next-slice), which start with the agreement on the slice in the roadmap.
2. Add a plan node to `plan.go`, with a case in `NewPlan` and in `Format`.
3. Add an operator to `operator.go`, and build it in `build.node`, `buildValues`, or `buildSink`. Make the lookups there in the order in which SQLite gives their errors. Replace the internal error of each shape of a plan that the slice adds.
4. Add a constructor to `errors.go` for each new error of SQLite, and pin its codes, its message, and the form of each name against SQLite in `TestEngine_errors`.
5. Add the kind of the statement to `report.of` in `telemetry.go`. If the statement sets SQLite's count of changes, add its plan to `setsChanges` in `engine.go`. `write` of `engine.go`, which runs a statement of its own, and `writeIn` of `tx.go`, which runs a statement in a transaction, both ask it. `checked` in `engine.go` decides the count for an error of the sink's `check`: SQLite's error sets it, and "not supported" leaves it.
6. Extend the generator in [`stmttest`](../sql/stmt/stmttest/stmttest.go), and `Sequence` in [`stmttest`](../sql/stmt/stmttest/sequence.go), which the differential tests of the engine and of the driver share: the weights and the odds of the profiles in [`profile.go`](../sql/stmt/stmttest/profile.go), and the near misses in `Others`. [The README of `stmttest`](../sql/stmt/stmttest/README.md) has the steps to add an area of the generator, and to count the least numbers of the tests again.
7. Extend `fake` and `memory` to what the new operator calls.
8. Update this README.

## Errors

SQLite's errors have one type, `*sqlerr.Error` of [`internal/sql/sqlerr`](../sql/sqlerr/sqlerr.go), which the syntax errors of the pass have too. It holds `Code`, `ExtendedCode`, and `Message`, each exactly as SQLite gives it. `Offset` is extra information, and s4ql does not promise it.

| Message | Code | Extended code | The names in the message |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `table %s already exists` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Raw` of the statement: the name as written, with its quotes. |
| `no such table: %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the statement: the name without its quotes. For the table of a create index, the message is `no such table: main.%s`, with the name of the schema before the name. |
| `there is already an index named %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the table of a create table. |
| `there is already a table named %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the index of a create index. |
| `index %s already exists` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the index of a create index. |
| `no such index: %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the index of a drop index. |
| `table %s has no column named %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the statement, for both. |
| `duplicate column name: %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the later column of the statement. From `CreateTable.ColumnsError()` of the pass. |
| `too many columns on %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the table of the statement. From `CreateTable.ColumnsError()` of the pass. |
| `table %s has %d columns but %d values were supplied` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the statement. |
| `%d values for %d columns` | 1 | 1 | None. |
| `no such column: %s` | 1 | 1 | `Name.Value` of the statement. For a name in double quotes, the message is `no such column: "%s" - should this be a string literal in single-quotes?`, because the pinned build of SQLite takes double quotes for names only (`DQS=0`). A column of a create index has both forms too, and so has a column of an ORDER BY. A column of the SET of an update is the exception: its message is `no such column: %s` with `Name.Value` also for a name in double quotes (`noSuchSetColumn`). SQLite's update gives that error itself, where its resolver of names gives the others and adds the question. |
| `NOT NULL constraint failed: %s.%s` | 19 | 1299, `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_NOTNULL` | The names as the table stores them. The column is the first, in the order of the table, that does not take NULL and gets it. |
| `UNIQUE constraint failed: %s.%s` | 19 | 1555, `SQLITE_CONSTRAINT_PRIMARYKEY` | The names as the table stores them, which are those of its CREATE TABLE. |
| `attempt to write a readonly database` | 8, `SQLITE_READONLY` | 8 | None. |
| `string or blob too big` | 18, `SQLITE_TOOBIG` | 18 | None. |
| `row value misused` | 1 | 1 | None. For a scalar subquery of `*` of more than one column: in a select, and in an update or a delete on the left of `!=` or `<>`, or on the left of an arithmetic expression with any operator. See [Scalar subqueries](#scalar-subqueries). |
| `sub-select returns %d columns - expected 1` | 1 | 1 | The number of the columns of the table of a scalar subquery of `*`, in an update or a delete. |
| A syntax error, such as `near "frm": syntax error` | 1 | 1 | From the pass. |

The code 8 is the constant `sqlerr.CodeReadOnly`. The engine gives this error for a statement that would write in a read-only transaction, and `TestConn_Begin` pins it against SQLite under `pragma query_only = 1`. The extended code 1299 is the constant `sqlerr.CodeConstraintNotNull`.

Examples, each pinned against SQLite in `TestEngine_errors`:

- With a table `t1`, `create table "T1" (x int primary key) strict, without rowid` gives `table "T1" already exists`.
- `select * from "T""2"` gives `no such table: T"2`.
- With a table `t1`, `insert into "T1" ("V""2") values (1)` gives `table T1 has no column named V"2`.
- After `create table "Foo bar" ("Bar""x" int primary key) strict, without rowid`, a second insert of the same key through `"FOO BAR" ("BAR""X")` gives `UNIQUE constraint failed: Foo bar.Bar"x`.

The examples of several columns are on a table `t3`, of `create table t3 (v1 int primary key, v2 text not null, v3 real not null) strict, without rowid`:

- `insert into T3 values (1, 'a')` gives `table T3 has 3 columns but 2 values were supplied`.
- `insert into t3 (v3, v1) values (1.5, 1, 'a')` gives `3 values for 2 columns`.
- `insert into T3 (V3, V1) values (1.5, 1)` gives `NOT NULL constraint failed: t3.v2`.
- `select "V""1" from t3` gives `no such column: "V"1" - should this be a string literal in single-quotes?`.
- `create table t (v int primary key, w text not null, "V" real not null) strict, without rowid` gives `duplicate column name: V`.

The examples of NULL are on a table `t`, of `create table t (a text, k int primary key, b real not null, c blob) strict, without rowid`. `TestEngine_nulls` pins each against SQLite:

- `insert into t values ('a', null, 1.5, x'01')` gives `NOT NULL constraint failed: t.k`, and so does `insert into t (a, b) values ('a', 1.5)`, which leaves the key out.
- `insert into t (k, a) values (2, 'a')` gives `NOT NULL constraint failed: t.b`.
- `insert into t (b, a) values (null, 'a')` gives `NOT NULL constraint failed: t.k`. The key, which the list leaves out, is before `b`, which gets the literal, in the order of the table.
- `insert into t values (1, 2, null, null)` gives `NOT NULL constraint failed: t.b`, before the refusal of the integer 1 for the TEXT column `a`.

The order of the errors is SQLite's:

- **A table that does not exist comes before a column that does not exist.** `insert into t2 (v2) values (1)` without `t2` gives `no such table: t2`.
- **A create table gives that the table exists before the errors of its columns:** `table %s already exists`, then `there is already an index named %s` if an index has the name, then `too many columns on %s` or `duplicate column name: %s`, as `CreateTable.ColumnsError()` of the pass gives them, then `string or blob too big` for a table that SQLite cannot write into its schema under the length limit: SQLite writes it with an UPDATE of `sqlite_schema` that holds the name twice and `CreateTable.SQL` of the pass, each in single quotes with a quote doubled, and 96 bytes more, and refuses the statement when it prepares it if that UPDATE is the limit or longer (`schemaTooBig`). With `t3`, `create table T3 (v int primary key, v text not null) strict, without rowid` gives `table T3 already exists`. With IF NOT EXISTS, the statement does nothing where the plain one gives `table %s already exists`, and gives none of the errors after it there; see [IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS](#if-not-exists-and-if-exists).
- **A create index gives the table, then the name of the index, then its columns.** The errors, in order:
  1. `no such table: main.%s`, if the table does not exist.
  2. `there is already a table named %s`, if a table has the name of the index, the table of the statement among them.
  3. `index %s already exists`, if an index has the name, on any table.
  4. `no such column: %s`, for the first column, in the order of the text, that the table does not have, with the question about quotes for a name in double quotes.
  5. `string or blob too big`, if SQLite cannot write the index into its schema under the length limit. SQLite writes it with an INSERT into its schema. The text of that INSERT holds the name of the index, the name of the table as the table stores it, and `CreateIndex.SQL` of the pass, the text that SQLite keeps for the index, each in single quotes with a quote doubled. SQLite refuses the statement when it prepares it if that text is the limit or longer (`indexTooBig`).
  6. "Not supported", as `index of more than 250 columns`, at the 251st column. It is s4ql's own error, and it comes after every error of SQLite.

  With a table `t` that has a column `a` and an index `i`, and a table `u` without a column `nosuch`, `create index t on nosuch (a)` gives `no such table: main.nosuch`, `create index T on u (nosuch)` gives `there is already a table named T`, `create index I on u (nosuch)` gives `index I already exists`, and `create index j on t (a, z desc, y)` gives `no such column: z`. `no such column` has the offset of the name, and the other errors of SQLite have the offset -1. `TestEngine_indexes` pins the order and the offsets against SQLite. With IF NOT EXISTS, the statement does nothing where the plain one gives error 3, and gives none of the errors after it there.
- **A drop index gives `no such index: %s` for a name that no index has,** also when a table has the name. With IF EXISTS, it does nothing there. A drop table with IF EXISTS does nothing where the plain one gives `no such table: %s`.
- **An insert gives the columns of its list that the table does not have, in the order of the list, before the number of its values.** `insert into t3 (v1, x) values (1)` gives `table t3 has no column named x`. The errors that follow are in their order under [Types](#types).
- **A select gives the table before its columns, and the first name of its list that the table does not have.** `select x from t3` without `t3` gives `no such table: t3`, and with it `select v1, x, y from t3` gives `no such column: x`.
