- **A select with WHERE gives the names of its list before those of its conditions, and those in the order of the text.** The first comparison of a column that the table does not have gives `no such column`. A missing column under IS NULL or IS NOT NULL does not stop SQLite: a later such test replaces the message, and the next comparison ends the walk with it, before SQLite looks its own column up. On a table `t` with the key `a`, `select * from t where x is null and y is null` gives `no such column: y`, `select * from t where x is null and y = 1` gives `no such column: x`, and `select * from t where x is not null and a is null and y is not null and a = 2` gives `no such column: y`. A missing column inside the argument of a call does not stop SQLite either; see [The order of the names in text](#the-order-of-the-names-in-text). A name in double quotes gets `no such column: "y" - should this be a string literal in single-quotes?`, and `rowid`, `oid`, and `_rowid_` give `no such column`. After the names comes `string or blob too big` for a value of a condition that is longer than the length limit, an argument, for which SQLite gives it when it binds, or a literal. Last comes "not supported" for the first value of a condition, in the order of the text, that is not NULL and is of another type than its column; see [Types](#types). So `select * from t where a = 'x' and y = 1` gives `no such column: y`. `TestEngine_where` pins the order against SQLite.
- **A select with ORDER BY gives the names of its ORDER BY after those of its conditions.** The first column of the ORDER BY, in the order of the text, that the table does not have gives `no such column`, with the question about quotes for a name in double quotes. `no such table` comes before all the names, and a missing column of the select list or of WHERE, one under IS NULL included, before one of ORDER BY. On a table `t` with the columns `k` and `a`, `select k from t order by a, x, y` gives `no such column: x`, `select k from t where z = 1 order by x` gives `no such column: z`, and `select k from t order by "x" desc limit 1` gives `no such column: "x" - should this be a string literal in single-quotes?`. A column of ORDER BY need not be in the select list, and an alias does not exist yet. The names are checked whatever the LIMIT is: `select k from t where a = 1 order by x limit 0` gives `no such column: x`.
- **After the names of a select come the lengths, then the types, and the argument of LIMIT is the last of each.** `string or blob too big` comes for a value of a condition that is longer than the length limit, and then for an argument of LIMIT that is, as SQLite gives it when it binds. Then comes "not supported" for the first value of a condition of another type than its column, and last "not supported" for an argument of LIMIT that is no integer from 0; see [Parameters](#parameters). So the errors of the names come before those of the arguments, and SQLite's errors before the engine's own. With a TEXT argument for the INT column `a` and the real 1.5 for the LIMIT, `select k from t where a = ? order by x limit ?` gives `no such column: x`, and `select k from t where a = ? order by k limit ?` gives the refusal of argument 1. `TestEngine_orderBy` pins the order against SQLite, under a length limit of 1,000 bytes.
- **An update gives the table, then the columns of its SET, then those of its WHERE.** The first column of SET, in the order of the text, that the table does not have gives `no such column`, without the question about quotes: with `t3`, `update t3 set v2 = 'x', z = 1, y = 2` gives `no such column: z`, and `update t3 set "Z""z" = 1` gives `no such column: Z"z`. The columns of WHERE follow, by the rule of a select, with the question for a name in double quotes. So `update t3 set z = 1 where y is null` gives `no such column: z`. A delete gives the table, and then the columns of its WHERE by the same rule. The errors that follow are in their order under [Types](#types). `TestEngine_update` and `TestEngine_delete` pin the order against SQLite.
- **In a read-only transaction, every error that SQLite gives when it prepares the statement comes before `attempt to write a readonly database`:** the errors of the names, the number of values of an insert, and the errors of the columns of a create table. There, `insert into t2 (v1) values (1)` without `t2` gives `no such table: t2`, and `create table t1 …` with `t1` gives `table t1 already exists`. A create index and a drop index give the errors of their names there, and a create index or a drop index without such an error gives `attempt to write a readonly database`. A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that does nothing gives no error there. An insert of a key that exists gives `attempt to write a readonly database` there, and not `UNIQUE constraint failed`, as in SQLite. So does an insert of NULL, given or for a column left out, into a column that does not take it, and one of a value of another type than its column. So does an insert that would fail by a default: by DEFAULT NULL in a column that does not take NULL, by a record that a default makes too long, or by the default of the key where that key exists. An update and a delete give three kinds of error there before `attempt to write a readonly database`: the errors of their names, `string or blob too big` for a value that is too long, and "not supported" for a value of WHERE of another type than its column. Every other update and delete gives `attempt to write a readonly database` there, whether it finds a row or not: also an update that sets NULL in a column that does not take it, a value of another type, or a key that exists.
- **A syntax error never depends on the database.** The spec: "Text with a syntax error gets the error that SQLite gives for it on an empty database." So `stmt.Check` alone decides it, before the engine begins a transaction, and a text with a syntax error gives its syntax error also on a closed instance, without the lease, and with a context that has ended. SQLite differs on a database that is not empty: it starts to create a table as soon as it has read `CREATE TABLE`, the name, and the `(` or `AS` after it, and if a table of the name exists, it gives `table %s already exists` and never meets the syntax error. With a table `t1`, SQLite gives `table t1 already exists` for `create table t1 (v1 int primary key) strict without rowid`, and the engine gives `near "without": syntax error`, as SQLite does on an empty database. This is the one difference from SQLite that the tests accept: `stmttest.ExistsAfterStart`, which the differential tests of the engine and of the driver share, accepts it where `stmttest.StartedTable` gives the name of the table that the text starts, SQLite gives that the table exists with the name as written, and the engine gives what SQLite gives on an empty database. The other texts where SQLite gives an error that is not the syntax error are in [the known gaps of `stmt`](../sql/stmt/README.md#known-gaps).

The errors that are not SQLite's:

- **"Not supported"** is the `*stmt.NotSupportedError` of the pass, unchanged. It matches `stmt.ErrNotSupported`. The engine runs every form that the pass supports, and refuses none of them by its text: `check` in `engine.go` gives what `stmt.Check` gives, or `ErrNoStatement`. Its refusals of the texts of slices 10 and 11, `existsRefusal` and `defaultRefusal`, are gone since the engine runs both slices, and so are `expressionRefusal` and `textRefusal`, since the engine evaluates arithmetic and text. The driver's `check` in `conn.go` calls `stmt.Check` alone, and the `checkText` wrappers of the tests are gone. A form that the pass does not support, such as `-null`, which is `NULL after a sign`, or a WHERE with `or`, stays "not supported" by the text alone.
- **A value of another type than its column is "not supported",** in an insert, in the SET of an update, and in a condition of a select, an update, or a delete; see [Types](#types).
- **An argument of `limit ?` that is not an integer from 0 is "not supported",** by the same rule of exact types; see [Parameters](#parameters).
- **An evaluation error of arithmetic is the evaluator's `*expr.Error`,** which the engine passes through as it is, and which matches `expr.ErrEvaluation` and its kind: an integer overflow, a division by zero, or a real result that is not finite, as in `s4ql: integer overflow in + (at offset 17)`. It is neither "not supported" nor SQLite's error. See [Arithmetic](#arithmetic).
- **An index of more than 250 columns is "not supported",** as `index of more than 250 columns`, with the offset of the 251st column. SQLite takes an index of up to 2,000 columns.
- **A text without a statement** gives `ErrNoStatement`.
- **The state of the transaction of a connection has four errors:** `ErrTxOpen`, `ErrNoTx`, `ErrRowsOpen`, and `ErrTxDone`. Each is returned as it is, but for the commit of a write transaction while a loop reads its rows, whose error wraps `ErrRowsOpen`. See [Transactions](#transactions).
- **A commit that is too large gives an error that matches `ErrTooLarge`,** and that wraps the error of the core, which matches `core.ErrTooLarge`.
- **Every other error of the core passes through unchanged,** so that `errors.Is` finds its sentinel, such as `core.ErrStopped`, `core.ErrOutcomeUnknown`, or `core.ErrReaderBusy`. So does an error of the context. Neither is ever given as a `*sqlerr.Error`.
- **A refusal of the core becomes an error that matches `ErrInternal`** and wraps nothing else; see [The storage interface](#the-storage-interface). It is a fault of s4ql, and never SQLite's error.
- **A table or an index whose statement in the cache is longer than the length limit is "not supported",** where SQLite takes it under a lowered limit: `s4ql: not supported: the table t, whose statement in the cache is longer than the length limit`, or `the index i, …`. The cache writes its statement with every name in double quotes and each default in a canonical form, in which `.5` is `5e-01`, so the statement can be longer than the text that SQLite measures. The core refuses it with an error that wraps `core.ErrTooLongForCache` beside `core.ErrInvalid`. `coreTx.createTable` and `coreTx.createIndex` in `storage.go` turn that error into `tooLongForCache` of `errors.go`, and every other `core.ErrInvalid` stays an internal error. The error comes where the core creates the table or the index, after every error of SQLite, and the transaction goes on. Until 2026-11-21 the engine gave an internal error here. SQLite takes the text, and s4ql either does what SQLite does or says "not supported". [The todo](../../docs/todo/slice-04.md) has the known difference.

## Changes and the last insert rowid

The engine gives what is needed to report `sqlite3_changes` and `sqlite3_last_insert_rowid` as SQLite 3.54.1 does. The differential test compares both with SQLite after every statement.

- **An insert that succeeds sets the count of changes to 1.** Its result has `Changes` 1 and `SetsChanges` true.
- **An update and a delete that succeed set the count to the number of rows that they find.** An update counts a row also when its values do not change, as SQLite does. Without WHERE the count is every row of the table, and for a statement that finds no row it is 0. Their result has `SetsChanges` true.
- **CREATE TABLE, DROP TABLE, CREATE INDEX, DROP INDEX, and SELECT leave the count as the statement before left it.** Their result has `SetsChanges` false. A select with WHERE leaves the count also when it fails, with SQLite's error or with "not supported".
- **The four statements with IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS leave the count too,** whether they do their work or nothing, as the plain statements do. The driver gives the same `RowsAffected` for them as for the plain statements, and `LastInsertId` 0.
- **An insert that fails with `UNIQUE constraint failed` sets the count to 0.** SQLite gives this error when it steps the statement. The engine gives `Result{SetsChanges: true}` with the error of an insert that fails after its checks: in its operator, in its annotation, or in its commit. For an insert in a write transaction, the release of its savepoint takes the place of the commit.
- **An insert that fails with `NOT NULL constraint failed` sets the count to 0 too.** SQLite gives this error when it steps the statement, as it gives `UNIQUE constraint failed`. The sink's `check` finds it, and gives "not supported" for a value of another type than its column there too, which leaves the count. `checked` in `engine.go` tells the two apart: SQLite's error gets `Result{SetsChanges: true}`, and every other error of the check the empty result.
- **An insert that a read-only transaction refuses sets the count to 0,** as SQLite does under `pragma query_only = 1`. Its result is `Result{SetsChanges: true}`. `TestConn_Begin` measured it against SQLite. A create or a drop that is refused there leaves the count.
- **An update or a delete that fails when SQLite steps it sets the count to 0:** an update with `NOT NULL constraint failed`, with `UNIQUE constraint failed`, or with `string or blob too big` for a row whose record is too long, an update or a delete with `string or blob too big` for a text that an expression computes, also before any row, and an update or a delete that a read-only transaction refuses. Every other failure leaves the count: an error of its names, `string or blob too big` for a value that is too long, "not supported", and an evaluation error.
- **An evaluation error of arithmetic leaves the count,** also when it comes while an update changes its rows, where SQLite gives a value, runs the statement, and sets the count. It is s4ql's own refusal, as "not supported" is, and `ran` in `engine.go` gives the empty result for it. See [Arithmetic](#arithmetic).
- **An error that SQLite gives when it prepares the statement leaves the count:** `no such table`, `has no column named`, `already exists`, `no such column`, the two errors of the number of values of an insert, the two errors of the columns of a create table, the errors of the names of a create index and of a drop index, and a syntax error. A text without a statement leaves it too.
- **The last insert rowid never changes.** Every table is WITHOUT ROWID, and SQLite does not record an insert into such a table. The value is 0 on a new connection, and stays 0.

The engine keeps no count between statements. A connection that reports the counts as SQLite does keeps one number, its count of changes, which starts at 0. After a statement whose result has `SetsChanges`, also one that failed, the count is `Result.Changes`; after any other statement, the count stays.

Its last insert rowid is always 0.

## Annotations

- **Each write that is committed carries one annotation: the text of its statement,** from its first token to its end. The end is that of its semicolon, if it has one.
- **The commit object of a transaction that spans statements holds one annotation for each of its statements that succeeded and that write,** in their order. `TestConn_Commit` reads them from the log.
- **Comments and semicolons before the statement, and the text after it, are left out.** A comment inside the statement stays. `  /* before */ create table t1 (v1 int primary key) strict, without rowid; -- after` is annotated as `create table t1 (v1 int primary key) strict, without rowid;`.
- **An annotation holds all the arguments of the statement,** by position, in `Statement.parameters` of the commit object: one for each parameter of an insert, an update, or a delete, and none otherwise. Those of an update are the arguments of its SET and then those of its WHERE. `insert into t3 (v3, v1, v2) values (?, 1, ?)` with the arguments 1.5 and `a` is annotated with both, in that order. An argument that is NULL is kept as the format's NULL.
- **A statement that fails commits nothing,** and leaves no annotation. A query leaves none either.
- **A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that does nothing leaves no annotation,** of its own and in a transaction. SQLite changes nothing for it, so a replay of the log gives SQLite's state without it. One that does its work is annotated with its text, the clause in it.
- **Every write of slice 1 that succeeds changes something.** The core writes no commit object for a transaction that changed nothing, and would then store its annotation nowhere. No statement of slice 1 on its own meets that case: a create, an insert of a new key, and a drop each change the database.
- **A transaction that spans statements can meet that case.** A create and a drop of the same table succeed and change nothing in the end. The transaction then writes no commit object, and its annotations are stored nowhere.
- **An update or a delete that finds no row meets it too.** As a statement of its own it succeeds and writes no commit object, so its annotation is stored nowhere. One that finds a row writes a commit object, also an update that sets the values that the row has.

## Parameters

Slice 2 adds the plain `?` as a value of an insert; [the README of `stmt`](../sql/stmt/README.md#insert) defines it, and `Statement.Parameters()` gives how many a statement has.

- **The text is checked first, then the arguments,** in `prepare`, before the statement needs a transaction: a syntax error, "not supported", or `ErrNoStatement` comes whatever the arguments are, and the errors of the arguments come on a stopped instance and with a context that has ended too.
- **Another number of arguments than parameters** fails with an error that matches `ErrArguments`, such as `s4ql: wrong number of arguments: 2 for a statement with 1 parameter`. SQLite binds NULL to a parameter without an argument; the engine refuses it.
- **An argument that no column holds** is "not supported", in an error that matches `stmt.ErrNotSupported`, before the statement needs a transaction: a value of no kind (`s4ql: not supported: argument 1, which has no value`), a real that is not a number or is infinite (`which is a real that is not a number`, `which is an infinite real`), and text that is not valid UTF-8 or holds the character NUL (`which is text that is not valid UTF-8`, `which is text that holds the character NUL`). The core refuses each of these with `core.ErrInvalid`, which the engine would report as an internal error, so none may reach it. An integer, every other real and text, a blob, also an empty one, and NULL are values. Whether a value is of the type of its column, and whether its column takes NULL, is checked later, when the table is known: see [Types](#types).
- **An argument that is NULL is a value since slice 6.** `checkArgument` takes it, and the refusal `argument 1, which is NULL` is gone. The driver gives NULL for a nil argument; the decisions of slice 6 in [`docs/todo/slice-06.md`](../../docs/todo/slice-06.md) list what `database/sql` hands the driver as nil.
- **The insert binds the argument through `*Parameter`** of the plan, which `eval` reads from the arguments. A parameter without its argument is an internal error, which the check of the number rules out.
- **The parameters of a select are numbered across its conditions, in the order of the text.** `select v1, v2 from t3 where v1 >= ? and v2 != ? and v3 < ?` has the parameters 1, 2, and 3, and `build.values` reads each argument through `*Parameter` too. A select writes nothing, so no annotation holds its arguments.
- **The `?` of LIMIT is numbered last, after those of WHERE,** which is the order of the text. `select v1 from t1 where v2 > ? order by v1 limit ?` has the parameters 1 and 2, and its plan prints `limit ?2`. ORDER BY takes no parameter.
- **The argument of `limit ?` must be an integer from 0** (`limitCount`). An int64 from 0 runs, up to the largest of 64 bits. Every other argument is "not supported", in an error that matches `stmt.ErrNotSupported`: `s4ql: not supported: argument 1, which is a real, for LIMIT, which takes an integer from 0`, or `which is a negative integer`, `which is text`, `which is a blob`, or `which is NULL`. The message names the argument by its number and by what it is, in the manner of the message for a value of another type than its column, and holds no value.
- **The refusal follows the rule of exact types, so it differs from SQLite in three ways.** SQLite converts a real that holds an integer, such as 2.0, and a text that holds one, such as `'2'`, and limits by it; the engine refuses both. SQLite runs a negative integer as no limit; the engine refuses it, as the statement pass refuses a literal `-1`. SQLite gives `datatype mismatch` for every other real and text, for a blob, and for NULL; the engine says "not supported". So a literal and an argument of LIMIT follow one rule. `limit 0` gives no row, in SQLite and in the engine.
- **The refusal is the last error of a select.** It comes after SQLite's errors of the names and of the lengths, and after the refusal of a value of a condition by its type; see [Errors](#errors). It comes whatever the rows are: also on a table without rows that pass, and for a `get`.
- **The parameters of an update are numbered across its SET and then its WHERE, in the order of the text,** and those of a delete across its conditions. `update t3 set v2 = ?, v3 = ? where v1 >= ? and v2 < ?` has the parameters 1 to 4. `build.change` reads the arguments of SET, and `build.bind` those of WHERE.
- **No value of an argument is in an error or an event.**

## Types

Slice 4 adds the column types `integer`, `real`, `text`, and `blob` beside `int`, and real, string, and blob literals and arguments; [the roadmap](../../docs/roadmap.md#slice-4-the-other-types) has what it includes.

- **A column keeps its type as declared.** `NewPlan` maps `stmt.Type` to the format's `Type`, so INT and INTEGER stay apart, in the commit object and after a replay, and `Rows.Columns` gives the declared type as SQLite reports it: `INT`, `INTEGER`, `REAL`, `TEXT`, or `BLOB`.
- **A value must have the type of its column:** an integer for INT and INTEGER, a real for REAL, text for TEXT, and a blob for BLOB. Any other value is "not supported", also where SQLite would convert it (the integer 1 for a REAL column, `'1'` for an INT column) and where SQLite gives an error of its own (`'a'` for an INT column, text for a BLOB column). The message names the value and the column, with the names as the table stores them, and no value: `s4ql: not supported: argument 1, which is text, for the column t1.v1 of type INT`, and `s4ql: not supported: a string literal for the column t1.v1 of type INT`, or `an integer literal`, `a real literal`, `a blob literal`. Such a refusal leaves the count of changes, as every "not supported" does.
- **A value that a condition compares with a column must have the type of the column too,** also where SQLite would convert it, such as `'1'` for an INT column and `1` for a REAL column. `checkType`, which also checks the values of an insert, refuses the first such value, in the order of the text, after SQLite's errors of the names and of the length. The messages are `s4ql: not supported: a string literal compared with the column t.a of type INT`, or `an integer literal`, `a real literal`, `a blob literal`, and `s4ql: not supported: argument 1, which is text, compared with the column t.a of type INT`, with the names as the table stores them. A NULL has no type and is never refused. The refusal comes in a read-only transaction too, because a select reads there as anywhere.
- **A value of the SET of an update must have the type of its column, as a value of an insert,** with the messages of an insert: `s4ql: not supported: an integer literal for the column t3.v2 of type TEXT`. The rule is of the text, so it holds also when the update finds no row, where SQLite would change nothing: with `t3`, `update t3 set v2 = 1 where v1 = 9` is refused. A value of a condition of an update or a delete is checked as that of a select, with the messages of a select, and in a read-only transaction too.
- **NULL has no type.** A column of any type that takes NULL holds it, and a NULL is never refused for its type: the check of the type passes it and goes on to the other columns. Whether a column takes NULL is the check of NOT NULL, which comes before the check of the type. A query gives a NULL back as NULL.
- **The order of the errors of an insert is SQLite's,** where SQLite gives an error: the errors of the text and of the arguments, then the names (`no such table`, then `has no column named` for the columns of the list in their order) and the number of values (`table t has N columns but M values were supplied` without a list of columns, `M values for N columns` with one), which SQLite gives when it prepares the statement; then `string or blob too big` for a text or a blob longer than the limit, which SQLite gives when it binds such an argument, so only a value that the statement gives meets it, and never a default; then, in a read-only transaction, `attempt to write a readonly database`, which SQLite gives at its `OP_Transaction`, before it looks at a value; then `NOT NULL constraint failed` for the first column, in the order of the table, that does not take NULL and gets it, the key column too, which SQLite tests before it looks at a type; then "not supported" for the first column, in the order of the table, whose value is not NULL and is of another type, where SQLite has its `OP_TypeCheck`; then `string or blob too big` for a row whose record is longer than the limit while its values are not, also where a default makes it so and when its key exists, because SQLite builds the record of a row of a WITHOUT ROWID table before it looks for its key; and last `UNIQUE constraint failed`, also for a key from its default. A NULL that the statement gives, as a literal or as an argument, and a column that it leaves out and that gets NULL, from DEFAULT NULL or for want of a default, are one case of NOT NULL, in the order of the table, also mixed in one statement. `buildSink` makes the checks up to the length, the sink's `check` makes those of NOT NULL and of the type, and `run` the rest. `run` asks the core to insert each row only if its key is new, `core.Tx.Insert`, which runs SQLite's `insert ... on conflict do nothing`: SQLite measures the record there as it does for its own insert, so the length at which the row is too long is SQLite's for every shape of table, and for a row that fits and whose key the table has nothing is inserted. `TestEngine_errors`, `TestEngine_tooBig`, `TestEngine_nulls`, and `TestConn_Begin` pin the order against SQLite, and `TestEngine_defaults` pins each pair of neighbours of it with defaults in play.
- **The order of the errors of an update is SQLite's too,** where SQLite gives an error: the errors of the text and of the arguments; then the names (`no such table`, then `no such column` for the columns of SET in the order of the text, then `no such column` for those of WHERE by the rule of a select); then `string or blob too big` for a value of SET, and then of WHERE, that is longer than the limit, each in the order of the text, which is the order of the parameters; then "not supported" for the first value of WHERE, in the order of the text, of another type than its column, as for a select; then, in a read-only transaction, `attempt to write a readonly database`; then `NOT NULL constraint failed` for the first column of SET, in the order of the table, the key among them, that gets NULL and does not take it, if the update finds a row; then "not supported" for the first value of SET, in the order of the table, of another type than its column, whether the update finds a row or not; and last, for each row in the order of the key, `string or blob too big` for a row whose record is too long, and then `UNIQUE constraint failed` for a new key that another row has. `build.change` makes the checks up to the values of WHERE, `update.check` those of NOT NULL and of the type of SET, and `run` the rest. A delete has the errors of its table and of its WHERE, in the same places. `TestEngine_update`, `TestEngine_delete`, and `TestConn_Begin` pin the order against SQLite.
- **The limit is the core's,** `core.Options.LengthLimit`, 1,000,000,000 bytes by default, which `DB.LengthLimit` gives. Tests lower it to 1000, and compare with SQLite under `sqlite3_limit` at the same value. The engine checks the length of every value that a statement gives, a literal too, after the names; the core gives `core.ErrTooBig` for the record of a row, which `fromCore` turns into SQLite's error, and the transaction goes on after it. A literal longer than the limit is not compared with SQLite: SQLite meets one only with a limit below the length of the text that holds it, which `SQLITE_LIMIT_SQL_LENGTH`, also 1,000,000,000, rules out, and under the lowered limit alone it gives `string or blob too big` for a string literal only when it steps the statement, and turns a blob literal into NULL. In a condition of a select it does the same: such a string literal gets `string or blob too big`, also on an empty table, except as the upper bound of a search of the key, which SQLite evaluates only when the search reaches a row, and such a blob literal is NULL, so the select gives no rows. The engine gives `string or blob too big` for both, as for an insert. `TestEngine_where` compares the string literal with SQLite, and pins the difference of the blob literal. A literal of SET that is longer than the limit gets `string or blob too big` also when the update finds no row, where SQLite steps no value and gives no error; `TestEngine_update` pins that difference. [`docs/todo/sql-front-end.md`](../../docs/todo/sql-front-end.md) has the first, beside the open item on `statement too long`, and [`docs/todo/slice-08.md`](../../docs/todo/slice-08.md) the second.
- **A NULL takes no bytes of its own in SQLite's record,** so a row with NULL is too long at another length than the same row with a value in the place of the NULL. The core measures the record as SQLite does. `TestEngine_tooBig` finds a length at which a row with NULL fits and the same row with a real in its place does not, and compares both with SQLite, also in a transaction and after a replay of the log.
- **A real that is -0.0 is stored as 0.0,** by the core, as SQLite stores it, and read back as 0.0. It is the key of 0.0, so either after the other is `UNIQUE constraint failed`. The annotation keeps the argument as given, -0.0. The core keeps a default of -0.0 as 0.0 too, so a key from such a default is the key of 0.0.
- **Arithmetic and text in expressions follow the same rule, by classes of values:** integer, real, text, and blob, where INT and INTEGER are one class, arithmetic never meets text or a blob, and an expression of text meets text only. See [The types of arithmetic](#the-types-of-arithmetic) and [The types of text](#the-types-of-text).
- **Keys compare as SQLite compares them,** which the cache does: integers and reals by their number, text and blobs byte by byte. A scan gives the rows in that order of its key, or in the reverse of it; see [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows). The test double in `memory_internal_test.go` compares them so too.

## Events

The engine logs one event for each statement, named `statement`, when the statement ends. For a query, that is when its rows end. For an insert, an update, or a delete with RETURNING, it is when the write ends, before its rows are read: `report.end` logs once, and the end of the rows logs nothing more. It logs one event for each transaction that spans statements, named `transaction`, when the transaction ends; see [The event of a transaction](#the-event-of-a-transaction). The names are in `telemetry.go`.

| Attribute | Value | Present |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `statement.kind` | `create_table`, `drop_table`, `create_index`, `drop_index`, `insert`, `update`, `delete`, or `select`. | When the text is a supported statement. |
| `statement.table` | The name of the table, as its value. A drop index names no table, and has the empty name. | When the text is a supported statement. |
| `statement.sql` | The text that the call was given. | When the level of the event is Debug. |
| `transaction.kind` | The kind of the transaction that spans statements: `write` or `read_only`. | For a statement that the connection is given while such a transaction is open. |
| `statement.position` | The last commit object of the state that the statement saw: the position of its read transaction, or the parent of its write transaction. In a transaction that spans statements, it is the state that the transaction began from. | Once the statement has a transaction. |
| `commit.parent` | The same value, under the core's name. | For a statement in a write transaction, also a query in a write transaction that spans statements. |
| `statement.access` | How a select, an update, or a delete reads the rows of its table: `none` for no row, `primary_key` for a get or a scan by the primary key, `index` for a scan by an index, or `full_scan`. | Once the access is chosen. A statement that fails before that has neither this attribute nor `statement.rows_read`. |
| `statement.index` | The name of the index of an access by an index, as the index was created. For a create index and a drop index, the name of the index that the statement names, as its value. | For an access by an index, and for a create index and a drop index. |
| `statement.direction` | The direction that the engine drew for the scan or the get of a query, `forward` or `backward`, so that a failure that depends on the order of the rows of a query can be told from its event, also where the run has no seed. For a query without ORDER BY it is the direction of the scan, and for a query whose rows are sorted, the direction of the rows that tie. For an update or a delete with RETURNING through `Query` it is the direction of its rows, of which `forward` is the order in which the statement changed them. Through `Exec`, which gives no rows, the engine draws all the same, and the statement has none. | For a query, and for an update or a delete with RETURNING, with `statement.access`. An update and a delete without RETURNING read forward and draw nothing, and have none. An insert with RETURNING has no access, and has none. A query whose access delivers the order of its ORDER BY has none either: the draw decides nothing for it. Neither has a statement under a path of the tests that has a direction, nor a statement with RETURNING that a read-only transaction refuses: both draw nothing. |
| `statement.order` | How the rows of a query with ORDER BY came in its order. `delivered`: the key of the access holds them so, and nothing sorted. An access of no row and a get deliver every order. `sorted`: the storage sorted them, and read every row of the access before the first. The constant is `attrOrder`. | For a query with ORDER BY, with `statement.access`. A statement without ORDER BY has none. |
| `statement.rows_read` | The number of rows that the statement read from the cache after the choice of its access, by its scans and its gets and those of its scalar subqueries, whether or not its conditions held for them. For a query, these are the rows of its scan or its get. An update reads each row that it changes a second time, to write it, and one row more when it checks a NULL for a column that does not take it. A delete with RETURNING through `Query` reads each row that it deletes a second time, with the `get` before the delete; through `Exec` it reads none again. | With `statement.access`. |
| `statement.subqueries` | The number of scalar subqueries in the text of a select, an update, or a delete. | For a statement with a subquery, also one that failed. |
| `statement.subqueries.found` | How many of the subqueries found a row. 0 for a goqite receive says that no message was ready. | For a statement with a subquery, with `statement.access`. |
| `statement.subqueries.rows_read` | The rows that the subqueries read from the cache, together. `statement.rows_read` counts them too. | With `statement.subqueries.found`. |
| `statement.subqueries.access` | The access of each subquery, in the words of `statement.access`, in the order of the text, joined by commas. A subquery of LIMIT 0 has `none`. | With `statement.subqueries.found`. |
| `statement.subqueries.index` | The index of each subquery, in the order of the text, joined by commas, and empty for one that reads by no index. | With `statement.subqueries.found`, where a subquery reads by an index. |
| `statement.subqueries.order` | `delivered` or `sorted` for each subquery, in the words of `statement.order`, in the order of the text, joined by commas, and empty for one without ORDER BY. | With `statement.subqueries.found`, where a subquery has an ORDER BY. |
| `statement.nothing` | Whether a statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS did nothing: true, or false for one that went on to do what the plain statement does, also if it then failed, as in a read-only transaction. The constant is `attrNothing`. | For a statement of the clause, once its sink is built. A statement without the clause has none. Neither has one that failed before that: with an error of its names, of its columns, or of the length limit, or before it read the database. |
| `statement.rows` | The number of rows that the statement returned or changed. For a statement with RETURNING it is the rows that it changed, which are as many as it returns. | Always. |
| `outcome` | How the statement ended, in a word; see below. | Always. |
| `sqlite.code`, `sqlite.extended_code` | The result codes. | For SQLite's error. |
| `error` | The text of the error. | When the statement failed. |
| `duration.total` | The duration from the call to the end, on the monotonic clock of the host. | Always. |

The level follows the conventions of the core, so a healthy instance logs nothing at the default level, Info:

| Level | `outcome` | When |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Debug | `ok` | The statement succeeded. |
| Debug | `sqlite_error` | It failed with SQLite's error, which is an answer to the application. |
| Debug | `not_supported` | It is not supported. So is a statement that reads on a connection whose rows of an earlier query are open, which the core refuses with `ErrReaderBusy`: it is what the caller asked for, and nothing that went wrong. An error that matches `ErrRowsOpen` has this outcome too. |
| Debug | `evaluation_error` | Its arithmetic failed with an evaluation error: an integer overflow, a division by zero, or a real result that is not finite. `error` names the kind, the operator, and its offset. It is an answer to the application, and nothing that went wrong. See [Arithmetic](#arithmetic). |
| Debug | `tx_state` | It failed with `ErrTxOpen`, `ErrNoTx`, or `ErrTxDone`: the state of the transaction of the connection does not allow it. |
| Debug | `too_large` | Its commit failed with an error that matches `ErrTooLarge`. It is the application's transaction that is too large, and nothing that went wrong. |
| Debug | `no_statement` | The text holds no statement. |
| Debug | `wrong_arguments` | It was given another number of arguments than it has parameters. |
| Debug | `refused` | The core refused it with `ErrNoLease` or `ErrStopped`. The core logs that itself. |
| Debug | `canceled` | Its context ended. |
| Warn | `core_error` | It failed with another error of the core, such as a commit of unknown outcome. |
| Error | `internal_error` | It failed with an internal error, or panicked in the engine. |

- **A statement that panics in the engine logs one event,** with the outcome `internal_error` and the error `s4ql: internal error: the statement panicked`, and the panic goes on to the caller. This holds for a panic while the statement is checked, planned, or built, while a write runs, and while a source gives the rows of a query. A panic in the body of a loop over `Rows.All` is the application's, and ends the rows as a break does: the event has the outcome `ok` and the rows given until then.
- **The event of a write statement joins the core's event of its commit** by `commit.parent`, which is the same in both, and by the context, which both are logged with.
- **The SQL text is only in an event at Debug,** because it can hold the application's data. An event at Warn or Error has no text.
- **No value of a row is in any event.**
- **Of the three errors of `tx_state`, only `ErrTxDone` reaches an event now:** the event of a query whose loop the end of its transaction cut. A begin logs no event, and a commit or a roll back without a transaction logs none.

### The event of a transaction

The event `transaction` has the style of the event of a statement. Its level and its `outcome` follow the table of levels above, for the error of the commit or the roll back. A commit that succeeds and a roll back that succeeds are at Debug.

| Attribute | Value | Present |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `transaction.kind` | `write` or `read_only`. | Always. |
| `transaction.end` | `commit` or `rollback`. A close of the connection is a roll back. | Always. |
| `transaction.statements` | The number of statements that the connection was given while the transaction was open, also those that failed. | Always. |
| `transaction.writes` | The number of statements that changed the database and succeeded, each of which the transaction annotated. A commit object holds them only if the transaction commits and changes something in the end. | Always. |
| `transaction.position` | The last commit object of the state that the transaction began from. | Always. |
| `commit.parent` | The same value, under the core's name. | For a write transaction. |
| `outcome` | How the commit or the roll back ended, in a word. | Always. |
| `error` | The text of the error. | When the commit or the roll back failed. |
| `duration.total` | The duration from the call of the begin to the end, on the monotonic clock of the host. The wait of `Begin` for the place of the writer is in it. | Always. |

- **The event of a write transaction joins the core's event of its commit** by `commit.parent`, as the event of a write statement does.
- **The event of a statement of the transaction has the position of the transaction:** its `statement.position` is the `transaction.position`.
- **The event has no SQL text and no value.**
- **The event is logged when the transaction has ended.** That is when `Commit` or `Rollback` returns, or, if a loop reads rows of the transaction then, when the loop ends and the transaction ends with it. The event then has the error that `Commit` gave, or else the error of that later end, and its duration goes to that end.

## How it is tested

SQLite is the judge. `oracle` in `differential_test.go` runs each text on a database in memory through the binding in [`internal/sqlite`](../sqlite/README.md), as `sqlite3_prepare_v2` and `sqlite3_step` run it.

The layers:

1. **The differential test.** `TestDifferential` runs random sequences of statements, of each profile of the generator, on SQLite and on the engine, which runs on the real core with a simulated store. After each statement it compares the answer: the error as code, extended code, and message, or the columns and the rows. It also compares the count of changes, and requires a last insert rowid of 0. A statement that fails must leave SQLite's count as it was, with three exceptions that `pair.step` accepts, each of which sets the count to 0: a broken constraint, an insert, an update, or a delete that a read-only transaction refuses, and an insert or an update whose record is too long while none of its values is. The second occurs only in `TestConn_differential`, which runs the same step in transactions, and the third only under the lowered length limit. The generator marks each statement that is of slice 1, and the engine must support every one of them. A text that the engine does not support is not run in SQLite, which would change its database; SQLite prepares it on an empty database, and must give no syntax error, since a text that is not supported is one that SQLite accepts. The one difference from SQLite that the test accepts is that of [a syntax error after the start of a table that exists](#errors). Each call has the arguments that the generator gives. In the mixed profile, one value in three of an insert is a `?` with the value as its argument, an `int64`, a `float64`, a `string`, a `[]byte`, or nil for NULL, and one call in four of a statement with a parameter, and one in twenty of one without, has the cases around it, as [`docs/todo/slice-02.md`](../../docs/todo/slice-02.md) lists them. Among these cases are an `int`, an `int32`, a `uint64`, and a `float32` in place of the argument, and the arguments that no column holds: a `bool`, a `time.Time`, a `uint64` above the largest `int64`, NaN, an infinity, text that holds a NUL or is not valid UTF-8, and a nil `[]byte`. A nil argument is NULL since slice 6, so the engine takes it, and its call runs in SQLite. One of the cases around it puts a nil `[]byte` in its place, which the engine refuses. A call that the engine refuses for its arguments is not run in SQLite, which binds NULL to a missing argument and binds every type; the test checks it against s4ql's rule instead, with the exact message: the error of the number of arguments, or "not supported" for the first argument that no column holds. Half of the sequences, the third and the fourth of every four of a profile (`variantOf`), run with the length limit of the cache and of SQLite (`sqlite3_limit`) lowered to 1,000 bytes, `stmttest.LengthLimit`. An argument that is too long, and a row whose record is too long, are compared with SQLite. An insert with a literal that is too long is not run in SQLite, and must give `string or blob too big` after the names (`pair.checkLength`, which takes the longest literal of the insert). An update or a delete with a literal that is too long, in SET or in WHERE, is not run in SQLite either. It must give the same error after the names, whether it finds a row or not, and the test counts it as `excusedLongChange`. SQLite gives that error only when it changes a row, and turns a blob literal into NULL, which is the known difference of a long literal, for SET, in [the todo](../../docs/todo/slice-08.md). An insert that SQLite prepares, with a value, a literal or an argument, of another type than its column, is not run in SQLite either, and is checked against the rule of [Types](#types). `typeRule` gives that rule, and the simulation shares it. It reads the columns of the table from SQLite, in the order of the table (`oracle.columns`), and asks `stmttest.TypeRule`, which the test of the driver shares. The rule is "not supported" for the first column, in the order of the table, whose value is of another type, with the names and the type of that column from SQLite. NULL is of no other type than any column. The rule decides nothing for an insert that gives NULL to a column that does not take it, the key among them, or leaves out such a column whose default is NULL or none: SQLite gives `NOT NULL constraint failed` first, so such an insert runs in SQLite. `oracle.columns` reads from `pragma_table_info` whether a column takes NULL: its `notnull` is 0, and it is not the key. It reads the default of a column there too, as `dflt_value` gives it, into `stmttest.TableColumn.Default`. `TestTypeRule` in `stmttest` pins the rule, the faults of `stmttest.Faults` for NULL, which are "NULL in the key" and "NULL in a column that does not take it", and the cases of `stmttest.NullCases`. The rule also decides nothing in a read-only transaction, where SQLite and the engine refuse the write first, nor for an argument that is too long, which SQLite refuses when it binds it. For a select with WHERE, `typeRule` asks `stmttest.ConditionRule`: "not supported" for the first condition, in the order of the text, whose value is not NULL and is of another type than its column. That rule decides nothing where SQLite's error of a name comes first, as `stmttest.MissingColumn` gives it by SQLite's walk, or where the number of arguments differs. It holds in a read-only transaction too, where a select reads (`pair.checkType`), and `TestConditionRule` in `stmttest` pins it. For an update, `typeRule` asks `stmttest.UpdateRule`: "not supported" for the first condition of its WHERE whose value is of another type than its column, and then for the first column of its SET, in the order of the table, whose value is. That rule decides nothing where SQLite's error of a name comes first, nor where SET gives NULL to a column that does not take it and the update finds a row, because SQLite gives `NOT NULL constraint failed` first then. The rule asks SQLite whether the update finds a row, with the select of `stmttest.FoundQuery`. For a delete, `typeRule` asks `stmttest.DeleteRule`, which is the rule of a select with the same WHERE. In a read-only transaction `readOnlyRule` takes the place of `typeRule`: it decides nothing for an insert, and for an update it is the rule of the WHERE alone (`stmttest.WhereRule`), because the write is refused before a value of SET is looked at. `TestUpdateRule` in `stmttest` pins the rules of an update and of a delete. A select with a literal in a condition that is longer than the length limit is not checked against that rule, because s4ql gives `string or blob too big` before it looks at the type of a value (`pair.longCondition`). It runs in SQLite, which mostly gives the same. Where SQLite gives no error, which is [the known difference](../../docs/todo/sql-front-end.md), the engine must give `string or blob too big`, and the test excuses the select, and counts it as `excusedLongCondition`. An update that the engine refuses for a record that is too long, and that SQLite runs without that error, is excused as `excusedRecordSlip` only in one shape: it finds two rows or more, and when the rows change one at a time in SQLite, in the order of the key, the first error is `string or blob too big` (`pair.recordSlips`, which `TestPair_recordSlips` pins). SQLite does not always measure the record of a row after the first of a statement, which is [a known difference](../../docs/todo/sql-front-end.md) that depends on the platform, so the count of this excuse is not pinned. A create table that the engine refuses because its statement in the cache is longer than the length limit is excused as `excusedCacheLimit`, also in one shape only (`pair.tooLongForCache`): the message names the table of the statement, SQLite takes the text, in a savepoint that is rolled back, and `cacheStatementLength`, a second writing of the core's statement in the tests, is longer than the limit. `TestPair_tooLongForCache` pins that measure at the limit against the core, byte for byte, for every kind of default. No create table of the sequences comes near the limit, so the count of this excuse is pinned at 0. Every other call runs in SQLite with the same arguments bound, each by its type, so that an argument of another type than its column meets SQLite's errors of the names as the engine does. A run with the default flags, and without `-short`, the race detector, or the address sanitizer, pins how many texts each profile excuses from the comparison, by kind, in the rows with `=` of `testdata/reached/TestDifferential.tsv`. On 2026-11-21 the ten profiles together excuse 17 texts of a table that exists, none of the known gaps of `stmt`, 869 calls with the wrong number of arguments, 404 with an argument that no column holds, 1,375 with a value of another type than its column, 16 literals of an insert longer than the length limit, 32 updates and deletes with a literal longer than it, 16 literals of a condition longer than it for which SQLite gives no error, and no create table whose statement in the cache is longer than the limit, of 31,680 statements. The one sequence before the profiles gave 26, 817, 263, 540, 2, 2, and 2 of 14,400 statements, and every slice that added a draw to it had moved each count. The test also requires an insert through a parameter that succeeds, that meets a key that exists, and that the rule refuses, and each error of the names of slice 5: `duplicate column name`, `no such column`, `NOT NULL constraint failed`, and the two errors of the number of values. Every second sequence of a profile restarts the instance at random points, with an empty cache, which replays the log, or with its own cache, so each profile runs with and without restarts at both length limits. After each restart and at the end, the test compares every table and every row. The text of a state has ` NULL` after the type of a column that takes NULL, on both sides: `oracle.state`, `instance.state`, `coreState`, and the state of the database in memory print it. So every comparison of tables, also after a replay, checks which columns take NULL.

   Every comparison of rows with SQLite is a comparison of multisets. SQLite leaves the order of the rows of a select without `order by` unspecified, and the engine varies it from one query to the next; see [The order of the rows](#the-order-of-the-rows). So `answer.String`, which every comparison of two answers goes through, gives the rows sorted by their texts, each row in quotes. Two answers have one text only if they hold the same rows the same number of times. A row that is wrong changes an element of the sorted list. A row that is missing, one too many, or there twice changes the length of the list or another element. So the sorting hides no fault. `answer.rows` keeps the order in which the rows came, for the tests that check the order. The pinned cases of slice 13 compare a select with ORDER BY in its order, with `ordered.differs`; see `TestEngine_orderBy` below.

   A sequence comes from `stmttest.NewSequence`, and is a function of its seed alone. The seed names one of thirteen profiles (`stmttest.ProfileOf`): `mixed`, in which every area of the generator is on, and twelve that each weigh one feature area, which are `tables`, `where`, `changes`, `indexes`, `exists`, `defaults`, `valuefirst`, `transactions`, `arguments`, `order`, `returning`, and `subquery`. Each area draws from a random stream of its own, so a change of one area moves no statement of another. [The README of `stmttest`](../sql/stmt/stmttest/README.md) has the profiles, the streams, and the steps to add an area. What follows describes a sequence of the mixed profile; another profile changes the weights of the statements and the odds, and turns areas off. The statements of a sequence meet at one or two tables and at four or more names of columns, each in several spellings: some that SQLite takes for the same name, and some that it takes for another. Each name of a table has a shape of its own: one to three columns with names that differ, each of one of the five types, and the primary key at any position. A column after the first takes the type of an earlier one half of the time, so that two columns of one type are common. Of the columns outside the key, none takes NULL in a third of the shapes of more than one column, every one does in a third, and each does one time in two in the last third. Nine create tables in ten declare the shape. One in ten of these gives one column any type, and one in ten turns whether one column takes NULL, which changes nothing for the key. The tenth has one to three columns at random, whose names can repeat, which SQLite refuses, and each of them outside the key takes NULL one time in two. The sequence writes NULL since the generated checks of slice 6, through its hooks: it leaves `Generator.Nulls` at 0.

   The shapes have defaults since the generated checks of slice 11, of 2026-11-21. Of all the columns of a shape, none has a default in a third of the shapes, every one has in a third, and each has one time in two in the last third. A default is NULL one time in six, also on the key and on a column that does not take NULL. For a TEXT or a BLOB column it is long one time in three: a text of about a third of `stmttest.LengthLimit`, or a blob of a fifth. Otherwise it is a value as an insert gives the column. One create table in ten gives one column another default or none. The sequence writes the defaults through its hook `Generator.Create`, and leaves `Generator.Defaults` at 0.

   An insert has a list of columns two times in three, and two lists in three are shuffled. Without a list, its values are in the order of the shape. Each value has the type of its column, or is NULL one time in four for a column that takes NULL. A list also leaves a column that takes NULL out one time in three, so that the column gets NULL. It leaves out a column with a default one time in two, and the key with a default one time in four, so that the column gets its default. A list that leaves out a long default gives its first TEXT or BLOB column a value of 85 hundredths of the limit one time in two, so that the default makes the record too long. The faults come on top, each drawn on its own, so that some inserts have two. One list in eight leaves a column out. One list in twelve has another name of the sequence in the place of one of its own, which the table may not have. One insert in sixteen has a value more, and one in sixteen a value less. One insert in ten has a value of any type in the place of one of its values, and a third of these a second. One insert in twelve has NULL in the place of one of its values, also for the key. A select is `*` half of the time. Otherwise it has one to four names of columns of the shape, of which one in four after the first repeats an earlier one, and one in twelve is any name of a column of the sequence.

   One select in two has a WHERE, since the generated checks of slice 7. The sequence writes it through the hook `Generator.Where`, which is `Sequence.where`, and leaves `Generator.Conditions` at 0. One WHERE in five is a range of the key: a lower and an upper bound, each open or closed, in either order, with none to two more conditions. If the value of the lower bound is above that of the upper one, the two change places three times in four, and the fourth time the range holds no key. The other WHERE have one or two conditions, or four to seven one time in eight. The column of a condition is that of an earlier condition one time in four, so that a column is often tested twice. One time in ten it is a name that the shape does not have, which is in double quotes in some spellings. Otherwise it is the key one time in three, and any column of the shape else. The operator is IS NULL one time in twelve, IS NOT NULL one time in twelve, and otherwise one of the six comparisons. One WHERE in eight gets one more condition, the inverse of one of its own, such as `<` for `>=` with the same value, which contradicts it.

   A fault of the use of the key that drops a row shows only where the value of a condition meets a key that is stored. So most values of the conditions come from the rows (`Sequence.conditionValue`). Two times in five, the value is one that an insert of the sequence gave the column. `Sequence.store` keeps the last twelve of these for each column, whether SQLite took the insert or not. One time in eight, the value is a neighbour of such a value (`neighbour`): the next integer or real above or below, or, for text and a blob, a prefix, the least value above, or the value with its last character or byte one more or one less. One time in ten, it is an edge of the type (`edgeOf`), such as a limit of 64 bits, -0.0, the largest real, the empty text, or one byte of 255. One time in twenty it is NULL, and one time in twenty a value of another type than the column. Otherwise it is a value as an insert gets it. One value in three of a condition is a `?` with the value as its argument, as in an insert, so NULL comes as a literal and as a nil argument, and the selects get the cases around their arguments too.

   The sequence writes updates and deletes since the generated checks of slice 8. It writes them through the hooks `Generator.Update` and `Generator.Delete`, which are `Sequence.update` and `Sequence.delete`, and leaves `Generator.Changes` at 0. Of every 29 statements that a sequence of the mixed profile writes itself, 4 are a create table, 10 an insert, 5 a select, 1 a drop table, 3 an update, 2 a delete, 3 a create index, and 1 a drop index (`Profile.Weights`). An update sets from one to all of the columns of the shape, each once, in any order. One update in two of two or more columns leaves the key out. One update in twelve has a name that the shape does not have in place of one of its columns. Each value is NULL one time in ten, also for the key and for a column that does not take NULL. Of the other values, one in four of a REAL column is 0.0 or -0.0, and one in twelve of a TEXT or a BLOB column is about `stmttest.LengthLimit` bytes long, so that the record of a row is too long now and then. Every other value is drawn as `Sequence.conditionValue` draws one for a condition on the column, so a change of the key meets a key that exists and one that does not. `Sequence.store` keeps the values of an update for the conditions, as it keeps those of an insert. Three updates in four and four deletes in five have a WHERE, from `Sequence.changeConditions`. Most WHERE of a select find no row in a table of a few rows. So one WHERE in two of a change is one condition on the key with a value that the sequence gave it: that the key is the value, or, for every third of the values that the sequence keeps, that the key is on one side of it. The others are those of a select.

   One comparison in three of a WHERE has its value on the left, since the generated checks of slice 12, of 2026-11-21: in a select, an update, and a delete, the conditions of the hooks too. `stmttest.Sequence` sets `Generator.ValueFirst` to `Profile.ValueFirst` for it, which is 3 in the mixed profile and 2 in the profile `valuefirst`. The generator writes such a comparison with its operator turned round and its column on the right, and gives the condition with `ValueFirst`.

   Three values in four come from a small set of their type, so keys repeat, and the sets hold keys that SQLite takes for one, 0.0 and -0.0. The fourth comes from `stmttest.ValueOf`, with weight on the edges of its type. One text or blob in 16 is about 1,000 bytes long, at the lowered length limit or within a few bytes of it. One statement in twelve is a text that is not supported, a syntax error, or no statement. Some queries run through `Exec`, and some other statements through `Query`.

   Every select with WHERE is read both ways, and every update and delete with WHERE finds its rows both ways. A `pair` has a second engine, `pair.other`: a second instance of the kind of its instance, in memory or on the core, with the same length limit. After `pair.step` has run a call, `pair.bothWays` runs the same call on the second engine by a path that it forces, with `Engine.SetPath` of `export_test.go`, where the first engine reads as the rules choose. The calls take the paths of `otherPaths` in turn: every row forward, every row backward, the primary key forward, and the primary key backward, and then each index of the table of the call, forward and backward. The two engines must give the same answer, with the rows as a multiset, the same message, and the same count of changes. After an update or a delete with WHERE, a select of `*` of its table must give the same rows on both. An update or a delete cannot run twice on one engine, because it changes what a second run would find. Before the generated checks of slice 8, `pair.bothWays` ran a select a second time on the same engine. The second engine runs every call, so it holds what the first holds, also in transactions, which `txDifferential` and `FuzzChanges` begin and end on both. At the end of each sequence and of each transaction, the tables of both engines are compared with SQLite. Every test that goes through `pair.step` therefore compares both ways of reading rows with SQLite, without a flag: `TestDifferential`, `TestConn_differential`, in transactions too, and the fuzz targets below. With the flag `-scanall`, the first engine reads every row, and the second reads as the rules choose.

   The comparison of the rows cannot tell a plan that reads every row from one that reads by the key. So `reachWhere` also checks the access of each select with WHERE against [the rules](#the-rules). `stmttest.KeyAccess` gives the rules for a table without an index a second time, apart from the engine's `chooseAccess`, from the columns of the table in SQLite, the conditions, and the arguments. `engine.AccessOf` in `export_test.go` gives the access that `buildQuery` gives the scan, on a reader of the one table, without an index, that reads no rows. The text of that access, as `Access.String` gives it, must be the text of the rule, so a plan that reads more rows than the rule says fails. `engine.AccessOf` knows an update and a delete too. It builds their sink with `buildSink` on the same table, which reads and writes no rows, and gives the access of the scan that finds the rows. `reachChange` compares that access with `stmttest.KeyAccess` of the select of the same conditions, which `stmttest.SelectOf` gives with its parameters numbered from 1. The check runs where a test counts what it reaches, which is in `TestDifferential` and `TestConn_differential`.

   `pair.reach` counts what the statements reach, under "reached: " in the tally. For an insert, it counts every argument by its kind, as `stmttest.ArgumentKind` names it; every literal by its kind, as `stmttest.LiteralKind` names it, which is integer, real, real -0.0, text, empty text, blob, empty blob, or null; each insert with a text or a blob of more than 900 bytes; each insert that SQLite prepares, by the type of the key column and its outcome; -0.0 in the key that meets a key that exists; and the outcome by what the insert reaches of NULL, as `stmttest.NullCases` names it. These cases are each NULL, as a literal or as an argument, into the key, into a column that takes NULL, or into one that does not; an insert that leaves out a column that takes NULL; and NULL beside a value of another type. It counts the outcome by what the insert reaches of defaults too, as `stmttest.DefaultCases` names it. These cases are an insert that leaves out the key with a default, a column with a default of each type, the key or a column with DEFAULT NULL, by whether the column takes NULL, and a column with a long default; and NULL given for a column with a default, by whether the column takes NULL. For an insert into a table of several columns, it also counts the outcome by the form of the insert, which is without a list, with a list in the order of the table, or with a list in another order, and by its faults, as `stmttest.Faults` names them: a column that the table does not have, the wrong number of values, a column without a value, NULL in the key, NULL in a column that does not take it, a value of another type in the key, and a value of another type in another column. An insert with more than one fault is counted as "two faults". For a create table that SQLite prepares, it counts the outcome by the number of columns and by the position of the key, for a table of mixed types and one with two columns of a type, and for a table of several columns by whether none, all, or some of the columns outside the key take NULL. For a select of a table that SQLite has, it counts the outcome by the form: `*` by the number of columns, names, names in another order than the table's, a name twice, and a name that the table does not have, as written and in double quotes. It also counts a select of `*` and one of names with NULL among the values of its rows. A select of such a name in double quotes is compared with the pinned build of SQLite here, which the test of the driver cannot do with mattn's build; the default run requires it.

   For a select with WHERE, `reachWhere` counts the outcome by each case of `stmttest.WhereCases`. These cases are each operator on the key or on another column; the value of each comparison by its kind, as a literal or as an argument; a column that the table does not have, in a comparison or under IS NULL or IS NOT NULL, alone, before other conditions, or after them, and such a column in double quotes; a value of another type; a column that is tested more than once; contradicting conditions; and how the plan reads the rows, as `stmttest.KeyAccess` gives it. The plan reads no row, a get of one key, a scan from a lower bound, a scan to an upper bound, a scan between two bounds, also two bounds that hold no key, or a scan of every row. `reachWhere` counts a select whose arguments the engine refuses apart, for their number or for an argument that no column holds. For a query that succeeds on a table with rows, it counts whether the WHERE gives none, some, or all of them. `pair.reach` counts a select with a literal in a condition longer than the length limit, and `pair.bothWays` each select that it reads both ways, and those of them in a read-only transaction.

   For an update and a delete of a table that SQLite has, `reachChange` counts the statement by its kind, with or without WHERE, and by its outcome. An outcome "ok" is "ok, rows changed" or "ok, no row found", by the count of changes. It counts apart a statement whose arguments the engine refuses. For an update, it counts each case of `stmttest.SetCases`: a column that the table does not have, also in double quotes; the key; every column; NULL for a column that does not take it; each kind of value; and a value of another type. For a WHERE, it counts the cases of `stmttest.WhereCases` of the WHERE as a whole. For a statement that succeeds on a table with rows, it counts whether the statement changes none, some, or all of them. `pair.reach` counts an update of a row whose record is too long, and an update or a delete with a literal longer than the length limit, in SET or in WHERE. `pair.bothWays` counts each update and delete with WHERE that it changes both ways, and those of them in a read-only transaction.

   The full run with the default flags requires each kind of `testdata/reached/TestDifferential.kinds`: for each of the five types an insert that succeeds, one that meets a key that exists, and one of a value of another type; -0.0 that meets a key that exists, as a literal and as an argument; `string or blob too big` for TEXT and for BLOB; and every kind of literal and of argument. For slice 5 it requires create tables of one, two, and three columns, with the key at each position, of mixed types, and with two columns of a type; inserts of each form that succeed; inserts with a list in another order that meet a key that exists, a value of another type, and a column without a value; inserts with each fault, and with two; and selects of each form. For slice 6 it requires create tables of which no column, every column, and some columns outside the key take NULL; NULL as a literal and as an argument in a column that takes it, also with a key that exists and beside a value of another type; NULL as a literal and as an argument in the key and in a column that does not take it; inserts that leave out a column that takes NULL; NULL beside a value of another type in another column; and selects of `*` and of names with NULL in their rows. For slice 7 it requires each of the eight operators on the key and on another column; each comparison on the key with a value of another type; each way that the plan reads rows, also two bounds that hold no key; a WHERE that gives no row, some rows, and every row of a table with rows; each kind of value as a literal and as an argument, with NULL as both; each fault of a WHERE, which is a column that the table does not have, in each place and in double quotes, a value of another type, an argument that no column holds, the wrong number of arguments, a column tested more than once, and contradicting conditions; a literal of a condition longer than the length limit; and the selects that are read both ways. For slice 8 it requires updates and deletes, with and without WHERE, that change rows and that find none; updates and deletes that change every row, some rows, and no row of a table with rows; a change of the key that succeeds, that meets a key that exists, and that gets NULL; NULL for a column that does not take it, with a row found and without; a column of SET that the table does not have, also in double quotes; a value of another type in SET and in WHERE; each kind of literal in SET, and an integer, a real, text, a blob, and NULL as an argument there; a column of WHERE that the table does not have; `NOT NULL constraint failed` and `UNIQUE constraint failed` with and without WHERE; a record that is too long; a literal of SET longer than the length limit; arguments that s4ql refuses; each way that the scan reads the rows; and the updates and deletes that are changed both ways. For slice 11 it requires create tables with a default of each type, with a long one, with one on the key, and with DEFAULT NULL on a column that takes NULL and on one that does not; inserts that leave a column out and store its default of each type, also that of the key, and NULL from DEFAULT NULL in a column that takes it; `NOT NULL constraint failed` from DEFAULT NULL in a column that does not take NULL and in the key; `UNIQUE constraint failed` from the default of the key; a record that a long default makes too long; and NULL given for a column with a default, which is kept where the column takes NULL and refused where it does not. For slice 12 it requires each of the six comparisons with its value on the left in a statement that finds rows, in one that finds none, and with each refusal, which is a value of another type, a column that the table does not have, the wrong number of arguments, and an argument that no column holds; NULL on the left; a value on the left that is too long; `NOT NULL constraint failed` and `UNIQUE constraint failed` of an update with a value on the left; a select, an update, and a delete with one that find rows and that find none; the choices of an access that a value on the left decides; and the WHERE with one that are run by every path, with their paths that a range bounds. A profile must reach a kind at least a third as often as when the test last counted, so that the test fails when the generator stops reaching a case. `testdata/reached/TestDifferential.tsv` has a row for each kind in each profile that reached it three times or more, with the count and the least number, a third of it, so no least number is 0. A kind that no profile reaches three times fails the test. The profiles together must reach each kind at least as often as the number before it in `TestDifferential.kinds`, which only a builder writes, so that a floor falls only with a change of that file; the numbers started from the least numbers of `reachedDifferential`, below. A builder writes the file of the kinds, and the test writes the file of the counts with `S4QL_RECOUNT=1` in a full run (`stmttest.Reached`), so a recount is a command, and its result a diff. Before 2026-11-21 the least numbers were a map in the test, `reachedDifferential`, over 300 sequences that each wrote every feature; slices 8 and 12 had lowered five of them and dropped one, a delete by a scan between two bounds that changes rows, which the run requires again. The run also requires one literal longer than the length limit.
2. **Fuzzing.** `FuzzSQL` gives the engine any SQL text, on a database with a fixed set-up of tables, some with rows, with names in different cases and in quotes (`fuzzSetUp`), and gives SQLite the same text on the same tables. It compares them as the differential test does: the error as code, extended code, and message, or the columns and the rows; the count of changes; and the tables after the text. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzSQL_core` runs the same on the core, which is about 45 times slower. Their seeds are statements of a sequence of the mixed profile, the texts of `stmttest.Others`, and every 500th text of the corpus. Beside the difference of a table that exists, they excuse the known gaps of `stmt` as its tests do, and count them apart: a syntax error where an error of a grammar action of SQLite came first, and a syntax error where SQLite gives an error about the database first, such as `no such table: main.X` for a trigger.

   `FuzzDifferential` explores seeds of the generator of the differential test, on the database in memory: its input is a seed, which names its profile, and a length, not SQL text, so the guidance of the fuzzer by coverage has little to work with, and its count of executions is not a measure of the SQL text that it covers. `FuzzDifferential_core` runs such a sequence on the core, with or without restarts, and with or without the lowered length limit.

   `FuzzTypes` gives the engine the bytes of its input as the value of an insert into a table of each of the five types, in the form that its input chooses: a text, blob, real, or integer argument, a string literal, a blob literal in lower or upper case, or SQL text in the place of the value. For each type, the value is the key of a table of one column, and the first column of two tables of two, whose second column is an INT key that the insert sets to 1 and names first in its list. In one of the two, `t_<type>_n`, the first column takes NULL. The form `formNull` gives NULL in place of the bytes, by their length: as a nil argument for a length of 1 modulo 3, by leaving the first column out of the list of a table of two for 2, and otherwise as the literal. Each insert runs twice, so that the second meets the key of the first, and a query reads each table; `pair.step` compares each call with SQLite, by the rules for arguments, types, and lengths of the differential test. Since slice 11 it then creates a table `t_<type>_d` of each type whose column has the value of the input as its DEFAULT, written as a literal (`typesLiteral`), inserts a row that leaves the column out and one that gives it NULL, and reads the table. The tables are compared at the end. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzTypes_core` runs the same on the core, at a length limit from 1001 to 1255 bytes or SQLite's default, and replays the log into an empty cache and compares the tables again. Their seeds are 27 values at the edges of the types in every form, and values of 995 to 1002 bytes at the limit of 1001.

   `FuzzWhere` builds the WHERE of a select from the bytes of its input. The first byte chooses a table `w_<type>` of one of the five types, with a key `k` and a column `v` that takes NULL. The rows have keys at the edges of the type. One row in three has `v` NULL, and the others have `v` as the key. `whereOf` gives the select up to eight conditions, each on `k`, on `v`, or on `x`, which the table does not have, also in double quotes, and each with one of the eight operators in any of its spellings. The value of a comparison is the key of a row, a value of the type from the bytes that follow, NULL, or a value of another type, as a literal or as an argument. Since slice 12, a comparison has its value on the left of its column, with the operator turned round, when the highest bit of the byte of its operator is set (`fuzzBytes.typedConditions`). The byte chooses the operator as before. `pair.step` compares the select with SQLite. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzWhere_core` runs the same on the core. Their seeds have every operator on `k` and on `v` with each kind of value, a column that the table does not have, several conditions, and each operator of a comparison with its value on the left.
