   `FuzzChanges` builds updates and deletes from the bytes of its input. The first byte chooses a table `c_<type>` of one of the five types, with a key `k`, a column `v` that takes NULL, and a column `w` that does not. The rows are those of `FuzzWhere`, and `w` holds the value of the key. The second byte chooses whether the statements run on their own or in a write transaction, and whether that transaction commits or rolls back. `changesOf` builds up to four updates and deletes from the other bytes. An update sets the columns of a mask, which are `k`, `v`, `w`, and `x`, which the table does not have, in either order and also in double quotes. A WHERE has up to three conditions on the same four names. The values and the conditions are as `FuzzWhere` builds them, through the reader `fuzzBytes`, which `whereOf` uses too, so a comparison has its value on the left by the same bit. `pair.step` compares each call with SQLite and runs it both ways, and the tables of both engines are compared with SQLite at the end. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzChanges_core` runs the same on the core, and replays the log into an empty cache and compares the tables again. Their seeds have updates of each column and of several, also of `x`, with each kind of value as a literal and as an argument, with and without WHERE; deletes with and without WHERE, of one row and of many; a change of the key that meets a key that exists; and several statements in a transaction.

   `FuzzCreateIndex_core` creates an index under the lowered length limit `stmttest.LengthLimit`, on the storage core and in SQLite. Its input chooses the length of the name of the index, the length of the names of its columns, from one to four columns, their direction, double quotes around the name, and what follows the statement. The engine must refuse with `string or blob too big` exactly the statements that SQLite refuses, and hold the indexes that SQLite takes, also after a restart on the same cache and after a replay of the log into an empty cache. Its seeds are a length at which the cache needs SQLite's largest limit to hold the index, a length at which SQLite refuses a text far below the limit, a text longer than the limit, and the longest name that SQLite takes on a column of one byte with the name one byte longer.

   `FuzzColumns` builds a table of several columns, and the inserts and selects on it, from the bytes of its input. The table `t` has two to four columns, `c0` to `c3`, each of one of the five types, with the key at any position. Each column outside the key takes NULL or says NOT NULL, the key says NOT NULL before PRIMARY KEY, after it, or not at all, and the two options of the table come in either order. Up to 16 inserts and selects follow, each of `t` or of `T`, and then a select of every column. An insert has no list, a list in the order of the table, a shuffled list, or a shuffled part of the columns, with names also in upper case and in double quotes. It has no fault or one of four: a value more, a value fewer, the name `x`, which the table does not have, in the list, or a name twice in it. `columnsValue` gives each value, as a literal or as an argument: one of four values of the type, so that keys meet, a value from the bytes that follow, NULL, a value of another type, a text or a blob of up to 1275 bytes, a value at an edge of the type, or an argument that no column holds. A select is of `*` or of one to four names, in the same spellings and with `x` among them, and has a WHERE of up to three conditions, each on a column or on `x`, with one of the eight operators in any of its spellings. `pair.step` compares each call with SQLite, by the rules of the differential test, so it reads a select with WHERE both ways, and the tables are compared at the end. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzColumns_core` runs the same on the core, at a length limit from 1001 to 1255 bytes or SQLite's default, and replays the log into an empty cache and compares the tables again. Their seeds are built by hand for five shapes of table. For each shape, after four rows, they have each kind of list, NULL in a column that takes it and in one that does not, each fault, a value of another type in each column, each kind of select, and a WHERE on each column. For the four shapes with a TEXT or a BLOB column, they also have values of 800, 1000, and 1005 bytes at the limit of 1001, where a value or a record of several is too long. Beside these are 40 inputs of random bytes from a fixed seed, half of them at that limit.

   `FuzzIndexes` builds a table with indexes, and the statements on it, from the bytes of its input. The first byte chooses a table of one to three columns of the five types, with the key at any position and some columns outside the key taking NULL. The columns are `c0` to `c2` in the table `t`, or `queue`, `priority`, and `created` in the table `goqite`. One to three indexes follow, each of one or more columns, ascending or descending, with the primary key among them or without it, and created before the rows or after them. The rows are as many as `whereRows` has for the type of the key, and pairs of rows share values, so that a pin finds more than one row. Up to 16 statements follow. They are selects with a WHERE of conditions on any column; selects that pin and bound the key of an index, or the primary key if the table has no index, with `=`, IS NULL, and ranges, and now and then with NULL or a value of another type; inserts; updates; deletes; a drop of an index while the table has rows; and a create of an index, either again of the last one dropped or a new one. Three bits of the byte of a drop or a create give the statements of slice 10 (`indexesCalls`). With the bit 4, the statement says IF EXISTS or IF NOT EXISTS. With the bit 16, a drop is of the table, and a create makes the table again, of its columns. With the bit 8, the statement names something else: a drop of an index names an index that is not there, or the table; a drop of the table names an index, or a name that is not there; and a create of an index names an index that is there, with new columns. Since slice 12, the selects of conditions on any column write a comparison with its value on the left as `FuzzWhere` does. In the selects that pin and bound a key (`fuzzBytes.pins`), a pin has its value on the left with the bit 2 of its byte, and the lower and the upper bound have theirs there with the bits 16 and 32 of the byte of the bounds. `pair.step` compares each call with SQLite. The pair has `allPaths` set, so `pair.everyPath` runs each WHERE by every candidate path, where the generated tests run a sample of four, and compares the rows as a multiset and in the order of the path's key. The pair has a tally, so `checkChoice` checks the engine's choice of a path against the model `stmttest.ChosenAccess`, and the access that the engine chooses for the table without its indexes against `stmttest.KeyAccess`. At the end, the tables and their indexes in both engines are compared with SQLite. It runs on the database in memory, and `FuzzIndexes_core` runs the same on the core, and replays the log into an empty cache, compares again, and runs the selects again, which read by the indexes that the replay built. Their seeds are built by hand: pins of one, two, and three columns, a pin of NULL, ranges on a descending column, indexes that hold the primary key, a drop of an index with rows and selects after it, a create of the index again, the table `goqite` with its index on `(queue, priority desc, created)`, and, for each type, a table of one column and one of three. One seed of `indexesSeeds` has the four statements of slice 10, where they do their work and where they do nothing. Two seeds for each type have values on the left, of slice 12: pins, ranges, and a select and a delete of a comparison. `TestFuzzIndexes_seeds` runs the seeds, and fails if they stop reaching each of these kinds, those of slice 10 among them, as `reachExists` counts them, and the choices and the paths by a value on the left.

   Every fuzz target here runs its calls through `pair.step`, so each reads a select with WHERE both ways, and finds the rows of an update or a delete with WHERE both ways, as the differential test does. The others do not check the access against `stmttest.KeyAccess`, because that check runs only where a test counts what it reaches. `FuzzIndexes` and `FuzzIndexes_core` do, because their pair counts what it reaches.
3. **The simulation.** `TestSimulation` runs statements under the scheduler of [`internal/sim`](../sim/README.md) while the store fails, hangs, and loses requests. A simulation is an environment and a profile. The environment is a `workload` of the test: the instances, their callers, the crashes and closes, and the faults of the store. The profile is that of the seed, as in the differential test, so the statements of a seed are those of its sequence, whatever the scheduler does. There are three environments, the default, the idle, and the hammer workload of the results below. The default environment has two instances that compete for the lease, two callers on each, one crash, and one close. Each caller is a `client` with a connection of the engine of its own, which it replaces when its node has opened its database again. A caller whose statement is refused for want of the lease waits up to two minutes for its instance to serve, and stops if it does not, as a client behind a check of health would turn to another instance; without that, the callers of the instance that does not hold the lease made more than half of all statements, and each was refused. The crash takes an instance that runs a statement, if one does within ten seconds, so that it often cuts one. After a crash, a new instance starts on a copy of the cache or on an empty one. Every second engine of a node reads its rows another way than the rules choose (`run.open`): the first engine of a node reads as the rules choose, the second, which opens after a close or a stop, another way, and so on in turn. The other way is drawn from the seed of the engine, one engine in two of each: every row of a table in each query, or the path of one of the names of the indexes of the sequence (`engine.Path{Index: …}`), which reads by that index in a table that has an index of the name, and as the rules choose in another. The sequence of a profile without indexes has no such name, and the other way is then every row. A node rarely opens its database more than twice, so the way is drawn, and not taken from the number of the engine. So the checks of the queries against SQLite see three ways of reading rows, also across closes. The profiles `indexes` and `valuefirst` write more create indexes, and more WHERE that an index serves (`Profile.Indexes`). Each engine draws the directions of its queries from a seed of its own, which `run.open` derives from the seed of the run, the name of the node, and the number of the node's engine. It draws nothing from the host for them. So the draws of the scheduler are those of a run without the directions, and a replay of a seed reads in the same directions. The hammer environment has one instance with four callers that hardly wait, so that two statements often run at once. Its instance opens once, and reads as the rules choose. Each statement has a timeout, so a commit can end with its outcome unknown. Each statement carries an ID in a comment, which its annotation keeps, and its event gives the state that it saw.

   Each environment runs cells, each a profile and a number of its seeds (`simEnvironments`), and the first seed of each cell twice, whose traces must be the same. The default and the hammer environment run each of the thirteen profiles, and the idle one, whose kinds are those of the lease, runs five. Each environment takes the seeds of a profile from a number of its own on (`first`), which is 100,000, 200,000, or 300,000, so no two environments run the same sequences. The three run 288 seeds, 100 of them of `mixed`, two thirds of the 150 seeds before the profiles, since only `mixed` has every area on:

   | Environment | Seeds | Cells |
   | --- | --- | --- |
   | Default | 114 | 34 of `mixed`, 10 of `indexes`, 8 each of `changes` and `subquery`, and 6 each of `tables`, `where`, `exists`, `defaults`, `valuefirst`, `transactions`, `arguments`, `order`, and `returning` |
   | Idle | 69 | 33 of `mixed`, 12 of `transactions`, 10 of `indexes`, 8 of `changes`, and 6 of `exists` |
   | Hammer | 163 | 40 of `subquery`, 33 of `mixed`, 16 of `returning`, 10 each of `where`, `indexes`, and `valuefirst`, 8 of `tables`, and 6 each of `changes`, `exists`, `defaults`, `transactions`, `arguments`, and `order` |

   Before 2026-11-21, each environment ran the first 50 seeds of one sequence of every feature.

   In every environment, one turn of a caller in `Profile.Transactions` is a transaction that spans statements (`run.transaction`), on the caller's connection, which is one turn in four, and one in two in the profile `transactions`: a write transaction, or one time in four a read-only one, of two to six statements of the same sequence, with a wait of up to half a second before each. Its begin, each statement, and its end each have a timeout of up to the workload's, so a begin can give up its wait for the place of the writer, and a commit can end with its outcome unknown. It ends with a commit, or one time in four with a roll back. A statement that is refused for want of the lease or by a stop ends it early with a roll back, so these callers do not leave a transaction open. The idle environment adds the callers that do: it is the default one with one idler on each instance (`run.idler`), whose two write transactions each make their statements and then sit idle while the instance is cut off from the store, half of the time for 30 to 60 seconds, long enough for the other instance to take the lease. The first runs one more statement after up to a minute and ends. The second is cut off for two minutes always, sits idle for three, and stays open after the end of the workload; the write of the end runs in it (`run.endKept`), and `run.readsFree`, which closes each instance in turn once its connections have run their query, requires that an instance where it is still open serves within ten minutes, with a violation of its own, "is open and idle, does not serve". An instance that lost its lease meanwhile must have ended the transaction. The core logs the end of a transaction for the lease, and `run.verifyOverlaps` takes it for the end of the place of the writer of that transaction, because the core frees the place before the caller ends the transaction. The engine cannot plant the core's bug `keepIdleTx`, so the check was shown by hand on 2026-11-19: with `DB.endForLease` made to do nothing, 89 of the first 100 seeds of the idle workload failed, each by that violation. The 69 seeds of the idle environment end 99 transactions for the lease, 98 of them kept open, with no violation, and `TestSimulation` requires a third of both counts.

   `run.verify` checks each run against SQLite and the log:
   - Every commit object of the log holds the statements of one call. A statement of its own is of a call that succeeded, that had an unknown outcome, or whose instance crashed while it ran. A transaction that spans statements is a write transaction whose caller called its commit, and whose commit succeeded, had an unknown outcome, or did not return because its instance crashed; nothing of a transaction that was rolled back, or whose commit failed, is in the log. Its commit object holds exactly its statements that succeeded and that write, in order, and its parent is the state that its statements saw, so no commit landed while it was open. No statement and no transaction is in the log twice. The parameters of each statement are exactly the arguments that the caller gave, by position, kind, and value.
   - Every write that was committed is in the log: a statement of its own when it succeeded, and the writes of a transaction when its commit succeeded. A transaction whose net effect is nothing writes no commit object, and its writes must not be in the log: one whose writes are all of tables that SQLite does not have at its beginning nor at its end. A write transaction whose rows SQLite has at its end as at its beginning may leave no commit object either, as one that inserted a row and deleted it: the core commits nothing for a key whose row was not there before and is not there after. An update or a delete of its own that changed no row is in no commit object, and need not be in the log.
   - The statements of the log, run in SQLite in their order with their parameters, each succeed. A parameter that is NULL is bound as NULL (`oracleValues`).
   - Every value of every insert of the log has the type of its column in SQLite, where the insert runs, as `typeRule` says. An insert with a value that does not is the violation "stores a value of another type than its column". SQLite converts some such values without an error, so the replay of the log alone does not find them.
   - Every row that a commit of the log upserts fits its table (`run.verifyRows`). The log has the table at that commit. The rows are for every column of the table, in the order of the table. Each row has one value for each column, and each value has the type of its column, or is NULL in a column that takes NULL. A NULL in any other column is the violation "holds a row of the table … with NULL in the column …, which does not take NULL". The replay of the log in SQLite runs the statements of the log, and not its rows, so it does not see a row that holds the values of its statement in other columns.
   - A statement of its own that failed with SQLite's error gives that error in SQLite on the state that it saw. An insert that the engine refused for the type of its value is refused so by `typeRule` there. A query that succeeded gives its rows there, each value compared by its type. That state is the state after a commit of the log, and it holds every write that was committed before the statement started.
   - An update or a delete of its own that succeeded with a count of changes of 0 changes no row in SQLite on the state that it saw. Such a statement is in no commit object, so without this check only the engine's word says that it changed nothing. `run.verify` runs it in SQLite, in a savepoint that is rolled back, and requires no error and a count of changes of 0. A count above 0 is the violation "changes no row, is in no commit, and changes … rows in SQLite".
   - A statement of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that did nothing is in no commit object, and changes nothing in SQLite on the state that it saw. The simulation reads `statement.nothing` from the event of each statement into `attempt.nothing`. It keeps such a statement out of the committed writes and out of the writes of its transaction. A commit object that holds it is the violation "did nothing, and is in commit …". `run.verify` runs a statement of its own in SQLite, and `run.verifyTxs` one of a transaction, and SQLite's database must stay the same. If it changes, that is the violation "does nothing, … and changes SQLite's database". The run counts these checks as "statements that do nothing, compared with SQLite" and "statements that do nothing in a transaction, compared with SQLite".
   - The count of changes of each update and delete that succeeded is SQLite's: for a statement of its own by the run of the statement of its commit, and for a statement of a transaction by the run of the statements of the transaction (`run.verifyTxs`). Another count is the violation "changes … rows in SQLite, and its caller was told …".
   - The statements of a transaction that spans statements all saw one state, which holds every write that was committed before the begin (`run.verifyTxs`). SQLite runs the log up to that state, under `pragma query_only = 1` for a read-only transaction, and then each statement of the transaction that succeeded or failed with SQLite's error, in order, and each must give what the engine gave, a write that succeeded included: for a write transaction, the state of its beginning and its own writes so far; for a read-only one, the state of its beginning, whatever committed meanwhile. An insert of a write transaction that the engine refused for the type of its value is refused so by `typeRule` on SQLite's state at that statement. A read-only transaction must refuse no insert for its type, since it refuses the write, with `attempt to write a readonly database`, before it looks at the value, and an update only for a value of its WHERE (`readOnlyRule`). So no caller sees a write of a transaction that has not committed, of its own transaction excepted.
   - Two write transactions of one engine never overlap (`run.verifyOverlaps`): no write transaction's begin returns between the return of another's begin and the call of its end, and no statement of its own that writes both starts and commits in that time. The calls are ordered by a clock of events of the run, and an engine is one node's between two opens of its database.
   - A text that the engine does not support is one that SQLite prepares on an empty database without a syntax error, as in the differential test.
   - SQLite's integrity check, `pragma integrity_check`, finds no fault in the cache of the replay below, in the cache of a node after its crash, and in the cache of each live node at the end of the workload, once it is closed (`run.checkCache`). The check runs on a copy of the cache with its write-ahead log, as `copyCache` makes it, so it never opens the file that the next node starts on. It finds an index that does not hold what its table holds, which no comparison of rows sees.
   - A new instance with an empty cache replays the log to the state that SQLite has after it, with every value of every row.

   A commit of a transaction whose outcome is unknown may be in the log or not, as for a statement of its own. One query in four is not read to its end: its loop stops at the first row, it is closed unread, or its consumer panics. Its rows are not compared, and the rest of the run must still hold. A query that is read to its end and reads by an index, as `Rows.Access` says, has the index dropped while its rows are open, before their first row, by a statement of its own on a new connection of its engine (`run.dropUnder`), unless the query is of a write transaction, whose place of the writer the drop would wait for. Its rows are compared with SQLite on the state that the query saw, which holds the index. The callers run the calls of the differential test's generator with their arguments, and a call that the engine refuses for its arguments must be refused by the rule, as in the differential test. A refusal of an insert for the type of its value is checked by `typeRule` against SQLite on the state that the statement saw: in `run.verify` for a statement of its own, and in `run.verifyTxs` for a statement of a transaction. An insert of such a value that succeeds is found in the log, by the check of the type of each value there. A statement of slice 1 must not be refused as not supported. A statement that saw no state, because it failed before it had a transaction, is a syntax error, and must give what SQLite gives for it on an empty database.

   `TestSimulation` counts what the seeds of each cell reach, and what those of each environment reach together: the statements by how they ended (succeeded, SQLite's error, unknown outcome, cut by a crash, not supported or without a statement, and refused for want of the lease, by a stop, or by the timeout), the queries not read to their end, the inserts and the selects refused for the type of a value, the committed writes, the commits, the commits of more than one statement, the rows of tables of several columns in the log, the NULL values in the rows of the log, the selects with WHERE that the checks compare with SQLite, the updates and deletes, the statements in a transaction, the write and the read-only transactions, the transactions by how they ended (committed, of unknown outcome, not committed, rolled back, and not begun), the crashes followed by an empty cache and by a copy of the cache, the closes, and the faults that the store injects, by kind, from the lines of the trace that the store writes for each. For slice 9 it counts the create indexes and the drop indexes by outcome, with SQLite's errors by kind without their names (`errorKind`): `index … already exists`, `no such table`, `no such column`, `there is already a table named …`, `no such index`, and `attempt to write a readonly database`; the selects, updates, and deletes that read by an index, by the attribute `statement.access` of their event; the statements on an engine that forces an index path, and those of them that read by that index; the selects with WHERE that the checks compare with SQLite on an engine that forces an index path, and those that read by an index; the crashes followed by an empty cache whose log has an index at that time, and the replays of `run.verify` of a log with an index at its end (`indexesAtEnd`); the queries that read by an index whose index a drop index of their node dropped while their rows were open, and whose rows the checks compare with SQLite (`run.droppedUnder`); and the integrity checks after a crash, after the replay, and at the end. For slice 10, `tallyOf` counts the statements of IF NOT EXISTS or IF EXISTS that did nothing, that did their work, and that failed with SQLite's error, and the run counts those that did nothing and that the checks run in SQLite, of their own and in a transaction. For slice 11, `run.verifyRows` counts the create tables of the log with a default, and the values of its rows that are the default of their column, NULL apart, as "create tables with a default in the log" and "values of a default in the log". For slice 12, `tallyOf` counts the selects, updates, and deletes with a value on the left (`hasValueFirst`): all of them, those that found rows, those with SQLite's error, and those that read by the primary key or an index. For slice 14, `tallyOf` counts the inserts, updates, and deletes with RETURNING (`hasReturning`): all of them; those that gave rows, of their own, in a transaction, and in a transaction that was rolled back; those with SQLite's error; and those of their own through `Query` that failed in their write transaction or ended with an unknown outcome. The run counts those whose rows the checks compare, of their own with the log and in a transaction with SQLite.

   The kinds that the test requires are in `testdata/reached/TestSimulation.kinds`, and what the seeds reached of each at the last recount is in `TestSimulation.tsv`, as for the differential test. A run of that file is a cell, named as `default/indexes`, or an environment, named as `default`, whose counts are the sums of those of its cells. A cell and an environment must each reach each kind of their rows at least a third as often, so that the test fails when a kind stops happening, the environments together must reach each kind at least as often as the number before it in `TestSimulation.kinds`, which only a builder writes and which started from the sum of the least numbers that the three environments of 50 seeds had before the profiles, a kind needs a row in one run at least, and `S4QL_RECOUNT=1` writes the counts again. The three kinds of integrity checks of the caches are not in `TestSimulation.kinds`: their counts are pinned exactly (`Reached.Exact`), since every seed of an environment runs the same number of them. A cell that is added to an environment adds to what the environment reaches, and takes nothing from another cell. Before the profiles, a least number was a quarter of what the first 1,000 seeds of an environment gave, scaled to its run of 50 seeds, and 14 of them rounded to no occurrence and were dropped: one of the default environment, one of the hammer one, and 12 of the idle one. The default and the hammer environment have theirs again, and the idle one has 5 of its 12; it reaches the other 7 kinds fewer than three times, and another environment requires each of those. In every run but one under `-short`, the race detector, or the address sanitizer, at most the share `noLease` of the statements of an environment may be refused for want of the lease: 0.07 of them in the default environment, 0.2 in the idle one, and 0.06 in the hammer one, about twice the 0.034, 0.106, and 0.032 that their seeds refused on 2026-11-21. The first 1,000 seeds of the one sequence refused 0.08 and 0.07 in the default and the hammer environment before the workloads had transactions, and 0.04 and 0.03 with them. A caller that does not wait for its instance to serve took the share to 0.60 and 0.31, measured before the workloads had transactions. The seeds are the same in every run, so the counts are too.

   A run also fails in these cases: a statement gives an error that the workload does not expect, as an internal error is; an instance stops; a close of an instance does not end within five minutes; a close of a connection fails; no instance runs a write within ten minutes after the store has calmed; a connection of a caller of a live instance has a read transaction left open after the workload; or a live instance does not run a query on each connection of its callers within ten minutes after that.

   `run.readsFree` makes the last two checks. Every connection of the callers of a live instance must run a query of a table that does not exist, which gives SQLite's `no such table` once the query has its read transaction. A connection whose read transaction was left open fails the query with `core.ErrReaderBusy`, and that is a violation at once: "… has a connection of a caller with a read transaction left open after the end of the workload". An instance that has not run the queries within ten minutes is a violation too: "… does not run a query on each connection of its callers ten minutes after the end of the workload". Only the instance that holds the lease can read, so each instance whose connections have all run their query is closed, which hands the lease to the next.
4. **Planted bugs.** `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` plants each of forty-eight bugs in the engine, and runs seeds of one profile in one environment, which the table below names, until two have found it by its own violation, among the first 100 seeds of the profile. The profile is the one that weighs what the bug breaks. The default run found each of the first nineteen bugs of the table twice within 15 seeds; the three of slice 13, the three of slice 14, and the nine of slice 15 are described with the generated checks of their slices, below, the six of arithmetic under [The tests of arithmetic](#the-tests-of-arithmetic), and the eight of several statements under [Several statements](#several-statements). A seed finds a bug only with a violation that the bug causes: a commit that fails in SQLite for `skipUnique` and `checkOutside`, and for `skipUnique` also an insert of a write transaction that succeeds where SQLite fails, a committed statement that is not in the log for `ackUnknown`, no write after the workload, or an instance that does not run a query on each connection of its callers then, for `keepOpen`, whose open write transaction also keeps its instance from releasing the lease, for `keepRead` a connection with a read transaction left open after the workload, or a later query of the same connection that fails with "the reader has a read transaction open", and for `annotateNext`, which annotates each argument plus one, a commit whose parameters are not the arguments that its caller gave. Of the bugs of transactions, `keepFailed` is found by a commit object that holds other statements than its transaction's that succeeded and write, or a commit that fails in SQLite; `readNewest` by a statement of a read-only transaction that gives in SQLite another answer on the state of the beginning of its transaction, a read-only transaction whose statements saw more than one state, or an end of the transaction that fails with "the transaction has ended", because the transaction keeps the read that the bug ended when its read of the newest state fails; and `rollbackKeeps` by a transaction in the log that its caller rolled back. `convertReal` is found by an insert of the log that stores a value of another type than its column, or by a refusal for the type that is not the rule's: in a table of several columns, the real that the bug converts can be the first value of another type than its column, and the engine then refuses a later one, where the rule refuses the real. `listOrder` puts the values of a row in other columns than its statement names. It is found by a replay of the log into an empty cache that differs from SQLite, because the replay writes the rows and SQLite runs the statements; by a later insert of the log that fails in SQLite, or a statement that gives another answer than in SQLite, because the key of the row is another value; or by a refusal for the type where the rule refuses nothing, because a value is in a column of another type. No row of the log holds a value of another type than its column, because the engine's check of the type sees the values in the columns where the bug puts them. `zeroLeftOut` gives a column that takes NULL, has no default, and that the list of an insert leaves out, the zero value of its type. It is found by a query that gives the zero value where SQLite has NULL, or by a replay of the log into an empty cache that differs from SQLite, because the replay writes the rows and SQLite runs the statements. No check of a value that is not NULL finds it, because the zero value has the type of its column, and no column that takes NULL is the key. `nullDefault` gives a column with a default, which the list of an insert leaves out, NULL in place of its default. It is found by a query that gives NULL where SQLite has the default, or by the replay of the log, as `zeroLeftOut` is. Where the column does not take NULL, the engine gives `NOT NULL constraint failed` and SQLite stores the default, so a later statement can differ. `openBound` makes a scan between bounds of the key read a closed bound, of `<=` or `>=`, as open, so that the scan leaves out the row whose key is the bound, which the filter above it would give. It is found only by a select with WHERE, of its own or in a transaction, that gives fewer rows than in SQLite, because a select writes nothing and fails nothing. An engine that reads every row, as some engines of a node do, gives its scan no bound, and does not have the bug. `pinByPlace` makes the fit of the conditions to the key of an index, or of the primary key, pin a column of the key to the value of a condition on another column. It is found by the storage core's refusal of the scan, an internal error, where that value is of another type than the column or is NULL in the primary key; and otherwise by a statement that gives another answer or another count of changes than in SQLite, by a commit that fails in SQLite, or by a replay of the log that differs from SQLite. `changeNothing` makes an update and a delete find no row, change nothing, and report a count of changes of 0. It is found by an update or a delete of its own that is in no commit and changes rows in SQLite on the state that it saw; by an update or a delete in a transaction whose count of changes differs from SQLite's; or by a later statement that gives another answer than in SQLite, because the rows that it should have changed are as they were. `keepOldKey` makes an update that gives a row a new key leave the row of the old key. It is found by a replay of the log into an empty cache that differs from SQLite, because the commit object holds the new row and no delete of the old key; or by a later statement that gives another answer than in SQLite, because the table holds the row twice. `replaceExisting` makes a create table with IF NOT EXISTS of a table that exists drop that table and create the table of the statement, where SQLite does nothing. It is found by a replay of the log into an empty cache that differs from SQLite, because the commit object holds the drop and the create, and SQLite runs the statement and does nothing; or by a later statement that gives another answer, another count of changes, or another refusal for the type of a value than in SQLite, because the table has lost its rows and has the columns of the statement. `unturnedBound` makes the fit of the conditions to the key of an index, or of the primary key, bound a column by a comparison with its value on the left, of slice 12, with the operator as the text writes it, so that the scan of `5 < a` reads the rows below 5. The filter above the scan drops the rows that the scan read wrongly, and cannot give those that it did not read. So the bug is found by a select with WHERE, of its own or in a transaction, that gives fewer rows than in SQLite; by an update or a delete that changes fewer rows, through its count of changes, or of its own through no commit where SQLite changes rows; or by a later statement that gives another answer than in SQLite, a commit that fails in SQLite, or a replay of the log that differs from SQLite. An engine that reads every row gives its scan no bound, and does not have the bug. Since the simulation compares the counts of changes, `listOrder` and `zeroLeftOut` are also found by an update or a delete whose count of changes differs from SQLite's, as when a WHERE meets the zero value of `zeroLeftOut`. `keepRead` also leaves open the rows of a query in a transaction, so a later statement of the connection fails with "the rows of an earlier query of the connection are open". The first of these for each bug is its own, and two seeds must find the bug by it: `keepOpen` by no write after the workload, `keepRead` by the read transaction left open after the workload, `listOrder`, `keepOldKey`, and `replaceExisting` by the replay of the log, `zeroLeftOut`, `nullDefault`, `openBound`, and `unturnedBound` by the query that gives another answer than in SQLite, `changeNothing` by the update or the delete of its own that is in no commit and changes rows in SQLite, and `pinByPlace` by the refusal of the core. Each seed that finds a bug runs again without it, and must find nothing. The bugs are fields of `bugs` in `bugs.go`, which only the test files can set, through `Options.Plant` in `export_test.go`.

   Of the bugs of statements of their own, a write in a transaction that spans statements (`writeIn`) plants only `skipUnique`, `convertReal`, `changeNothing`, `keepOldKey`, `replaceExisting`, and `nullDefault`, of the five bugs of RETURNING all but `rowsOfFailed`, and of the bugs of arithmetic of an update `setFromNew`, `evalStops`, `copyAcross`, and `copySelf`. `convertReal` is in `insert.check` in `operator.go`, which a statement of its own and a write in a transaction both run. In a table of one column, only the check of the log finds it, with "stores a value of another type than its column": SQLite converts the real of the log as the bug did, so the replay of the log and every comparison with SQLite agree with the engine. In a table of several columns, the check of a refusal against the rule can find it too, with "and the rule for the type of a value". `listOrder`, `zeroLeftOut`, and `nullDefault` are in `buildSink` in `operator.go`, and only a statement of its own plants the first two: `writeIn` gives `buildSink` only `nullDefault` of them. `changeNothing` is at the top of `changeRows`, and `keepOldKey` is in `update.run`, both in `operator.go`. They get there through a field `bugs` of `build`, `update`, and `remove`. `writeIn` gives both to `buildSink` too, so a statement of its own and one in a transaction both plant them. `replaceExisting` is in the CREATE TABLE case of `buildSink`, which gives the statement the sink `replaceTable` in place of `nothing`, and `writeIn` gives it to `buildSink` too. `ackUnknown`, `checkOutside`, `keepOpen`, and `annotateNext` are in `write`, which runs a statement of its own. `checkOutside` checks the key itself, with a `get` in its own read transaction, after the checks of the sink: the sink's `run` calls no `get` since every row goes through `insertNew`. The write that follows goes through `upsert`, as with `skipUnique`. The pinned order of its calls did not change. `keepRead` is in `Rows.Close`, so it also leaves open the rows of a query in a transaction. The three bugs of transactions are in `tx.go`: `keepFailed` in `writeIn`, `readNewest` in `Conn.ready`, which every statement of a transaction passes, and `rollbackKeeps` in `Conn.Rollback`. `openBound` is in `bugs.reader` in `bugs.go`, which wraps the reader that `Conn.query` and `Conn.queryIn` give `buildQuery`, so a query of its own and a query in a transaction both plant it. `unturnedBound` is in `fit` in `access.go`, as `pinByPlace` is. Both get there through `Engine.reading`, which gives every query, update, and delete its way of reading rows, so a statement of its own and one in a transaction both plant them. Of the bugs of RETURNING, of slice 14, `rowsOfFailed` is in `write`, so only a statement of its own plants it. `oldValues` is in `update.run`, `deleteNone` in `remove.run`, and `starKeyFirst` in `buildReturning`, and `writeIn` gives the three to `buildSink` too. `dropLast` is in `newReturned`, and gets there through `Engine.order`, which draws the direction of the rows of every statement with RETURNING. So a statement of its own and one in a transaction both plant these four. Of the bugs of the scalar subqueries, of slice 15, `lastRow`, `firstNotNull`, `limitZeroRow`, and `orderIgnored` are in `build.value` in `subquery.go`, and `keepValue`, `noRowMatches`, and `swapSubqueries` in `build.compute` there. `ownChanges` is in `build.bind` in `operator.go`, and `changeRows` then changes the rows one at a time (`changedOneByOne` in `bugs.go`). The seven get there through `Engine.reading`, so a statement of its own and one in a transaction both plant them. `outsideTx` is in `writeIn`, so only a write in a transaction plants it. Of the seven bugs of arithmetic, `nullCompares` is in `condition.holds` and `condition.computed`, and `errorSkipsRow` in `condition.holds`, both in `operator.go`. `constantsServeNothing` is in `condition.perRow`, which reads a field of the condition that `build.arithmetic` sets. The three get there through `Engine.reading`, as the field `bugs` of `how` of `build`, which `build.arithmetic` gives the condition, so a statement of its own and one in a transaction both plant them. `setFromNew` and `copySelf` are in `update.newRow`, `evalStops` in `update.run`, which it ends with `errStopped`, and `copyAcross` in `update.check`. `writeIn` gives the four to `buildSink` too.

   The bug that the brief of the checks of transactions asked for first, a rollback to the savepoint that is skipped after a failed statement so that its change commits, cannot be found with the statements of slice 1, because no statement of slice 1 fails after it has changed anything. An insert writes one row, checks its table and its columns before it writes, and writes nothing for a key that exists; a create checks the name, and a drop the table, before they change anything; the context is checked before the write and not after it; and nothing yields to the scheduler between the write and the annotation, where a stop of the instance would come. A failed statement therefore has nothing to roll back, and a release in place of the rollback to the savepoint leaves the same transaction. A probe on 2026-11-19 planted it as `skipRollbackTo`, which released the savepoint of a failed statement instead of rolling back to it, and 100 seeds of the default workload found nothing. `keepFailed` is the nearest bug that the statements of slice 1 make visible: it skips the rollback to the savepoint and keeps the failed statement, annotated, in the transaction. Since slice 8 a statement can fail after a write: an update that gives a row the key of another has deleted the old row when `insertNew` finds the key. `TestConn_Begin` shows that the rollback to the savepoint puts the old row back, and `TestConn_writeIn` in `tx_internal_test.go` shows the order of its calls against a fake.

   Each bug with its environment and its profile; the shares of 200 seeds, and of 1,000 for the bugs of arithmetic, are under [Results](#results):

   | Bug | The wrong decision | Environment and profile |
   | --- | --- | --- |
   | `skipUnique` | An insert writes its row without the check of its key, so the row replaces one. | Default, `tables` |
   | `ackUnknown` | A statement whose commit has an unknown outcome succeeds. | Default, `tables`, which finds it in more seeds than `mixed` does, 48 of 200 against 39. |
   | `checkOutside` | An insert checks its table, its column, and its key in a read transaction before its write transaction. | Hammer, because the bug needs two inserts of one key on one instance at once; `tables` |
   | `keepOpen` | A write statement that fails leaves its write transaction open. | Default, `mixed` |
   | `keepRead` | `Rows.Close` of rows that no loop has read leaves the read transaction open. | Default, `where` |
   | `annotateNext` | A statement annotates each argument plus one, while its row gets the argument. | Default, `arguments` |
   | `keepFailed` | A statement that fails in a write transaction after its savepoint is annotated, and its savepoint released, as if it had succeeded. | Default, `transactions` |
   | `readNewest` | Each statement of a read-only transaction reads the newest state that is committed, and not the state of the beginning of the transaction. | Hammer, because the bug needs commits between two statements of one transaction; `transactions` |
   | `rollbackKeeps` | The roll back of a write transaction with one write commits it. | Default, `transactions` |
   | `convertReal` | An insert of a real that is a whole number into an INT or INTEGER column stores the integer, as SQLite converts it. | Hammer, because the bug needs an insert of a whole real into an INT or INTEGER column that otherwise succeeds, and the hammer environment runs more statements, on one instance that no other takes the lease from; `tables`, of which one insert in eight gives an integer of such a column as the real of its value (`Profile.Mistyped`). |
   | `listOrder` | An insert with a list of columns gives its values to the columns of the table in the order of the list, the first value to the first column, and not to the columns that the list names. | Default, `tables` |
   | `zeroLeftOut` | An insert with a list of columns gives a column that takes NULL, has no default, and that the list leaves out, the zero value of its type, 0, 0.0, the empty text, or the empty blob, in place of NULL. | Hammer, which runs more statements; `tables`, whose shapes have no defaults, because the bug meets only a column without one. |
   | `openBound` | A scan by one or two bounds of the key reads a closed bound, of `<=` or `>=`, as open, and leaves out the row whose key is the bound. | Hammer, because the bug needs a select whose bound of the key is the key of a row, on an engine that reads by the key, and the one engine of the hammer environment reads as the rules choose; `where`, which weighs the selects with WHERE, and writes no update or delete that takes their rows. |
   | `pinByPlace` | The fit of the conditions to a key pins a column of the key to the value of a condition on another column. | Hammer, which finds it in 81 of 200 seeds, where the default environment finds it in 43; `indexes` |
   | `changeNothing` | An update and a delete find no row, change nothing, and report a count of 0. | Default, `changes` |
   | `keepOldKey` | An update that gives a row a new key writes the row under the new key and leaves the row of the old key. | Default, `changes` |
   | `replaceExisting` | A create table with IF NOT EXISTS of a table that exists drops that table and creates the table of the statement, where SQLite does nothing. | Default, `exists` |
   | `nullDefault` | An insert with a list of columns gives a column with a default that the list leaves out NULL in place of its default. | Default, `defaults` |
   | `unturnedBound` | The fit of the conditions to a key bounds a column by a comparison with its value on the left with the operator as the text writes it, and not turned round, so that the scan of `5 < a` reads the rows below 5. | Hammer, because an engine that reads every row does not have the bug, and the one engine of the hammer environment reads as the rules choose; `valuefirst` |
   | `turnMixed` | A key delivers an ORDER BY of mixed directions as if each of its columns were in the direction of the first. | Hammer, which runs more statements on one instance; `order` |
   | `limitFirst` | A select with ORDER BY and LIMIT whose rows are sorted takes the first rows of its access, as many as the LIMIT keeps, and sorts those. | Hammer, `order` |
   | `ascendLast` | A sort by the last column of an ORDER BY sorts it ascending, also where the column is descending. | Hammer, `order` |
   | `rowsOfFailed` | An insert, an update, or a delete with RETURNING of its own through `Query` whose commit fails gives its rows without the error. | Hammer, `returning` |
   | `oldValues` | An update with RETURNING gives each row as it was before the update. | Hammer, `returning` |
   | `dropLast` | A statement with RETURNING that changes two rows or more leaves out the last row that it changed. | Hammer, `returning` |
   | `lastRow` | A scalar subquery gives the value of the last row of its access, and not of the first. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `firstNotNull` | A scalar subquery gives the value of the first row whose value is not NULL, and NULL only where every row has NULL. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `ownChanges` | An update or a delete with a scalar subquery changes its rows one at a time, and computes its subqueries again after each change, so that a subquery of its own table sees the rows that it changed. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `keepValue` | A scalar subquery keeps its value from the last run of a statement whose plan prints alike, also where the arguments or the rows changed since. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `noRowMatches` | A scalar subquery that finds no row makes its comparison true of every row whose column is not NULL, where a comparison with NULL is never true. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `outsideTx` | A scalar subquery of an update or a delete in a transaction reads the newest state that is committed, without the changes of the transaction. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `swapSubqueries` | A statement with two scalar subqueries or more gives the first the value of the second, and the second that of the first. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `limitZeroRow` | A scalar subquery with LIMIT 0 gives the value of its first row, where it has no row and its value is NULL. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `orderIgnored` | A scalar subquery with an ORDER BY gives the value of the first row of the access that its conditions alone choose, and not of the first row of its ORDER BY. | Hammer, `subquery` |
   | `setFromNew` | An update computes each value of its SET from the row as the assignments before it left it, and not from the row as it was before the statement, also a column alone that it copies. | Hammer, which runs more statements on one instance; `arithmetic` |
   | `nullCompares` | A comparison that is computed for each row, of arithmetic or of a column with a column, is true where a side is NULL. | Hammer, `arithmetic` |
   | `evalStops` | An update whose SET fails with an evaluation error on a row stops there and succeeds, with the rows that it changed before that row and their count. | Hammer, `arithmetic` |
   | `errorSkipsRow` | A comparison that is computed for each row and fails with an evaluation error on a row is false for that row, so that the statement leaves the row out and goes on. | Hammer, `arithmetic` |
   | `copyAcross` | An update copies a column alone into a column of another class, as `set r = i` of an INT column into a REAL one, where s4ql refuses it, and stores the value as it is. | Hammer, `arithmetic` |
   | `copySelf` | An update that copies a column alone, as `set a = b`, gives the column the value that it had, and not that of the column that it names. | Hammer, `arithmetic` |
   | `oneCommit` | `ExecAll` runs the statements of a text outside a transaction in a write transaction of its own, which commits them all at the end of the text. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `goOn` | `ExecAll` goes on after a statement that fails, and gives the error of the first. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `commitFailed` | A statement of a text outside a transaction that fails while it runs commits what it changed before it failed. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `commitLater` | A write of a text outside a transaction that another statement follows succeeds, and logs its event, with its commit left for the next statement or the end of the text. | Default, for its crashes; `several` |
   | `annotateText` | Each statement of a text annotates its write with the whole text. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `argsFromStart` | Each statement of a text annotates its write with the arguments of the text from the first on. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `undoInTx` | In a write transaction, a statement of a text that fails after the first rolls the transaction back and begins a new one. | Hammer, `several` |
   | `firstResult` | `ExecAll` gives the result of the first statement that sets the count of changes, and not of the last. | Hammer, `several` |

   `constantsServeNothing` makes a comparison with an expression without a column serve no access, so that a statement reads more rows than the rules choose. Its rows and errors are SQLite's, so only `TestDifferential_plantedBugs` plants it, where `pair.checkAccess` finds it.
5. **The operators alone.** `operator_internal_test.go` runs each operator, `buildQuery`, and `buildSink` against `fake`, and `engine_internal_test.go` the engine's handling of transactions and its events: the order of the calls, an early stop, a context that ends, a failure at each step, a panic, and each planted bug. The fake records an insert as `insert`, the name of the table, and the values of the row, and fails or panics there on demand (`insertFails`, `insertPanics`). So the pinned orders of the calls of an insert have one `insert` where they had a `get` and an `upsert` before every row went through `insertNew`. In `TestEngine_write` and `TestConn_writeIn` they read `table t1 insert t1 1 annotate …`, where they read `table t1 get t1 1 upsert t1 1 annotate …`, and `TestInsert` and `TestBuild` changed in the same way. `TestChangeRows` pins the calls of an update and a delete, as the rules choose and with every row read: first `keys` and an `add` for each row that the scan gives, and then, for each `key`, the change of its row. The change is a `get` and an `upsert` for an update, a `get`, a `delete`, and an `insert` for an update of the key, and a `delete` for a delete. It also pins the read of the check of NOT NULL, which comes before `keys`. `TestConn` there has the reader of a connection: none for a connection that only writes, one from its first read until its close, one for each connection of an engine, a reader that cannot be opened, a close while the reader is in use, and a statement on a closed connection.
6. **The pinned cases.** `TestEngine_errors` pins each error against SQLite. `TestNewPlan` pins the plans, `TestEngine_annotations` the annotations as the log holds them, and `TestEngine_events` the events.

   `TestEngine_columns` compares 44 shapes of tables of several columns with SQLite: two and three columns of mixed types, each with the key at each position. Each of these comes with every column NOT NULL, with every column but the key taking NULL, and, for three columns, with each column but the key taking NULL alone. Before slice 6 the shapes were 13. The steps of a shape give NULL as a literal in either case of its letters, and as a nil argument in a list in the order of the table and in another. They give it to each column, with a key that exists, beside a value of another type, and beside a column left out. For each statement of a shape it compares the answer, which is the error with its codes and its message, or the names and the declared types of the columns and the rows as a multiset. It also compares the count of changes after each statement, the tables at the end, and the tables after a replay of the log into an empty cache. A statement that the engine refuses by its own rule of the type is checked against the message of the rule, and must leave the count. The test also creates, fills, reads, and replays a table of 2,000 columns, which is as many as SQLite allows.

   `TestEngine_nulls` has the NULL of slice 6, on a table of four columns of which two take NULL. It reads `nullable` of each column from the commit object, and compares the table after a replay of the log. It writes NULL as a literal, as a nil argument, and through a column left out, reads each back as NULL, and reads the annotation of a nil argument, `with null`. It compares every case of `NOT NULL constraint failed` with SQLite, with its codes and the count of changes 0. It gives NULL beside a value of another type. It runs NULL in a write transaction, also after a roll back to the savepoint of a statement that failed, and in a read-only one.

   `TestEngine_where` has the WHERE of slice 7. It runs every query on an engine that reads as the rules choose, on one that reads every row, with `Options.ScanAll`, and on SQLite, and requires the same answer of each. It compares each operator with the edges of each type and with values beside them, on the key and on another column. It compares the rows that one, two, and three conditions select from a table of every shape of `TestEngine_columns`. It pins SQLite's errors of the names in SQLite's order, `string or blob too big` for a value that is too long, and the refusal of a value of another type, with its message and the count of changes left. It numbers the parameters across the conditions, selects in a write transaction and in a read-only one, and selects again after a replay of the log into an empty cache. `TestFilter` in `operator_internal_test.go` pins the calls of the fake for each access of a table without an index: the look-up of the indexes where the choice needs it, and then a `get`, a `range` with its bounds, or neither. It requires the same rows with every row read.

   `TestEngine_update` and `TestEngine_delete` have the UPDATE and DELETE of slice 8. They run each statement on SQLite, on an engine that reads as the rules choose, and on one that reads every row (`changeTrio`), and require of both engines SQLite's answer, SQLite's count of changes, and SQLite's tables after the statement. The cases change rows without WHERE, by the key, by a range of the key, by another column, and by a WHERE that holds for no row. They bind arguments in SET and in WHERE, change the column that the WHERE reads, and change the key: to a key that no row has, to the key that the row has, and to a key that another row has. They pin SQLite's errors in SQLite's order, and the refusal of a value of another type, with its message and with the tables and the count of changes left. Under a length limit of 1000 they pin `string or blob too big` for a value and for a row, and the known difference of a long literal of SET. They update and delete in a table of every shape of `TestEngine_columns`, column by column and by the key. The tests also update a table of 2,000 columns, and delete 2,000 of 3,000 rows with one statement.

   `TestEngine_cancelChange` cancels an update or a delete at each point of its run. Its context is a `flipContext`, whose `Err` gives `context.Canceled` once it has been called n times. For every n from 0 up, until the statement succeeds, the test runs one of five updates and deletes on a table of 40 rows, on its own and in a write transaction. Each canceled statement must give `context.Canceled` and leave the rows as SQLite has them, and the statement must be canceled more often than it changes rows. The transaction goes on, inserts a row, and commits. At the end, and after a replay of the log into an empty cache, the engine must hold SQLite's state.

   `TestEngine_indexes` in `index_test.go` has the create index and the drop index of slice 9. It runs each statement on the engine and on SQLite (`indexPair`), and requires SQLite's answer, with the rows as a multiset, and SQLite's count of changes. Outside a transaction it also requires SQLite's rows and SQLite's indexes after the statement. `oracle.indexes` reads the indexes of each table from SQLite, in the order in which they were created, and `instance.indexes` reads them from the core. The cases create an index on a table with rows and drop it. They keep the order of creation through a drop, a replay of the log, and a restart. They pin SQLite's errors in SQLite's order, and the offsets of seven of them, each on a fresh connection to SQLite. They drop a table with its indexes, and create and drop indexes in a transaction that rolls back and in one that commits. They refuse both statements in a read-only transaction, and while rows of the transaction are open. They take an index of 250 columns and refuse one of 251. Under a length limit of 1,000 they compare `string or blob too big` with SQLite at every length of a name from 400 to 480 bytes, in seven forms of the statement. They pin the events, and they require the same choice of an access after a replay of the log into an empty cache.

   `TestChooseAccess` in `access_internal_test.go` pins the rules case by case, on a table with a key of one column, on one with a key of two columns, and on goqite's. A case writes its conditions as text, such as `a = 1 and e > 2`, and the access that it expects as text, such as `i2 = 1 > 2`. Each case also asks the rules without the indexes, and requires the same answer where either answer is "no row" or the row of a primary key. Since slice 12, each case also goes through the statement (`textAccess`) three times: with its comparisons as written, with each written with its value first, and with every second one so (`turnedWhere`). Each must give the access of the case. A subtest pins the access that the planted bug `unturnedBound` gives. `TestForcedAccess` pins what each kind of forced path reads. `TestEngine_reading` pins the draw of the direction, with a host that counts its draws: one draw for each query, backward for an odd number, and no draw from the host for a statement that is no query, for an engine with a seed, or for a path with a direction.

   `TestEngine_paths` in `access_test.go` runs statements by every path of a table. The table has columns of all five types, 60 rows, and seven indexes: on a column of each type, on descending columns, on several columns, on columns that take NULL, and with the primary key among the columns of the index. The paths are nothing forced, every row, the primary key, and each index, each forward and backward. Beside them are the primary key limited to no pinned column, and each index limited to each number of pinned columns below the length of its key. The test runs 56 conditions, each with three select lists, by every path. Since slice 12, the first select of a condition has its comparisons as written, the second has each with its value first, and the third has every second one so. Each select must give SQLite's rows as a multiset, and `Rows.Access` must show the access of the path. For a path with a direction, the rows must also come in the order of SQLite's own `order by` of the key of the path, or of its reverse for a path backward. Each path must give some of the rows, and not all of them, for at least 30 selects. The test then runs updates and deletes by every path, each on a fresh database: every eighth condition for each path, and another eighth for the next path. Of the paths that run a condition, every second writes its comparisons with their values first. It requires SQLite's answer, SQLite's count of changes, and SQLite's tables afterwards.

   `TestEngine_order` runs four queries 40 times on an engine with a seed: a select of every row, a range of the primary key, an equality by an index, and a range by an index. Each run must give its rows in the order of the key of its access or in the reverse, as `Rows.Access` says. Each query must run at least 5 times in each direction. A second engine with the same seed must read in the same directions, and an engine with another seed in others.

   `TestEngine_rowsRead` pins the rows that named queries read from the cache, in each direction; see [The rows read from the cache](#the-rows-read-from-the-cache).

   `TestAccessOf_golden` keeps the access of named queries on fixed schemas as golden files, so that a change of the rules shows as a diff of them; see [The golden plans](#the-golden-plans).

   `TestEngine_exists` in `exists_test.go` has the IF NOT EXISTS and IF EXISTS of slice 10. It runs each statement on the engine and on SQLite (`indexPair`), as `TestEngine_indexes` does. The cases have each of the four statements where it does its work, where it does nothing, and where it gives each of SQLite's errors, with names in other cases and in double quotes. The test reads the log (`logAnnotations`). A statement of its own that does nothing must write no commit object, and neither must a transaction of only such statements. The commit object of a transaction that also has statements that do their work must hold the annotations of those, and no other. The test compares the tables after a replay of the log into an empty cache, and after a restart on the same cache. It runs the cases in a write transaction that rolls back and in one that commits. In a read-only transaction, a statement that does nothing must succeed, and one that would write must give `attempt to write a readonly database` after the errors of its names and of its columns. While rows of a transaction are open, each of the four statements must fail with `ErrRowsOpen`. Under a length limit of 1,000, a statement that does nothing must give no error, also where its text is longer than the limit, and one that would do its work must give `string or blob too big` as the plain statement does. A create index of 251 columns must do nothing where an index has its name, and be "not supported" where none has. The test compares the offsets of five errors, each on a fresh connection to SQLite, and pins `statement.nothing` of the events.

   `TestEngine_defaults` in `defaults_test.go` has the defaults of slice 11. It runs each statement on a `duo`, an instance of the engine and SQLite, and requires SQLite's answer and SQLite's count of changes. It creates a table with every kind of default, reads the defaults from the log, and compares the table after a replay of the log and after a restart on the same cache, where an insert must store the same defaults. It stores the default of each column that a list leaves out, and keeps a NULL that the statement gives. It pins `NOT NULL constraint failed` from DEFAULT NULL, also in the key; `UNIQUE constraint failed` from the default of the key, also from -0.0 against 0.0; and `string or blob too big` for a record that a default makes too long. It pins each pair of neighbours of the order of the errors of an insert, and `attempt to write a readonly database` before every error of a row. An insert without a list, an update, a delete, and a select must stay as they are. In transactions it runs a statement that fails, a roll back, and a drop and a create of the table with other defaults. A create table with IF NOT EXISTS of a table that exists, with other defaults, must do nothing and stay out of the log. Under a length limit of 1,000, a table of 42 REAL columns with the default `.5` must be "not supported", because its statement in the cache is longer than the limit, and the transaction must go on. The test also runs goqite's schema as far as slices 1 to 11 take it, with the inserts that leave out the columns with defaults. The defaults of `id`, `created`, `updated`, and `timeout` are expressions, so they are cut, and so are the trigger and `returning`; the table says WITHOUT ROWID beside STRICT; the two `default 0` and the index are goqite's. `TestNewPlan` pins the plan of a create table with every kind of default, and `TestBuild` in `operator_internal_test.go` the planted bugs `zeroLeftOut` and `nullDefault`. `TestDefaults` in `conn_test.go` of the driver has the defaults through `database/sql`.

   `TestEngine_valueFirst` in `valuefirst_test.go` has the values on the left of slice 12, against SQLite. Its helper `valueFirst` writes the comparisons of a WHERE with their values first. The test compares each operator, in each spelling, with the edges of each type and with values beside them, as a literal and as an argument, on the key and on another column, on an engine that reads as the rules choose and on one that reads every row. It requires SQLite's rows, and the same rows of the comparison with its column first. It gives NULL on the left as a literal and as an argument. On a table of every shape of `TestEngine_columns` it mixes conditions of both orders in one WHERE, also bounds of the key of both orders that contradict, that hold one row, and that fix it. It numbers the parameters in the order of the text, in a select, an update, and a delete. It runs each operator with its value first in an update and a delete, and requires SQLite's count of changes and SQLite's tables. It pins SQLite's errors of the names for a column on the right, with SQLite's offset, also in double quotes. Under a length limit of 1,000 it pins the order of the errors: the names, then `string or blob too big`, then the refusal by type. It pins the refusal of the first value of another type in the order of the text, as a literal and as an argument, in a select, an update, and a delete, and the refusals of the arguments by their number and by what they are, also through `Check`. `TestNewPlan` pins the plan of a condition with its value on the left. `TestValueFirst` in `conn_test.go` of the driver has goqite's receive both ways through `database/sql`.

   `TestEngine_orderBy` in `orderby_test.go` has the ORDER BY and LIMIT of slice 13, against SQLite. Its table has 12 rows and a column of each type, with rows that tie in each column but the key, NULL in the columns that take it, and 0.0 and -0.0. It has three indexes, of which one holds a column descending. `ordered.differs` compares an answer with SQLite's in order. SQLite gives the rows of the select without its LIMIT, each with its dense rank under the ORDER BY, so two rows have one rank exactly if they tie. The row of the engine at each place must be a row of the rank that SQLite has at that place. The rows of a rank are compared as a multiset, of which a LIMIT that cuts a rank leaves any part. Without ORDER BY, the rows of a select with a LIMIT must be rows of the select without it, each no more often than that select gives it. The test has three parts:
   - **The rows, the access, and the rows read.** Each query runs as the rules choose, then forward, and then backward. Each run must give SQLite's rows in SQLite's order. Its event must have the access, the index, the `statement.order`, the rows read, and the rows given that the case names; the numbers are under [The rows read from the cache](#the-rows-read-from-the-cache). `checkPlanOf` then reads SQLite's plan for the statement that the cache runs for the scan of each query with ORDER BY or WHERE. It must be the one row of the search, and after it one `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` for an access whose rows are sorted, and no row more for every other. So the cache sorts exactly where the engine says so.
   - **The rows that tie.** On an engine with a seed, a query with a sort gives its rows that tie in the order of the primary key under a path forward, and in its reverse under a path backward. A query whose order an index delivers gives one order under both. A path with `Sort` turns the rows that tie by its direction, also where the index delivers. By the rules, 40 runs of the sorted query give both orders, each at least 5 times, and 40 runs of the delivered one give one.
   - **The errors.** Under a length limit of 1,000 bytes, the test pins SQLite's errors of the names of ORDER BY and their place after those of the select list and of WHERE, `string or blob too big` for an argument of a condition before that of LIMIT, and both before the refusal of a type. Each of these must be SQLite's answer too. It pins the refusal of each kind of argument of LIMIT with its message, and that an integer from 0 runs, up to the largest.

   `TestDelivers` in `access_internal_test.go` pins the rule of a delivered order case by case: on the primary key, on indexes of a table with a key of one column and of one with a key of two, and on goqite's index and an index on the queue and the timeout with the conditions of goqite's receive. Each case says forward, backward, or no. `TestChooseAccess_orderBy` pins the choice with an ORDER BY. Each case gives the access without a LIMIT, and the access with one where it is another. It also requires that the access without the ORDER BY is the one of slice 9, with a LIMIT and without. `TestForcedAccess_orderBy` pins what a forced path of the primary key or of an index does with an ORDER BY, with `sort` and without. `TestEngine_events_order` in `access_test.go` pins `statement.order` and the presence of `statement.direction` in the events of selects with ORDER BY and without, of an update, and of a delete. It also pins that a query that fails before it has an access says nothing of its order, and that a query under a path with a direction has no `statement.direction`. `TestNewPlan` pins the plans of slice 13.

   `TestEngine_orderBy_paths` in `orderby_test.go` runs 107 queries with ORDER BY or LIMIT through every access that can serve them: the paths `Full`, `Primary`, and each of the five indexes of its table, each with `Sort` and without, with `Limited` pins from none to as many as the conditions pin, forward and backward, and the rules forward, backward, and with `Sort`. The table has a column of each of the five types, NULL, rows that tie, and 0.0 and -0.0. That is 1,948 runs: 322 with a delivered order, 1,554 with a sort, and 72 without ORDER BY, 337 of them with `limit 0` and 640 with a sort and a LIMIT above 0. Each run must give SQLite's rows in the exact order of its access, the key of the access where nothing sorts and the ORDER BY and then the primary key, forward or backward, where the cache sorts; `PathAccessOf`, the event's access, and `statement.order` must agree; the plan of the cache must have SQLite's sort row exactly where the access is sorted (`wantPlans`); a sort with a LIMIT must read the rows that the same path reads without the LIMIT; and `limit 0` must read none. Its subtests make 856 runs each in a write transaction that has written rows and again after its roll back, in a read-only transaction, after a restart with an empty cache, and on the database in memory. Every count is pinned, so a path that drops out shows.

   The machinery of the generated tests takes a select with ORDER BY or LIMIT too, and `stmttest.Sequence` writes them since the generated checks of slice 13, which are described below. `pair.step` compares such a query with SQLite by `ordered.compare`, the one comparison in order, which `ordered.differs` of the hand tests calls as well: the row at each place must have the rank that SQLite gives that place under the ORDER BY, so rows that tie are compared as a multiset within their place, and a LIMIT that cuts a group of ties may leave any part of it; without ORDER BY, the rows of a select with LIMIT must be rows of the select without it, no more often than it has them, and as many as SQLite gives. `pair.bothWays` compares the two instances of a pair by the number of their rows where a LIMIT may cut, `typeRule` knows the refusal of an argument of LIMIT and its place after the types of the conditions (`limitRule`), and `everyPath` runs a select with ORDER BY through a `Sort` variant of every path. `TestPair_orderBy` runs the queries of `TestEngine_orderBy_paths` and refusals of an argument of LIMIT through that machinery, in transactions and outside them and in memory, with its tally pinned. The fuzz targets `FuzzSQL` and `FuzzSQL_core` reach a text with ORDER BY or LIMIT through it.

   The driver has `TestOrderBy` in `conn_test.go` of the root package, which compares `Query` and `QueryRow` with SQLite in order, runs a prepared statement with `limit ?` in transactions and outside them, runs goqite's receive with goqite's index and without it, and pins the answer for an argument of LIMIT of each type of Go. The driver check in `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck` compares a select with ORDER BY or LIMIT in the same way (`orderedDiffers`, `limitRefusal`).

   `TestEngine_returning` in `returning_test.go` has the RETURNING of slice 14, against SQLite. Its table has a column of each type, two columns with a default, and an index. Its 44 calls run through `pair.step`, most through `Query` and some through `Exec`. They are inserts, updates, and deletes with `*`, with named columns, with a column twice, and with names in another case and in double quotes. They have the defaults, NULL, and -0.0; an update of the key, of every row, by the index, with the values that the row has, and of no row; and each failure: a constraint, a value of another type, and a column of RETURNING that the table does not have, which must come after the other errors of the names. The test has three parts:
   - **The rows, the columns, and the errors.** The calls run in rounds on the core and in memory, at least two, and until the order check below has seen the rows in both directions, which the seed of the engine decides, at most 20. Each must give SQLite's rows, columns, error, and count of changes, and the table must be SQLite's after each round.
   - **Transactions.** The calls run in a write transaction that commits and in one that rolls back. The table must be SQLite's after the end, and again after a restart with an empty cache. In a read-only transaction, a statement with RETURNING must give `attempt to write a readonly database` after the errors of its names.
   - **Restarts.** The calls run with a restart after every seventh, with an empty cache and with its cache in turn.

   `pair.step` compares the rows of a RETURNING with SQLite's as a multiset, as the rows of any query, and then checks their order (`pair.returnedInOrder`, `pair.checkReturned`), for every update and delete with RETURNING that gives two rows or more through `Query`. Before the statement runs, SQLite gives a select of the columns of RETURNING with the WHERE of the statement, in the order of the primary key, with each value of SET in the place of its column. The rows of the engine must be these rows in this order, or in its reverse where `Rows.Backward` of `export_test.go` says that the engine drew backward. The keys before the change give the order after it: SET holds only constants, so an update of the key that finds two rows fails with `UNIQUE constraint failed` and gives no rows.
